Most organizations start with showback to build cost awareness, then graduate to chargeback once teams understand their consumption patterns. Tools like Kubecost and OpenCost enable both models.

📝 Detailed Explanation
FinOps Maturity: Showback → Chargeback
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SHOWBACK (Awareness)              CHARGEBACK (Accountability)           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ "Team Alpha spent $1,200  │     │ "Team Alpha's $1,200 cloud cost │  │
  │  │  on Kubernetes this month"│     │  has been deducted from their    │  │
  │  │                           │     │  departmental budget"            │  │
  │  │ 📊 Dashboard only         │     │ 💰 Actual financial transfer     │  │
  │  │ No financial consequence  │     │ Teams optimize or pay more      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  For anihpj/jobpost: Kubecost shows the Web team their pods cost        │
  │  $450/mo and the Data team their GPU workloads cost $3,200/mo.          │
  │  Showback → teams see costs. Chargeback → teams are billed.             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Reversed — showback is informational, chargeback is financial. Why not C? Both work across all clouds and on-prem. Why not D? They are different stages of FinOps maturity.

Platform Arch Q20 of 200
What capability do Kubecost and OpenCost provide that native Kubernetes resource metrics (metrics-server) does NOT?
A) Collecting CPU and memory usage metrics per pod and node
B) Enforcing resource limits and preventing pods from exceeding their allocated CPU/memory
C) Translating resource usage (CPU hours, GB-hours, network egress, PV storage) into actual monetary cost — broken down by namespace, deployment, team label, and environment
D) Automatically scaling pods based on CPU/memory utilization thresholds
✅ Correct Answer: C

metrics-server provides raw resource metrics (CPU, memory) for autoscaling and `kubectl top`. Kubecost/OpenCost add the financial layer: they combine resource usage with cloud provider pricing (EC2 instance costs, EBS pricing, data transfer rates) to show actual dollar costs per namespace, deployment, team, or even individual pod. This is essential for FinOps — you can't optimize what you can't measure in dollars.

Kubecost — Cost Attribution
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kubecost Dashboard:                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Namespace        CPU Cost    RAM Cost    Storage    Network  Total│  │
  │  │  anihpj-prod      $320.00     $180.00    $45.00    $12.00   $557  │  │
  │  │  anihpj-staging    $85.00      $52.00    $22.00     $4.00   $163  │  │
  │  │  anihpj-dev        $40.00      $28.00    $15.00     $2.00    $85  │  │
  │  │  monitoring        $65.00      $95.00     $8.00     $3.00   $171  │  │
  │  │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │  │
  │  │  TOTAL             $510.00     $355.00    $90.00    $21.00   $976  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? metrics-server already does that. Why not B? That's LimitRange/ResourceQuota, not a cost tool. Why not D? That's HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler).

Platform Arch Q21 of 200
What does the Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA) do, and how does it differ from the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA)?
A) VPA adjusts individual pod resource requests/limits based on historical usage (right-sizing); HPA adjusts the number of pod replicas based on current metrics. They complement each other — VPA sizes pods correctly, HPA scales pod count.
B) VPA and HPA do the same thing but VPA is the newer replacement for HPA
C) VPA scales pods across multiple clusters; HPA scales within a single cluster
D) VPA adjusts pod memory only; HPA adjusts CPU only
✅ Correct Answer: A

VPA and HPA solve different problems: VPA = right-sizing ("this pod needs 500m CPU, not 200m"), HPA = scaling ("I need 5 replicas, not 2"). VPA observes actual usage over time and recommends (or automatically sets) optimal requests/limits. For anihpj/jobpost: VPA ensures the Django API pods have the right CPU/memory allocation, while HPA ensures there are enough replicas to handle traffic.

VPA (Vertical) vs HPA (Horizontal)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VPA: Right-size pods              HPA: Scale pod count                 │
  │  ┌────────┐    ┌────────┐          ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐ ┌──┐                 │
  │  │ 200m   │ →  │ 500m   │          │  │ │  │ │  │ │  │  ← 4 replicas   │
  │  │ 256Mi  │    │ 512Mi  │          └──┘ └──┘ └──┘ └──┘                 │
  │  └────────┘    └────────┘           ┌──┐ ┌──┐                           │
  │  (before)      (after VPA)         │  │ │  │  ← 2 replicas (before)     │
  │                                     └──┘ └──┘                           │
  │  VPA modes:                        HPA targets:                         │
  │  • Off: recommend only              • CPU utilization %                 │
  │  • Initial: set at pod creation     • Memory utilization                │
  │  • Auto: update running pods        • Custom metrics (Prometheus)       │
  │  (pod restart required!)            (no restart needed)                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? They serve different purposes — VPA doesn't replace HPA. Why not C? Neither scales across clusters. Why not D? Both can adjust CPU and memory.

Platform Arch Q22 of 200
A platform team runs anihpj/jobpost on AWS EKS. Which cost optimization strategy provides the largest savings for stateless, fault-tolerant workloads like the Django API pods?
A) Reserved Instances — commit to 1-3 year terms for predictable, steady-state workloads
B) On-Demand pricing — pay as you go with no commitment for maximum flexibility
C) Dedicated Hosts — reserve entire physical servers for compliance or licensing requirements
D) Spot Instances — use spare EC2 capacity at up to 90% discount, combined with Karpenter or Cluster Autoscaler to handle interruptions gracefully
✅ Correct Answer: D

Spot Instances offer up to 90% savings over On-Demand pricing. For stateless, fault-tolerant workloads (like Django API pods behind a load balancer), spot is ideal — if AWS reclaims a spot instance (2-minute warning), Karpenter or Cluster Autoscaler automatically launches a replacement on another instance. Combine spot for API pods with Reserved Instances for the stateful database to maximize savings.

Cost Optimization Strategy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Workload Type          Strategy              Savings      anihpj use    │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Stateless API pods     Spot Instances        Up to 90%    anihpj-api   │
  │  Stateful DB            Reserved Instances    30-60%       anihpj-db    │
  │  Batch/CI jobs          Spot + Karpenter      Up to 90%    CI runners   │
  │  Base infra (monitoring) Savings Plans        20-30%       Prom/Grafana │
  │  Dev/staging            Spot only             Up to 90%    anihpj-dev   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Reserved Instances are better for databases — predictable, always-on. Why not B? On-Demand is the most expensive option — use only when spot isn't suitable. Why not C? Dedicated Hosts are for compliance/licensing — not cost optimization.

Platform Arch Q23 of 200
What is the PRIMARY purpose of a ResourceQuota in a namespace, and what does it enforce?
A) It sets default CPU/memory requests and limits for any pod created without explicit resource specifications
B) It caps the TOTAL resource consumption (CPU, memory, storage, object count) across all resources in a namespace — preventing any single namespace from consuming the entire cluster's capacity
C) It automatically scales pods within a namespace based on CPU and memory utilization thresholds
D) It creates network isolation rules between namespaces, acting as a firewall between tenant workloads
✅ Correct Answer: B

ResourceQuota is a namespace-level cap. It limits total CPU, memory, storage, and object counts (pods, services, secrets, PVCs, etc.) within a namespace. For example: "ns:anihpj-dev gets max 8 CPUs and 16Gi RAM total." Once the quota is reached, new resource creation is denied — protecting the cluster from a runaway namespace.

ResourceQuota Example
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ResourceQuota: anihpj-dev-quota                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  hard:                                                            │  │
  │  │    requests.cpu: "8"          # Total CPU requests across all pods│  │
  │  │    requests.memory: "16Gi"    # Total memory requests             │  │
  │  │    limits.cpu: "16"           # Total CPU limits                  │  │
  │  │    limits.memory: "32Gi"      # Total memory limits               │  │
  │  │    pods: "20"                 # Max 20 pods in this namespace    │  │
  │  │    services: "10"             # Max 10 services                   │  │
  │  │    persistentvolumeclaims: "5"# Max 5 PVCs                       │  │
  │  │    secrets: "20"              # Max 20 secrets                    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  ⚠️  Difference: ResourceQuota limits TOTAL namespace consumption       │
  │     LimitRange sets DEFAULTS and MIN/MAX per individual pod            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's LimitRange's job. Why not C? That's HPA. Why not D? That's NetworkPolicy.

Platform Arch Q24 of 200
What happens when a developer creates a pod in a namespace with a LimitRange that sets defaultRequest.cpu: 200m and defaultLimit.cpu: 500m, but the developer's pod YAML has NO resource requests or limits specified?
A) The pod is automatically assigned requests=200m and limits=500m CPU — the LimitRange defaults fill in any missing resource specifications
B) The pod creation is rejected — pods without explicit resource requests are not allowed in namespaces with LimitRange
C) The pod runs with no resource constraints — LimitRange only provides documentation, not enforcement
D) The pod gets requests=0 and limits=500m — only the limit default is applied
✅ Correct Answer: A

LimitRange fills in missing values. If a pod doesn't specify CPU/memory requests or limits, LimitRange automatically applies the defaults. This ensures every pod has resource specifications — preventing BestEffort pods (which get evicted first) and ensuring predictable scheduling. LimitRange can also enforce min/max values and ratio constraints.

LimitRange — Defaults and Constraints
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LimitRange: anihpj-limits                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  limits:                                                          │  │
  │  │  - type: Container                                                │  │
  │  │    default:                                                       │  │
  │  │      cpu: "200m"           # ← Pod gets this if not specified    │  │
  │  │      memory: "256Mi"                                              │  │
  │  │    defaultRequest:                                                │  │
  │  │      cpu: "100m"           # ← Pod gets this if not specified    │  │
  │  │      memory: "128Mi"                                              │  │
  │  │    max:                    # ← Hard cap — cannot exceed          │  │
  │  │      cpu: "2"                                                    │  │
  │  │      memory: "4Gi"                                               │  │
  │  │    min:                    # ← Floor — cannot go below           │  │
  │  │      cpu: "50m"                                                  │  │
  │  │      memory: "64Mi"                                              │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? LimitRange doesn't require explicit resource specs — it fills them in. Why not C? LimitRange actively enforces defaults, mins, and maxes. Why not D? Both request AND limit defaults are applied.

Platform Arch Q25 of 200
A platform team must choose between soft multi-tenancy (namespaces) and hard multi-tenancy (vClusters) for hosting 20 different teams. Which scenario REQUIRES hard multi-tenancy?
A) Teams need to deploy their own Deployments and Services without interfering with each other
B) Teams need separate ResourceQuotas and LimitRanges per team
C) Teams need to install their own CRDs, Operators, and custom Admission Webhooks — which are cluster-scoped resources that cannot be isolated via namespaces alone
D) Teams need to use NetworkPolicies to isolate their pod-to-pod traffic
✅ Correct Answer: C

CRDs, Operators, and Admission Webhooks are cluster-scoped — they cannot be namespaced. If Team A installs cert-manager CRDs and Team B installs a conflicting CRD, soft multi-tenancy can't prevent it. Hard multi-tenancy (vCluster) gives each team their own virtual API server where they can install any CRD or operator without affecting others.

When Soft Multi-Tenancy Isn't Enough
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Resource Type        Namespace-Scoped?   Requires Hard MT?             │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Pods, Deployments     ✅ Yes              ❌ No                        │
  │  Services, ConfigMaps  ✅ Yes              ❌ No                        │
  │  ResourceQuota, RBAC   ✅ Yes              ❌ No                        │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  CRDs                  ❌ Cluster-scoped   ✅ YES — need vCluster      │
  │  ClusterRoles          ❌ Cluster-scoped   ✅ YES — need vCluster      │
  │  StorageClasses        ❌ Cluster-scoped   ✅ YES — need vCluster      │
  │  Admission Webhooks    ❌ Cluster-scoped   ✅ YES — need vCluster      │
  │  PersistentVolumes     ❌ Cluster-scoped   ⚠️  PVs can be pre-bound    │
  │  Nodes                 ❌ Cluster-scoped   ⚠️  Shared worker nodes     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A/B/D? Deployments, ResourceQuotas, and NetworkPolicies are namespace-scoped — soft multi-tenancy handles them fine.

Platform Arch Q26 of 200
How does a NetworkPolicy provide tenant isolation in a soft multi-tenancy model?
A) NetworkPolicy encrypts all pod-to-pod traffic within a namespace using TLS certificates
B) NetworkPolicy creates separate virtual networks for each namespace at the cloud provider level (VPC isolation)
C) NetworkPolicy automatically routes tenant traffic through a central firewall appliance for inspection
D) NetworkPolicy defines ingress/egress rules using podSelector and namespaceSelector — allowing you to create a "default-deny" policy per namespace that blocks all cross-namespace traffic unless explicitly allowed
✅ Correct Answer: D

NetworkPolicy allows namespace-level network isolation. A "default-deny" policy in each team's namespace blocks ALL ingress from other namespaces. Then specific allow rules permit only authorized cross-namespace traffic (e.g., anihpj-dev pods can only talk to anihpj-dev pods). Combined with RBAC, ResourceQuota, and LimitRange, NetworkPolicy completes the soft multi-tenancy isolation model.

NetworkPolicy for Tenant Isolation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Default-Deny per namespace:                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                              │  │
  │  │  metadata:                                                        │  │
  │  │    name: default-deny-ingress                                     │  │
  │  │    namespace: team-alpha                                          │  │
  │  │  spec:                                                            │  │
  │  │    podSelector: {}          # applies to ALL pods                 │  │
  │  │    policyTypes:                                                    │  │
  │  │    - Ingress                                                      │  │
  │  │    # No ingress rules = deny ALL incoming traffic                 │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Result: team-alpha pods can't receive traffic from team-beta          │
  │  unless an explicit allow rule is created.                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's mTLS via service mesh. Why not B? NetworkPolicy works at K8s level, not cloud VPC level. Why not C? NetworkPolicy is enforced by CNI, not a central firewall.

Platform Arch Q27 of 200
How should RBAC be configured to give developers in Team Alpha full access to their team-alpha namespace but ZERO access to any other namespace?
A) Create a ClusterRole with full permissions and bind it to the developers — ClusterRoles are automatically scoped to one namespace
B) Create a Role in the team-alpha namespace with the needed permissions (get, list, create, update, delete on pods, deployments, services, etc.), then create a RoleBinding in team-alpha that binds the Role to the developers' group or users
C) Add the developers to the cluster-admin ClusterRoleBinding — they'll naturally only access their own namespace
D) Create a NetworkPolicy restricting their API access to the team-alpha namespace — RBAC is not needed for namespace isolation
✅ Correct Answer: B

Role + RoleBinding is the correct pattern for namespace-scoped access. A Role defines permissions (get pods, create deployments, etc.) within a specific namespace. A RoleBinding grants those permissions to users/groups WITHIN that same namespace. Developers can only see and modify resources in their namespace — they can't even list pods in other namespaces.

RBAC for Multi-Tenancy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ns: team-alpha                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Role: developer                                                  │  │
  │  │    verbs: [get, list, create, update, delete, watch]             │  │
  │  │    resources: [pods, deployments, services, configmaps, secrets] │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  RoleBinding: team-alpha-devs                                     │  │
  │  │    roleRef: developer                                             │  │
  │  │    subjects:                                                      │  │
  │  │    - kind: Group, name: team-alpha-devs                           │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Result: team-alpha-devs can manage pods/deployments/svc IN team-alpha  │
  │  but kubectl get pods -n team-beta returns FORBIDDEN ✅                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? ClusterRole is cluster-wide — binding it in one namespace still grants cluster-wide permissions. Why not C? cluster-admin gives FULL access to ALL namespaces — the opposite of isolation! Why not D? NetworkPolicy controls pod networking, not API access — RBAC is essential.

Platform Arch Q28 of 200
A platform team wants to ensure Team Alpha's pods only run on specific high-memory nodes. Which combination achieves this?
A) Label the high-memory nodes with team=alpha, add a nodeSelector: {team: alpha} to Team Alpha's pod specs, AND taint the nodes with team=alpha:NoSchedule with corresponding tolerations on Team Alpha's pods — so only their pods can schedule there
B) Create a separate Kubernetes cluster for Team Alpha — node-level isolation requires separate clusters
C) Use ResourceQuota to reserve CPU/memory exclusively for Team Alpha's namespace
D) Set the node's hostname to "team-alpha-node" — Kubernetes automatically routes Team Alpha pods to matching hostnames
✅ Correct Answer: A

Dedicated nodes per tenant use taints + tolerations + nodeSelector/affinity. Label nodes (team=alpha), taint them (team=alpha:NoSchedule) to repel other pods, and add tolerations to Team Alpha's pods so they can schedule on those nodes. This gives Team Alpha exclusive use of specific hardware while other teams use different nodes.

Node Affinity per Tenant
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Node: high-mem-1                    Node: high-mem-2                   │
  │  labels: team=alpha                  labels: team=alpha                 │
  │  taints: team=alpha:NoSchedule       taints: team=alpha:NoSchedule      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────┐         │
  │  │ anihpj-db-0            │         │ anihpj-db-1            │         │
  │  │ tolerations:           │         │ tolerations:           │         │
  │  │  team=alpha:NoSchedule │         │  team=alpha:NoSchedule │         │
  │  └────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────┘         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Node: standard-1                                                   │
  │  taints: (none)                                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐                                         │
  │  │ anihpj-api-0           │  ← Team Beta pods run here              │
  │  │ (no special toleration)│                                         │
  │  └────────────────────────┘                                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? Node isolation within a single cluster using taints/tolerations is more cost-effective than separate clusters. Why not C? ResourceQuota limits consumption — doesn't pin to specific nodes. Why not D? Hostname has no scheduling effect — Kubernetes doesn't route by hostname.

Platform Arch Q29 of 200
What is platform capacity planning, and which metric is most critical for forecasting when to add nodes?
A) Counting the total number of pods and ensuring it stays below 110 per node
B) Monitoring etcd database size and adding nodes when it exceeds 2GB
C) Analyzing historical resource utilization trends (CPU/memory requests vs allocatable capacity) and projecting when current nodes will reach saturation — typically targeting 70-80% allocation as the trigger to scale
D) Tracking the number of kubectl commands executed per minute as a proxy for cluster load
✅ Correct Answer: C

Capacity planning is about predicting when you'll run out of resources BEFORE it happens. Key metric: (total resource requests ÷ total allocatable capacity) across nodes. When this ratio reaches ~70-80%, it's time to add nodes or optimize. Tools like Kubecost, Kube-resource-report, and Prometheus recording rules help track this trend over weeks/months.

Capacity Planning Dashboard
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Cluster Capacity (Allocatable: 48 CPU, 192Gi RAM)                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  CPU:  ████████████████████░░░░░░░░  75% used (36/48)  ⚠️        │  │
  │  │  RAM:  ██████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░  58% used (112/192Gi) ✅     │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  📈 Trend: CPU utilization increasing 5%/week                    │  │
  │  │  ⏰ Projected: CPU saturation in ~4 weeks                         │  │
  │  │  🎯 Action: Add 2 nodes (8 CPU) within 2 weeks                   │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Pod count is secondary — CPU/memory saturation matters more. Why not B? etcd size relates to cluster state, not compute capacity. Why not D? kubectl command count is meaningless for capacity.

Platform Arch Q30 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between CapEx (Capital Expenditure) and OpEx (Operational Expenditure) models for platform infrastructure?
A) CapEx is always cheaper than OpEx for Kubernetes infrastructure regardless of scale
B) CapEx involves upfront investment in physical hardware/servers (owning assets, depreciating over time); OpEx is pay-as-you-go (cloud) — paying for what you use monthly with no upfront cost. Cloud native platforms typically favor OpEx for flexibility, but large-scale predictable workloads may benefit from CapEx (Reserved Instances, bare metal)
C) CapEx only applies to software licenses; OpEx only applies to hardware costs
D) Kubernetes cannot run on CapEx infrastructure — it requires OpEx cloud services
✅ Correct Answer: B

CapEx = buying servers upfront (high initial cost, depreciated over 3-5 years). OpEx = paying monthly for cloud resources (AWS/GCP/Azure bills). Cloud native platforms favor OpEx for elasticity — scale up during Black Friday, scale down after. But Reserved Instances (1-3 year commitment) blur the line, giving CapEx-like discounts in an OpEx model.

CapEx vs OpEx for Platform Infrastructure
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CapEx (Own)                          OpEx (Rent)                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Buy 10 servers: $100K    │        │ EKS cluster: $0.10/hr/node   │  │
  │  │ Depreciate over 3 years  │        │ RDS PostgreSQL: $0.30/hr     │  │
  │  │ Fixed capacity            │        │ Elastic capacity              │  │
  │  │ ✅ Predictable cost      │        │ ✅ No upfront investment      │  │
  │  │ ✅ Full control          │        │ ✅ Scale up/down instantly    │  │
  │  │ ❌ Upfront cash needed   │        │ ❌ Can be expensive at scale  │  │
  │  │ ❌ Over-provisioning risk│        │ ❌ Less control over hardware │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Hybrid: Reserved Instances (CapEx discount, OpEx billing)              │
  │  Spot Instances (extreme OpEx — 90% discount, but interruptible)        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Not always — cloud OpEx can be cheaper at small/medium scale. Why not C? Both apply to hardware, software, and services. Why not D? Kubernetes runs perfectly on bare metal (CapEx) — kubeadm clusters are common on-prem.

🚀 Category 2: GitOps & Continuous Delivery (25%)Q31-Q66

🟡 Intermediate: The Engine Room of Platform Delivery

This category covers how code flows from laptop to production. GitOps + CI/CD is the backbone of modern platform engineering. Every commit triggers automated pipelines that build, test, scan, and deploy — with Git as the single source of truth.

Q31-Q80 50 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

2.1 GitOps FundamentalsQ31-Q40

GitOps & CD Q31 of 200
What is the core principle of GitOps as a deployment methodology?
A) Using Git as a backup storage for Kubernetes YAML files alongside the container registry
B) Manually pushing kubectl apply commands from Git repositories to production clusters
C) Running all CI/CD pipelines directly inside the Kubernetes cluster without external tools
D) Git is the single source of truth for both application code AND infrastructure configuration — a GitOps agent (Argo CD/Flux) continuously reconciles the cluster state to match what's declared in the Git repository (pull-based, not push-based)
✅ Correct Answer: D

GitOps flips the deployment model: instead of CI pushing to the cluster (kubectl apply from Jenkins), a GitOps agent inside the cluster pulls from Git. Argo CD or Flux continuously watches the Git repo and reconciles — if someone manually changes a deployment, the agent reverts it to match Git. Git is the source of truth; the cluster is a reflection.

Push vs Pull (GitOps) Deployment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUSH (Traditional CI/CD)              PULL (GitOps — Argo CD / Flux)    │
  │  ┌──────────┐    kubectl apply         ┌──────────┐                     │
  │  │ CI Server│ ──────────────────▶      │ Git Repo │                     │
  │  │ (Jenkins)│         ┌─────────┐      └────┬─────┘                     │
  │  └──────────┘         │ Cluster │           │ watch & pull               │
  │                       └─────────┘           ▼                            │
  │  ❌ CI needs cluster                     ┌──────────────┐               │
  │     credentials                          │ Argo CD/Flux │               │
  │  ❌ Push may fail                        │ (in cluster) │               │
  │  ❌ Manual rollback                      └──────┬───────┘               │
  │                                               │ reconcile               │
  │  ✅ PULL (GitOps):                             ▼                         │
  │  • Git = source of truth                  ┌─────────┐                  │
  │  • Agent in cluster reconciles            │ Cluster │                  │
  │  • Auto-heal: reverts manual changes      └─────────┘                  │
  │  • Rollback = git revert + agent syncs                                │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Argo CD watches the anihpj-k8s Git repo. When a developer merges a PR that changes the anihpj-api image tag, Argo CD auto-syncs and deploys the new version. If someone manually edits a deployment, Argo CD reverts it within 3 minutes.

Why not A? Git is the source of truth, not just backup. Why not B? GitOps is pull-based, not push-based. Why not C? GitOps agents run in the cluster but pipelines can run anywhere.

GitOps & CD Q32 of 200
What is the App of Apps pattern in Argo CD, and why is it essential for platform engineering?
A) It runs all applications inside a single Kubernetes pod for resource efficiency
B) It combines multiple container images into one deployment manifest for faster deploys
C) A parent Argo CD Application that deploys child Applications — enabling one Git repo to declaratively manage dozens or hundreds of applications across multiple clusters. Adding a new app is a single YAML commit (no UI clicks needed)
D) It provides a GUI dashboard where developers can drag-and-drop applications between clusters
✅ Correct Answer: C

The App of Apps pattern means one "root" Application deploys other Applications. This makes Argo CD self-bootstrapping: install Argo CD, point it at your root app, and it deploys everything else. For a platform team managing 100+ services across 5 clusters, adding a new service is just a YAML commit — no Argo CD UI interaction needed.

App of Apps Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Git Repo: anihpj-platform                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  argocd/                                                          │  │
  │  │  ├── root-app.yaml        ← Root App (points to apps/ dir)       │  │
  │  │  └── apps/                                                        │  │
  │  │      ├── anihpj-dev.yaml   ← Child App → deploys to dev cluster  │  │
  │  │      ├── anihpj-staging.yaml ← Child App → deploys to staging    │  │
  │  │      ├── anihpj-prod.yaml  ← Child App → deploys to prod cluster │  │
  │  │      ├── monitoring.yaml   ← Child App → Prometheus/Grafana      │  │
  │  │      └── policies.yaml     ← Child App → Kyverno policies        │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  Argo CD syncs root-app → root-app syncs all children → everything     │
  │  managed from ONE repo, ONE commit, ONE pattern.                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? App of Apps is a management pattern, not a pod-scheduling strategy. Why not B? It manages Argo CD Applications, not container images. Why not D? It's declarative YAML, not drag-and-drop.

GitOps & CD Q33 of 200
What does Argo CD auto-sync with self-heal do that manual sync does not?
A) It automatically generates YAML manifests from application source code without human intervention
B) It continuously monitors the cluster and automatically reverts ANY manual changes (kubectl edit, kubectl scale) back to match the Git state — ensuring the cluster never drifts from the declared configuration
C) It automatically merges Git pull requests that modify Kubernetes manifests after CI tests pass
D) It syncs application data between multiple clusters for disaster recovery purposes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Auto-sync + self-heal is the killer GitOps feature: Argo CD detects drift (someone ran `kubectl scale deploy anihpj-api --replicas=5` directly on the cluster) and automatically reverts it back to Git's declared state (replicas: 3). This prevents "configuration drift" — the #1 source of production incidents where nobody knows why the cluster state differs from what's documented.

Auto-Sync + Self-Heal
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Git declares: replicas: 3                                              │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Time 0: Cluster has 3 replicas ✅                                      │
  │  Time 1: Someone runs: kubectl scale deploy anihpj-api --replicas=5    │
  │          Cluster now has 5 replicas ❌ (DRIFT!)                         │
  │  Time 2: Argo CD detects drift (cluster != git)                         │
  │  Time 3: Argo CD auto-syncs + self-heals → scales back to 3 replicas ✅│
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Result: Manual change lasted ~3 minutes before GitOps reverted it.     │
  │  Lesson: NEVER kubectl edit/scale directly — always commit to Git.      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Argo CD deploys existing manifests — doesn't generate them. Why not C? Argo CD watches for YAML changes in Git — doesn't merge PRs. Why not D? Data sync between clusters is a different concern.

GitOps & CD Q34 of 200
What does an Argo CD ApplicationSet provide that a regular Application does not?
A) It uses generators (list, cluster, Git, matrix) to automatically create multiple Applications from a template — for example, one Application per cluster, per branch, or per directory — without manually defining each one
B) It combines multiple Application resources into a single deployment for atomic rollouts across namespaces
C) It provides a web UI for developers to create applications without writing YAML
D) It automatically scales Application replicas based on cluster resource utilization
✅ Correct Answer: A

ApplicationSet = templated Application factory. Instead of creating 15 separate Argo CD Applications (one per cluster × environment), you define ONE ApplicationSet template with a generator. The generator expands it into the needed Applications. Common generators: list (explicit list), cluster (one per registered cluster), Git (one per directory/branch).

ApplicationSet Generators
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ApplicationSet: anihpj-deploy                                          │
  │  generators:                                                            │
  │  - list:                                                                │
  │      elements:                                                          │
  │      - env: dev, cluster: kind-dev                                      │
  │      - env: staging, cluster: kind-staging                              │
  │      - env: prod, cluster: eks-prod                                     │
  │  template:                                                              │
  │    project: anihpj                                                      │
  │    source: repoURL: github.com/anihpj/k8s, path: overlays/{{env}}       │
  │    destination: server: {{cluster}}, namespace: anihpj-{{env}}          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Generates 3 Applications: anihpj-dev, anihpj-staging, anihpj-prod     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's sync waves. Why not C? Argo CD has UI but ApplicationSet is YAML-driven. Why not D? ApplicationSet manages Applications, not pod replicas.

GitOps & CD Q35 of 200
What is Flux CD and how does it compare architecturally to Argo CD?
A) Flux is a push-based deployment tool; Argo CD is pull-based
B) Flux only supports Helm charts; Argo CD only supports raw YAML and Kustomize
C) Flux requires a separate database (PostgreSQL); Argo CD is fully self-contained in etcd
D) Both are pull-based GitOps tools that reconcile cluster state from Git. Flux uses a microservices architecture (source-controller, kustomize-controller, helm-controller) while Argo CD uses a monolithic application server. Flux is more composable; Argo CD has a richer built-in UI
✅ Correct Answer: D

Both Flux and Argo CD are CNCF-graduated GitOps tools. Flux's architecture is controller-based (each function is a separate controller), making it highly composable but requiring more initial setup. Argo CD is a single application with a rich web UI out of the box. Both achieve the same goal: pull-based reconciliation from Git.

Argo CD vs Flux CD
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Feature              Argo CD                Flux CD                    │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Architecture         Monolithic app        Microservice controllers   │
  │  UI                   Rich web UI            Web UI (newer)            │
  │  Multi-cluster        Native (central)       Per-cluster (decentral)   │
  │  Sync mechanism       Application CRD        Kustomize/Helm CRDs       │
  │  Auto-sync/self-heal  ✅                     ✅                         │
  │  ApplicationSet       ✅ (native)            ❌ (use Kustomize)        │
  │  Image automation     ❌ (external tool)     ✅ (image-reflector)      │
  │  CNCF status          Graduated              Graduated                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Both are pull-based. Why not B? Both support YAML, Kustomize, and Helm. Why not C? Neither requires an external database.

GitOps & CD Q36 of 200
What is the recommended Git repository structure for a monorepo GitOps setup managing multiple applications?
A) One flat directory with all YAML files mixed together for simplicity
B) Separate directories per application (apps/anihpj-api/, apps/anihpj-db/), each containing base/ and overlays/ for environment-specific configuration — with an App of Apps root pointing to the apps/ directory
C) One Git repository per application, each with its own Argo CD Application resource
D) Store all YAML in a database and use Argo CD's database connector to sync
✅ Correct Answer: B

Monorepo GitOps structure: apps/<app-name>/base/ (common config) and apps/<app-name>/overlays/<env>/ (env-specific patches). The App of Apps root points to apps/ and auto-discovers all child apps. Adding a new service is: create apps/new-service/, commit, done.

Monorepo GitOps Structure
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-k8s (Git repo)                                                  │
  │  ├── argocd/                                                            │
  │  │   └── root-app.yaml           ← App of Apps root                    │
  │  └── apps/                                                              │
  │      ├── anihpj-api/                                                    │
  │      │   ├── base/               ← deployment, service, configmap      │
  │      │   └── overlays/                                                  │
  │      │       ├── dev/            ← patches, images, replicas            │
  │      │       ├── staging/                                               │
  │      │       └── prod/                                                  │
  │      ├── anihpj-db/                                                     │
  │      ├── anihpj-redis/                                                  │
  │      └── monitoring/                                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Flat structure doesn't scale beyond 3-4 apps. Why not C? That's polyrepo — different tradeoffs (more isolation, more repo management). Why not D? GitOps = Git as source of truth, not a database.

GitOps & CD Q37 of 200
Why should Kubernetes Secrets NOT be stored as plain-text YAML in a GitOps repository, and what is the recommended approach?
A) Secrets CAN be stored in Git — Kubernetes automatically encrypts them when applied to the cluster
B) Store secrets in a separate private Git repository and use Argo CD to sync from both repos
C) Plain-text secrets in Git are a security risk (visible to anyone with repo access, forever in git history). Use Sealed Secrets, SOPS, External Secrets Operator, or HashiCorp Vault to encrypt secrets before committing, or sync them from an external secrets manager
D) Don't use Kubernetes Secrets — hardcode credentials in the application code instead
✅ Correct Answer: C

Git is forever — plain-text secrets committed to Git can be recovered from history even if deleted. Solutions: Sealed Secrets (encrypt with cluster's public key — only that cluster can decrypt), SOPS (encrypt with cloud KMS), External Secrets Operator (sync from AWS Secrets Manager/Vault to K8s Secrets at runtime), Vault (inject secrets via sidecar).

GitOps Secrets Management
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Approach              Git Contains        How It Works                 │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Sealed Secrets        Encrypted blob      Only target cluster decrypts │
  │  SOPS + Age/KMS        Encrypted file      Decrypted during CI/sync     │
  │  External Secrets      Reference only      Syncs from cloud SM at runtime│
  │  Vault Sidecar         Nothing             Injects at pod startup       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  For anihpj/jobpost: External Secrets Operator syncs AWS Secrets Manager│
  │  → K8s Secret → PostgreSQL password injected as env var. Git never sees │
  │  the actual password.                                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Kubernetes base64-encodes Secrets (not encrypted) — trivially decoded. Why not B? Private repos are still plain-text. Why not D? Never hardcode credentials — that's even worse.

GitOps & CD Q38 of 200
How can Crossplane be integrated into a GitOps workflow for managing cloud infrastructure?
A) Crossplane CRDs (like RDSInstance, S3Bucket) are committed to Git alongside application manifests. Argo CD/Flux syncs them → Crossplane provisions actual cloud resources (RDS, S3, IAM) — infrastructure becomes just another Kubernetes resource managed via GitOps
B) Crossplane replaces Argo CD — it handles both infrastructure provisioning AND application deployment
C) Crossplane requires a separate Git repository from the application manifests and a separate GitOps tool
D) Crossplane only works with Terraform and cannot be used with Kubernetes-native GitOps workflows
✅ Correct Answer: A

Crossplane extends Kubernetes with infrastructure CRDs. You commit RDSInstance, S3Bucket, IAMRole YAML to Git → Argo CD syncs → Crossplane provisions real AWS resources. Infrastructure-as-Code becomes Infrastructure-as-Data (K8s CRDs), managed via the same GitOps workflow as applications.

GitOps + Crossplane
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Git Repo: anihpj-platform                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  apps/anihpj-api/   ← Application manifests                     │  │
  │  │  infra/aws/                                                        │  │
  │  │  ├── rds-postgres.yaml    ← Crossplane RDSInstance CRD           │  │
  │  │  ├── s3-assets.yaml       ← Crossplane S3Bucket CRD              │  │
  │  │  └── iam-pod-role.yaml    ← Crossplane IAMRole CRD               │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Argo CD syncs → Crossplane sees CRDs → provisions actual AWS resources │
  │  RDS PostgreSQL, S3 bucket, IAM role appear in AWS console ✅           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? Crossplane handles infra, Argo CD handles deployment — complementary. Why not C? Same repo, same GitOps tool. Why not D? Crossplane is an alternative TO Terraform — K8s-native, not Terraform-only.

GitOps & CD Q39 of 200
How does GitOps enable disaster recovery after a complete cluster loss?
A) Argo CD automatically creates a new Kubernetes cluster if the original one is destroyed
B) GitOps tools back up etcd snapshots to Git repositories for cluster restoration
C) Disaster recovery requires manual re-creation of all YAML files — GitOps only helps with deployment, not recovery
D) Since ALL desired state is in Git, you create a new cluster, install Argo CD, point it at the same Git repo — and it redeploys EVERYTHING exactly as before. Recovery time = cluster creation time + sync time (minutes, not days)
✅ Correct Answer: D

GitOps makes disaster recovery trivial: the entire platform configuration lives in Git. New cluster → install Argo CD → point at repo → everything redeploys automatically. No manual reconstruction, no lost configs. Combined with Crossplane for infrastructure, you can recover the ENTIRE platform (infra + apps) from scratch using just Git.

GitOps Disaster Recovery
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Disaster: Production cluster completely destroyed                       │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Step 1: Create new EKS cluster (eksctl/Terraform, ~15 min)            │
  │  Step 2: Install Argo CD (kubectl apply, ~2 min)                       │
  │  Step 3: Apply root App of Apps (points to Git repo)                    │
  │  Step 4: Argo CD syncs: Crossplane provisions RDS/S3/IAM → apps deploy │
  │  Step 5: Restore DB from snapshot (Velero/RDS snapshot)                 │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Total recovery: ~30 minutes. All config from Git, all data from backup.│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Argo CD doesn't create clusters — you need eksctl/Terraform/Crossplane for that. Why not B? etcd snapshots are separate from GitOps (use Velero). Why not C? GitOps IS the recovery mechanism — all YAML is already in Git.

GitOps & CD Q40 of 200
What is the multi-cluster GitOps pattern using Argo CD, and why use it?
A) A central Argo CD instance manages Applications on multiple remote clusters — enabling one dashboard, one Git repo, and one control plane to deploy to dev/staging/prod clusters consistently. ApplicationSets automate per-cluster templating
B) Install separate Argo CD on each cluster and manually sync each one from different Git repositories
C) Run a single Argo CD that only manages one cluster at a time — switch between clusters manually
D) Use Argo CD to migrate applications between clusters automatically for load balancing
✅ Correct Answer: A

Multi-cluster GitOps with Argo CD: one central instance manages multiple remote clusters via their API server endpoints. One Git repo, one UI, consistent deployments everywhere. For anihpj/jobpost: the platform team manages dev, staging, prod EKS clusters from a single Argo CD instance — promoting from dev→staging→prod is a Git merge, not a manual deployment.

Multi-Cluster GitOps
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    Central Argo CD (management cluster)                   │
  │                              │                                           │
  │         ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐                     │
  │         ▼                    ▼                    ▼                      │
  │  ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐      ┌────────────┐                │
  │  │ Dev (kind)  │      │ Staging    │      │ Prod (EKS) │                │
  │  │ anihpj-dev  │      │ anihpj-stg │      │ anihpj-prod│                │
  │  └────────────┘      └────────────┘      └────────────┘                │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ApplicationSet generates per-cluster Applications from ONE template.   │
  │  Git merge to staging branch → Argo CD deploys to staging cluster.     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? Decentralized Argo CD per cluster works but loses unified visibility. Why not C? Argo CD manages multiple clusters simultaneously — no manual switching. Why not D? Argo CD deploys to clusters — doesn't migrate between them.

2.2 CI/CD Pipeline DesignQ41-Q52

GitOps & CD Q41 of 200
What is the key difference between Continuous Integration (CI), Continuous Delivery (CD), and Continuous Deployment?
A) CI tests code, CD builds containers, and Continuous Deployment monitors production — they are sequential pipeline stages
B) All three terms mean the same thing — automating the software release process
C) CI = automatically building and testing every commit. Continuous Delivery = every commit is deployable but requires manual approval to reach production. Continuous Deployment = every commit that passes tests is automatically deployed to production with NO manual approval
D) CI runs on developers' machines; CD runs in the cloud; Continuous Deployment runs on edge servers
✅ Correct Answer: C

CI (Continuous Integration): every commit triggers build + test. CD (Continuous Delivery): every commit is production-ready but deployment requires a manual approval (button push). Continuous Deployment: fully automated — passing tests = automatic production deploy. Most organizations start with CI → Continuous Delivery → eventually Continuous Deployment for low-risk services.

CI vs Continuous Delivery vs Continuous Deployment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CI (Continuous Integration)                                            │
  │  Commit → Build → Unit Test → Lint → [STOP — artifact created]         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Continuous Delivery (CI + manual deploy)                               │
  │  Commit → Build → Test → [STOP] → [MANUAL APPROVAL] → Deploy to Prod   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Continuous Deployment (CI + auto deploy)                               │
  │  Commit → Build → Test → Deploy to Prod (NO manual step)               │
  │                                                                         │
  │  For anihpj/jobpost: CI on every PR → Continuous Delivery for prod      │
  │  (manual approval) → Continuous Deployment for dev/staging (auto)       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? They're not sequential stages — they're levels of automation maturity. Why not B? They're distinct concepts. Why not D? Where they run is implementation detail, not the defining difference.

GitOps & CD Q42 of 200
What is the ideal pipeline stage order for a cloud native CI/CD pipeline building a containerized application?
A) Deploy → Test → Build → Security Scan → Notify
B) Lint → Unit Test → Build Image → Security Scan (Trivy) → Push to Registry → Deploy to Dev → Integration Test → Deploy to Staging → E2E Test → Deploy to Prod (with approval gate)
C) Build Image → Deploy to Prod → Monitor → Rollback if issues detected
D) Security Scan → Build Image → Deploy to Prod (skip dev/staging for speed)
✅ Correct Answer: B

The pipeline should fail fast: linting and unit tests run first (seconds), then build (minutes), then security scan (fail on CRITICAL CVEs), then progressive deployment through environments. "Shift-left" security: scan BEFORE deploying, not after.

CI/CD Pipeline for anihpj/jobpost
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GitHub PR opened                                                        │
  │     │                                                                    │
  │     ▼                                                                    │
  │  ┌─────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌───────────┐            │
  │  │  Lint   │ → │Unit Test │ → │  Build   │ → │ Trivy Scan│            │
  │  │ (30s)   │   │  (2min)  │   │ (3min)   │   │  (2min)   │            │
  │  └─────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └───────────┘            │
  │                                                    │                    │
  │                                                    ▼                    │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌───────────┐         │
  │  │ Push to ECR  │→ │ Deploy   │→ │Integration│→ │ Deploy    │         │
  │  │              │  │ to Dev   │  │  Tests    │  │ to Staging│         │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └───────────┘         │
  │                                                       │                │
  │                                                       ▼                │
  │                                               ┌──────────────┐        │
  │                                               │ E2E Tests    │        │
  │                                               └──────────────┘        │
  │                                                       │                │
  │                                                       ▼                │
  │                                               ┌──────────────┐        │
  │                                               │ Manual Approv│        │
  │                                               │ → Deploy Prod│        │
  │                                               └──────────────┘        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Deploy before test is backward. Why not C? Deploy-to-prod-first is reckless. Why not D? Skipping dev/staging removes safety nets.

GitOps & CD Q43 of 200
What is the OIDC (OpenID Connect) integration between GitHub Actions and AWS, and why is it preferred over storing AWS credentials as GitHub Secrets?
A) OIDC allows GitHub Actions to assume an AWS IAM role using a trust relationship — NO long-lived AWS credentials are stored anywhere. GitHub provides a short-lived token, AWS verifies it, and grants temporary credentials. This eliminates the risk of leaked AWS access keys
B) OIDC encrypts AWS credentials stored in GitHub Secrets using a rotating key managed by AWS KMS
C) OIDC is a GitHub Actions plugin that automatically rotates AWS access keys every 24 hours
D) OIDC replaces IAM entirely — you don't need AWS IAM roles when using OIDC with GitHub Actions
✅ Correct Answer: A

OIDC is the modern, secure way to connect CI/CD to AWS: configure an IAM OIDC identity provider, create an IAM role with a trust policy allowing github.com/org/repo to assume it, and GitHub Actions gets temporary credentials via `aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials`. No static access keys stored anywhere — no keys to leak, rotate, or compromise.

GitHub Actions OIDC → AWS
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GitHub Actions                                    AWS                  │
  │  ┌────────────────┐                              ┌──────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Workflow runs  │ ① Request OIDC token         │ IAM OIDC Provider│  │
  │  │                │─────────────────────────────▶│ (trusts GitHub)  │  │
  │  │                │ ② AWS returns temp creds      │                  │  │
  │  │                │◀─────────────────────────────│ IAM Role:        │  │
  │  │                │                              │ anihpj-gh-actions│  │
  │  │ ③ Uses temp    │                              │ (push to ECR,    │  │
  │  │   creds to     │ ④ Push image to ECR          │  deploy to EKS)  │  │
  │  │   push/deploy  │─────────────────────────────▶│                  │  │
  │  └────────────────┘                              └──────────────────┘  │
  │  ✅ No static credentials stored                                         │
  │  ✅ Tokens expire in 1 hour                                            │
  │  ✅ IAM role scoped to minimum permissions                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? OIDC doesn't encrypt GitHub Secrets — it eliminates the need for them. Why not C? OIDC provides temporary credentials, not key rotation. Why not D? OIDC works WITH IAM roles, doesn't replace them.

GitOps & CD Q44 of 200
What is the SLSA Framework (Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts) and what does Level 3 require?
A) SLSA is a container runtime security tool that detects anomalies in running pods
B) SLSA Level 3 only requires that source code is stored in a version control system
C) SLSA is a replacement for Kubernetes RBAC — it provides fine-grained access control for CI/CD pipelines
D) SLSA is a security framework for software supply chain integrity. Level 3 requires: auditable build platform, isolated build environments, parameterless and hermetic builds, signed provenance attestations, and verified artifact identities — preventing tampering between source and deployment
✅ Correct Answer: D

SLSA (pronounced "salsa") protects against supply chain attacks like SolarWinds. Level 1: basic provenance. Level 2: hosted build platform. Level 3: hardened builds (hermetic, isolated, parameterless) + attested provenance. Level 4: two-person review + hermeticity at the highest level. For CNPE, SLSA Level 3 is the gold standard for CI/CD security.

SLSA Levels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Level 1                             Level 3                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ • Source in Git          │       │ • Everything in Level 1+2    │   │
  │  │ • Automated build        │       │ • Hermetic build (no network)│   │
  │  │ • Provenance generated   │       │ • Isolated build environment │   │
  │  │                          │       │ • Parameterless (no user     │   │
  │  │                          │       │   input to build)            │   │
  │  │                          │       │ • Signed attestation         │   │
  │  │                          │       │ • Non-falsifiable provenance │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's Falco, not SLSA. Why not B? Source in Git is SLSA Level 1 — Level 3 requires far more. Why not C? SLSA is about supply chain integrity, not RBAC.

GitOps & CD Q45 of 200
What is image digest pinning (using sha256 instead of tags) and why is it critical for secure deployments?
A) Digest pinning stores the image locally on each node to avoid pulling from the registry on every restart
B) Digest pinning compresses images using SHA-256 hashing to reduce registry storage costs
C) Digest pinning references an image by its immutable sha256 hash (e.g., anihpj-api@sha256:abc123...) instead of a mutable tag (e.g., :latest). This prevents TOCTOU attacks where a tag is retagged to a malicious image between your scan and deploy
D) Digest pinning encrypts the container image layers using SHA-256 for confidentiality in transit
✅ Correct Answer: C

Tags are mutable pointers — `anihpj-api:v1.2.3` today might point to a different image tomorrow if someone re-tags. A sha256 digest is the cryptographic hash of the image manifest — immutable and unique. You scan the digest, verify it's clean, then deploy the exact same digest. This prevents the "time of check, time of use" (TOCTOU) attack.

Tag vs Digest
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ Image Tags (mutable):                                               │
  │  Day 1: anihpj-api:v1  →  sha256:aaa  (good image)                    │
  │  Day 2: anihpj-api:v1  →  sha256:bbb  (SAME TAG, DIFFERENT IMAGE!)    │
  │  If you deploy v1 on Day 2, you get a different image than Day 1.     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ Image Digest (immutable):                                           │
  │  anihpj-api@sha256:aaa = ALWAYS the same image, forever.               │
  │  Scan sha256:aaa → deploy sha256:aaa → same image guaranteed.          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent. Why not B? Digest is a hash reference, not compression. Why not D? Digest is for identification, not encryption.

GitOps & CD Q46 of 200
Why should kaniko or buildpacks be used for building container images in CI/CD pipelines instead of Docker-in-Docker (DinD)?
A) Kaniko produces smaller images than Docker by default
B) Docker-in-Docker requires privileged mode (security risk) and access to the host Docker socket (container escape vector). Kaniko and buildpacks build images in userspace without any special privileges — they are designed for secure CI/CD environments
C) Kaniko is faster than Docker because it caches layers in the container registry instead of locally
D) Docker-in-Docker only works on Linux; Kaniko works on all operating systems
✅ Correct Answer: B

Never use Docker-in-Docker in CI/CD. It requires mounting the host's Docker socket (`/var/run/docker.sock`) into the CI container — effectively giving the CI job root access to the host. Kaniko runs entirely in userspace (no daemon, no privileged mode), building OCI images from a Dockerfile without any special permissions.

Kaniko vs DinD
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DinD (Dangerous)                        Kaniko (Secure)                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ CI Container             │          │ CI Container             │    │
  │  │ ┌──────────────────────┐ │          │ ┌──────────────────────┐ │    │
  │  │ │ docker build         │ │          │ │ /kaniko/executor     │ │    │
  │  │ │ (needs docker socket)│ │          │ │ (no daemon, no priv) │ │    │
  │  │ └──────────┬───────────┘ │          │ └──────────────────────┘ │    │
  │  │            │              │          │                          │    │
  │  │            ▼              │          │  Reads Dockerfile        │    │
  │  │  /var/run/docker.sock     │          │  Builds layers in user   │    │
  │  │  ⚠️  ESCAPE VECTOR!       │          │  space                   │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘          │  Pushes to registry      │    │
  │                                         └──────────────────────────┘    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Image size depends on the Dockerfile, not the build tool. Why not C? Both can cache — Kaniko caches in registry, Docker caches locally. Why not D? Both work primarily on Linux.

GitOps & CD Q47 of 200
What is progressive delivery and which strategies fall under it?
A) Progressive delivery gradually shifts traffic to a new version while monitoring metrics — if metrics degrade, it automatically rolls back. Strategies include: Rolling Update (replace pods incrementally), Blue/Green (switch all traffic at once between two environments), Canary (shift 5%→25%→50%→100% traffic gradually with analysis), and A/B Testing (route based on headers/cookies)
B) Progressive delivery means deploying to one environment at a time in sequence: dev → staging → production
C) Progressive delivery only refers to Canary deployments — Blue/Green and Rolling are separate concepts
D) Progressive delivery is a feature exclusive to Argo Rollouts and cannot be implemented with standard Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: A

Progressive delivery = risk-managed deployments with automated feedback loops. Instead of "deploy and pray," you gradually expose the new version, watch metrics (error rate, latency), and either promote or rollback automatically. Argo Rollouts implements this natively in Kubernetes with BlueGreen and Canary strategies + AnalysisRuns.

Progressive Delivery Strategies
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Rolling Update: Replace pods 1-by-1 (newPodReady → deleteOldPod)       │
  │  Blue/Green:     Two full environments, switch Service selector at once │
  │  Canary:         5% traffic → check metrics → 25% → 50% → 100%         │
  │  A/B Testing:    Route based on headers (x-user-type:beta → new version)│
  │                                                                         │
  │  For anihpj/jobpost: Argo Rollouts canary-deploys the API with 10%      │
  │  traffic → checks error rate for 5 min → 50% → 100% if metrics pass.   │
  │  If error rate spikes → automatic rollback.                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's a deployment pipeline, not progressive delivery. Why not C? Blue/Green and Rolling are also progressive strategies. Why not D? Argo Rollouts extends K8s — it's not the only implementation.

GitOps & CD Q48 of 200
What is an Argo Rollouts AnalysisRun and how does it prevent bad deployments?
A) It analyzes the application source code for syntax errors before building the container image
B) It runs security vulnerability scans on the container image and blocks deployment if CRITICAL CVEs are found
C) During a canary or blue/green rollout, it queries metrics from Prometheus/Datadog/NewRelic (error rate, latency, success rate) and compares them against the stable version. If metrics degrade beyond configured thresholds, the AnalysisRun fails — triggering an automatic rollback
D) It analyzes cluster resource utilization to determine the optimal number of canary replicas
✅ Correct Answer: C

AnalysisRun is the "safety net" of progressive delivery. During a canary deployment (10% traffic to new version), an AnalysisRun queries Prometheus every 30 seconds: "Is the error rate > 1%? Is p99 latency > 500ms?" If yes → AnalysisRun fails → Argo Rollouts auto-rolls back to the stable version. No human needed — fully automated safety.

Argo Rollouts AnalysisRun
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Canary: 10% → AnalysisRun → 50% → AnalysisRun → 100%                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  AnalysisRun queries Prometheus:                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  metrics:                                                         │  │
  │  │  - name: error-rate                                               │  │
  │  │    successCondition: result[0] <= 0.01   # < 1% errors           │  │
  │  │    provider: prometheus                                           │  │
  │  │    query: |                                                       │  │
  │  │      sum(rate(http_errors{version="canary"}[5m])) /               │  │
  │  │      sum(rate(http_requests{version="canary"}[5m]))               │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  If error-rate > 1% → AnalysisRun fails → Auto-rollback ✅             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's linting/compilation — happens in CI, not during rollout. Why not B? That's Trivy/Grype image scanning. Why not D? That's HPA or Karpenter's job.

GitOps & CD Q49 of 200
What is artifact promotion in a CI/CD pipeline, and why use separate container registries per environment?
A) Artifact promotion means deploying the same YAML files to all environments without modification
B) Artifact promotion means rebuilding the container image for each environment with environment-specific configurations baked in
C) Separate registries per environment are an anti-pattern — a single registry with tags is sufficient
D) Artifact promotion means the EXACT same image (same sha256 digest) is promoted through environments — built once, tested in dev, promoted to staging, then to prod. Separate registries (dev-ecr, prod-ecr) prevent accidental deployment of unapproved images to production
✅ Correct Answer: D

"Build once, deploy many." The image built in CI is the EXACT image (same digest) that runs in dev, staging, and prod. You NEVER rebuild for different environments — that would mean you're testing a different binary than what goes to production. Separate registries provide an air gap: prod cluster can only pull from prod-ecr, which only contains promoted images.

Artifact Promotion
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CI builds image@sha256:abc → push to dev-ecr                           │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── Deploy to Dev (same digest) ✅                                │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── Integration tests pass ✅                                     │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── Promote: copy image@sha256:abc to staging-ecr                 │
  │       │   Deploy to Staging (same digest) ✅                             │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── E2E tests pass, manual approval ✅                             │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       └── Promote: copy image@sha256:abc to prod-ecr                    │
  │           Deploy to Production (same digest) ✅                          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ⚠️  NEVER rebuild for staging/prod — you'd deploy an untested binary!  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? YAML may differ per env (replicas, resources). Why not B? Rebuilding per env defeats the purpose — deploy what you tested. Why not C? Separate registries are a security best practice.

GitOps & CD Q50 of 200
What security checks should be integrated into a CI/CD pipeline before deploying to production (the "shift-left" approach)?
A) Only unit tests — security testing happens post-deployment by the security team
B) SAST (static analysis), secret scanning (detect hardcoded credentials), dependency scanning (vulnerable libraries), container image scanning (Trivy/Grype for CVEs), IaC scanning (misconfigurations in Terraform/K8s YAML), and signed provenance verification — ALL before the image reaches a registry
C) Penetration testing by an external security firm — performed annually after deployment
D) Manual code review by the security team — automated scanning is too prone to false positives
✅ Correct Answer: B

"Shift-left" means moving security checks as early as possible in the pipeline — before the image is even built, before it reaches a registry, before it deploys. The earlier you catch a vulnerability, the cheaper and faster it is to fix. A CRITICAL CVE caught during CI takes minutes to fix; the same CVE in production is a P0 incident.

Shift-Left Security in CI/CD
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pipeline Stage          Security Check               Tool              │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Pre-commit              Secret scanning              git-leaks, ggshield│
  │  PR open                 SAST (code analysis)         SonarQube, Semgrep│
  │  PR open                 Dependency scan              Dependabot, Snyk  │
  │  Post-build              Image CVE scan               Trivy, Grype      │
  │  Post-build              IaC scan (K8s YAML)          KICS, checkov     │
  │  Pre-deploy              Policy check (OPA/Kyverno)   Conftest          │
  │  Pre-deploy              Signed attestation verify    Cosign            │
  │  Runtime                 Behavioral monitoring        Falco             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Block deployment if: CRITICAL CVE found OR IaC misconfig OR unsigned   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Post-deployment security is too late — shift-left catches issues earlier. Why not C? Pen testing is important but not sufficient alone. Why not D? Automated scanning scales; manual review alone can't keep up.

GitOps & CD Q51 of 200
What is the purpose of Tekton Pipelines in a cloud native CI/CD architecture?
A) Tekton replaces Kubernetes — it's a standalone CI/CD platform that runs outside the cluster
B) Tekton is a Kubernetes-native CI/CD framework where pipelines, tasks, and triggers are defined as CRDs and executed as pods on the cluster — making CI/CD fully declarative, scalable, and integrated with the platform
C) Tekton is a GUI-based CI/CD tool similar to Jenkins Blue Ocean that requires a separate server
D) Tekton only handles container image building and cannot run tests or deployments
✅ Correct Answer: B

Tekton brings CI/CD into Kubernetes: Tasks, Pipelines, and Triggers are CRDs. Each Task step runs as a container in a pod. This means CI/CD inherits Kubernetes features — scaling, resource management, RBAC, and observability — without running a separate CI server. For a platform team, Tekton pipelines are just more Kubernetes resources to manage.

Tekton — Kubernetes-Native CI/CD
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Tekton CRDs:                                                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Task: "git-clone"           Pipeline: "anihpj-ci"               │  │
  │  │    steps:                      tasks:                             │  │
  │  │    - name: clone               - name: git-clone                 │  │
  │  │      image: alpine/git         - name: tests                     │  │
  │  │      script: git clone ...     - name: build-and-push            │  │
  │  │                                                            │  │
  │  │  PipelineRun triggers execution → each task runs as Pod → logs  │  │
  │  │  in kubectl, metrics in Prometheus, RBAC controlled             │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Tekton runs ON Kubernetes, doesn't replace it. Why not C? Tekton is declarative YAML, not GUI-based. Why not D? Tekton can run any container — tests, builds, deploys.

GitOps & CD Q52 of 200
What is the recommended image tagging strategy for production container images?
A) Use immutable tags: git commit SHA (anihpj-api:abc123f), semantic version + build (v2.1.0-b45), or sha256 digest pinning. NEVER use :latest in production — it's mutable and unpredictable
B) Use the :latest tag for all deployments — Kubernetes will automatically pull the newest version
C) Use environment-specific tags (anihpj-api:prod, anihpj-api:staging) — rebuild the image for each environment
D) Use date-based tags (anihpj-api:2026-06-14) — they're unique and easy to understand
✅ Correct Answer: A

:latest is a disaster waiting to happen. It's a mutable pointer — today's :latest might be a different image than yesterday's. Use git SHA (immutable, traceable to code), semver (human-readable), and ALWAYS pin by sha256 digest in production for cryptographic certainty. `anihpj-api@sha256:def456...` guarantees you're running exactly what you tested.

Image Tagging Best Practices
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Tag Strategy      Example                 Pros / Cons                  │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  :latest            anihpj-api:latest       ❌ Mutable, unpredictable    │
  │  Git SHA            anihpj-api:abc123f      ✅ Immutable, traceable     │
  │  Semver             anihpj-api:v2.1.0       ✅ Human-readable           │
  │  Semver+build       anihpj-api:v2.1.0-b45   ✅ Version + build number   │
  │  SHA256 digest      anihpj-api@sha256:...   ✅ Cryptographically unique │
  │  Date-based          anihpj-api:2026-06-14  ⚠️  Unique but no version   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Best: Tag with git SHA + semver, DEPLOY by sha256 digest.              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? :latest is the #1 cause of "worked yesterday, broken today" incidents. Why not C? Never rebuild per environment — deploy the same image. Why not D? Date tags don't indicate version or code changes.

2.3 Helm & Configuration ManagementQ53-Q58

GitOps & CD Q53 of 200
What is Helm, and what problem does it solve in Kubernetes?
A) Helm is a Kubernetes security scanner that checks deployments for vulnerabilities
