B) Helm is the package manager for Kubernetes — it bundles related YAML manifests into Charts with templating (values.yaml), versioning, and lifecycle hooks, enabling reusable, shareable, and parameterized application deployments
C) Helm replaces kubectl — all Kubernetes operations must go through Helm after it's installed
D) Helm is a monitoring tool that collects metrics from Kubernetes clusters and displays dashboards
✅ Correct Answer: B

Helm = apt/yum/brew for Kubernetes. Instead of managing 15 separate YAML files (deployment.yaml, service.yaml, configmap.yaml, ingress.yaml...), you package them into a Helm Chart with `values.yaml` for parameterization. `helm install anihpj ./chart --set replicas=5` deploys everything. Charts are versioned, shareable via OCI registries, and support pre/post-install hooks for database migrations.

Helm Chart Structure
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-chart/                                                          │
  │  ├── Chart.yaml          ← Metadata (name, version, appVersion)        │
  │  ├── values.yaml         ← Default configuration                       │
  │  ├── templates/                                                         │
  │  │   ├── deployment.yaml ← Uses {{ .Values.replicas }}                 │
  │  │   ├── service.yaml    ← Uses {{ .Values.service.port }}            │
  │  │   ├── ingress.yaml    ← Uses {{ .Values.ingress.host }}            │
  │  │   └── configmap.yaml  ← Uses {{ .Values.config }}                  │
  │  └── charts/             ← Sub-charts (dependencies)                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  helm install anihpj ./chart -f prod-values.yaml                       │
  │  → Renders templates → Applies all manifests → Tracks as a "release"   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's Trivy/Grype. Why not C? Helm works alongside kubectl, doesn't replace it. Why not D? That's Prometheus/Grafana.

GitOps & CD Q54 of 200
When should you use Kustomize instead of Helm for Kubernetes configuration management?
A) Kustomize should always be used — Helm is deprecated and no longer maintained
B) Kustomize is only for development; Helm is required for production deployments
C) Kustomize replaces the need for any YAML — it generates manifests from code annotations
D) Kustomize is ideal when you need template-free, purely declarative configuration variance (base + overlays pattern) without Go templating. Helm is better for packaging, distribution, and sharing via repositories. They're complementary — Kustomize is built into kubectl (kubectl apply -k)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kustomize = "base + overlays" pattern. Define a base configuration (common to all environments), then overlay environment-specific patches. No templates, no Go syntax — just YAML patching YAML. Helm = packaging + templating + sharing. Use Kustomize for environment variance within a team; use Helm to distribute to other teams/organizations. Argo CD supports both natively.

Helm vs Kustomize
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Kustomize (base + overlay)              Helm (template + values)       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ base/                    │          │ templates/deployment.yaml │    │
  │  │   deployment.yaml       │          │   replicas: {{ .Values.r }}│   │
  │  │ overlays/                │          │ values.yaml:              │    │
  │  │   dev/                   │          │   r: 3                    │    │
  │  │     kustomization.yaml  │          │ values-prod.yaml:         │    │
  │  │       replicas: 2       │          │   r: 10                   │    │
  │  │   prod/                  │          │                            │    │
  │  │     kustomization.yaml  │          │ helm template → YAML       │    │
  │  │       replicas: 10      │          └──────────────────────────┘    │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                                         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Kustomize: No templates, pure YAML patching. Built into kubectl.     │
  │  Helm: Go templates, packaging, OCI distribution, hooks.              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Helm is actively maintained (CNCF graduated). Why not B? Both work in production. Why not C? Kustomize patches existing YAML — doesn't generate from code.

GitOps & CD Q55 of 200
What does helm rollback do, and when would you use it?
A) It deletes all Kubernetes resources and recreates them from scratch using the original values
B) It reverts the Helm chart files in your Git repository to a previous version
C) It reverts a Helm release to a previous revision — Helm tracks every install/upgrade as a numbered revision. If a new deployment breaks your app, `helm rollback anihpj 3` instantly reverts to revision 3's manifests and configuration
D) It rolls back individual pods one at a time like a Deployment rolling update reversal
✅ Correct Answer: C

Helm maintains a release history in Kubernetes Secrets. Every `helm install` and `helm upgrade` creates a new numbered revision. `helm history anihpj` shows all revisions. `helm rollback anihpj 3` reverts to revision 3 within seconds — faster than any manual YAML fix. This is the #1 reason to use Helm: instant, reliable rollbacks.

Helm Rollback
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ helm history anihpj                                                   │
  │  REVISION  UPDATED                   STATUS      CHART         DESC     │
  │  1         Mon Jun 9 10:00:00        superseded  anihpj-1.0.0  Install  │
  │  2         Tue Jun 10 14:30:00       superseded  anihpj-1.1.0  Upgrade  │
  │  3         Wed Jun 11 09:15:00       superseded  anihpj-1.2.0  Upgrade  │
  │  4         Thu Jun 12 16:45:00       deployed    anihpj-2.0.0  Upgrade  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  anihpj v2.0.0 is broken → $ helm rollback anihpj 3                    │
  │  → Instantly reverts to v1.2.0's exact manifests and config            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's uninstall + reinstall. Why not B? Helm doesn't touch Git. Why not D? That's kubectl rollout undo, not Helm rollback.

GitOps & CD Q56 of 200
What is a Helm hook and what is a common use case?
A) Helm hooks are Jobs that run at specific lifecycle points (pre-install, post-upgrade, pre-delete). Common use: a post-upgrade hook runs database migrations AFTER the new deployment is applied but BEFORE it's considered "upgraded"
B) Helm hooks are webhook notifications sent to Slack/Teams when a release succeeds or fails
C) Helm hooks are Git webhooks that trigger Argo CD syncs when the chart repository changes
D) Helm hooks are Kubernetes admission webhooks registered by Helm to validate chart values
✅ Correct Answer: A

Helm hooks are annotated Kubernetes resources (usually Jobs) that execute at specific lifecycle points. `helm.sh/hook: post-upgrade` runs a migration job after the new pods are running. If the hook fails, the upgrade is marked as failed and can be rolled back. Hooks ensure ordered operations: backup before upgrade, migrate after deploy, cleanup before delete.

Helm Hooks Lifecycle
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  helm upgrade anihpj ./chart                                            │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  pre-upgrade  → Backup database (Job)                                   │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  UPGRADE → New pods roll out                                            │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  post-upgrade → Run Django migrations (Job)                             │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  post-upgrade succeeds → Release marked "deployed" ✅                   │
  │  OR                                                                     │
  │  post-upgrade FAILS → Release marked "failed" → helm rollback 🔄       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's Alertmanager/Slack integration. Why not C? That's Argo CD webhooks. Why not D? Admission webhooks are cluster-level, not Helm-specific.

GitOps & CD Q57 of 200
What is an Umbrella Chart in Helm, and why use it for microservices?
A) An umbrella chart deploys all services into a single Kubernetes pod for efficiency
B) An umbrella chart is a Helm chart that lists other charts as dependencies in Chart.yaml — enabling one command to deploy an entire microservice suite (anihpj-web + anihpj-api + anihpj-db + redis + monitoring) with coordinated versioning
C) An umbrella chart provides a graphical dashboard showing all running Helm releases
D) An umbrella chart encrypts all sub-charts into a single secure package for air-gapped deployments
✅ Correct Answer: B

Umbrella chart = meta-chart that depends on other charts. `Chart.yaml` lists dependencies (anihpj-api v2.1.0, postgresql v15.0.0, redis v18.0.0). `helm install anihpj-platform ./umbrella-chart` deploys the entire stack. This ensures consistent versioning across services — you test the umbrella, not individual charts.

Umbrella Chart
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-umbrella/Chart.yaml:                                            │
  │  dependencies:                                                          │
  │  - name: anihpj-web, version: "2.1.0"                                  │
  │  - name: anihpj-api, version: "3.0.1"                                  │
  │  - name: postgresql, version: "15.0.0", repository: oci://...          │
  │  - name: redis, version: "18.0.0", repository: oci://...               │
  │                                                                         │
  │  helm install anihpj ./umbrella → 4 services deployed, 1 command      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Each chart deploys independently as separate resources. Why not C? That's a dashboard tool, not a Helm concept. Why not D? Umbrella charts manage dependencies, not encryption.

GitOps & CD Q58 of 200
What does helm upgrade --install do that helm install alone does not?
A) It upgrades the Helm binary itself to the latest version before installing the chart
B) It performs a rolling update of existing pods without creating a new Helm release revision
C) It combines install and upgrade: if the release doesn't exist, it installs; if it already exists, it upgrades. This makes CI/CD pipelines simpler — one command works for both first deploy and subsequent updates
D) It upgrades all CRDs in the cluster before installing the chart to ensure API compatibility
✅ Correct Answer: C

`helm upgrade --install` is idempotent — run it once or a hundred times, same result. CI/CD pipelines love this: no need to check if the release exists. `helm upgrade --install anihpj ./chart --values prod.yaml` works for first deploy AND future updates. Combine with `--create-namespace` for fully automated deployments.

helm upgrade --install (Idempotent)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  First run:  helm upgrade --install anihpj ./chart --create-namespace  │
  │  → Release "anihpj" does not exist. Installing it now.                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Second run: helm upgrade --install anihpj ./chart --create-namespace  │
  │  → Release "anihpj" has been upgraded. Happy Helming!                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Same command, idempotent, CI/CD friendly.                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? It doesn't touch the Helm binary. Why not B? It does create a new revision. Why not D? CRD management is separate from install/upgrade.

2.4 Progressive DeliveryQ59-Q62

GitOps & CD Q59 of 200
What distinguishes a Blue/Green deployment from a Canary deployment?
A) Blue/Green deploys to one pod at a time; Canary deploys all pods simultaneously
B) Blue/Green maintains TWO complete environments (Blue=old, Green=new) and switches ALL traffic at once by updating a Service selector. Canary gradually shifts a PERCENTAGE of traffic (5%→25%→100%) to the new version while monitoring metrics
C) Blue/Green is for database changes; Canary is for application code changes
D) Blue/Green requires Argo Rollouts; Canary is built into standard Kubernetes Deployments
✅ Correct Answer: B

Blue/Green = instant switch (all-or-nothing). Canary = gradual shift (percentage-based). Blue/Green requires double the resources (two full environments) but provides instant rollback (switch selector back). Canary uses less resources but requires metric analysis to decide whether to promote or rollback.

Blue/Green vs Canary
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Blue/Green:                     Canary:                                │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐                           │
  │  │ Blue (v1)│ │Green (v2)│      │ v1: 90%  │  v2: 10%  → check metrics │
  │  │ 3 pods   │ │ 3 pods   │      │ v1: 50%  │  v2: 50%  → check metrics │
  │  └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘      │ v1: 0%   │  v2: 100% → promoted!    │
  │       │            │            └──────────┘                           │
  │  Service selector: app=v1       If metrics fail → auto-rollback        │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │  Switch selector: app=v2 → ALL traffic to Green instantly              │
  │  Rollback: switch selector back → ALL traffic to Blue instantly        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Reversed — Blue/Green switches all at once. Why not C? Both strategies work for any change. Why not D? Both can be done with standard Deployments + Services, or with Argo Rollouts for automation.

GitOps & CD Q60 of 200
What does Flagger provide for progressive delivery on Kubernetes?
A) Flagger automates canary deployments using service mesh (Istio/Linkerd) or ingress controllers — it gradually shifts traffic, queries metrics from Prometheus/Datadog, and automatically promotes or rolls back based on success thresholds
B) Flagger is a Kubernetes operator that automatically scales pods based on HTTP request rates
C) Flagger replaces Argo CD — it handles both GitOps synchronization and progressive delivery
D) Flagger is a CLI tool for developers to manually flag deployments for production rollout
✅ Correct Answer: A

Flagger integrates with service meshes (Istio, Linkerd) and ingress controllers (NGINX, Contour) to automate canary analysis. It creates a Canary CRD, shifts traffic gradually, runs acceptance tests and metric analysis, and auto-promotes or rollbacks. Flagger + Flux = full GitOps with progressive delivery.

Flagger Canary Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Flagger Canary CRD:                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  analysis:                                                        │  │
  │  │    interval: 30s              # Check every 30 seconds           │  │
  │  │    threshold: 5               # Must pass 5 consecutive checks    │  │
  │  │    maxWeight: 50              # Max 50% canary traffic           │  │
  │  │    stepWeight: 10             # Increase by 10% each step        │  │
  │  │    metrics:                                                       │  │
  │  │    - name: request-success-rate                                  │  │
  │  │      thresholdRange:                                           │  │
  │  │        min: 99                # Must be > 99% success rate       │  │
  │  │    - name: request-duration                                      │  │
  │  │      thresholdRange:                                           │  │
  │  │        max: 500               # Must be < 500ms p99 latency     │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's HPA/KEDA. Why not C? Flagger complements Flux/Argo CD, doesn't replace them. Why not D? Flagger is a Kubernetes operator, not a CLI tool.

GitOps & CD Q61 of 200
What do maxSurge and maxUnavailable control in a Kubernetes Rolling Update?
A) maxSurge limits total pods in the cluster; maxUnavailable limits failed pods
B) maxSurge controls how many new pods can be created ABOVE the desired count during rollout; maxUnavailable controls how many old pods can be UNAVAILABLE during rollout — together they ensure the service maintains capacity during updates
C) maxSurge controls maximum CPU usage per pod; maxUnavailable controls minimum memory per pod
D) maxSurge prevents autoscaling during rollouts; maxUnavailable prevents scheduling new pods
✅ Correct Answer: B

Rolling update = replace old pods with new ones gradually. `maxSurge: 1` allows 1 extra pod beyond desired count (faster rollout, more resources). `maxUnavailable: 0` ensures no pod is unavailable during rollout (zero-downtime, but slower). For anihpj-api with 3 replicas: surge=1, unavailable=0 → at most 4 pods running, always 3 available.

Rolling Update — maxSurge & maxUnavailable
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  replicas: 3, maxSurge: 1, maxUnavailable: 0  (zero-downtime)          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Step 1: [v1] [v1] [v1]            (3 old pods)                        │
  │  Step 2: [v1] [v1] [v1] [v2]       (surge +1 = 4 pods)                │
  │  Step 3: [v1] [v1] [v2]            (1 old terminated, 3 pods)          │
  │  Step 4: [v1] [v1] [v2] [v2]       (surge +1)                          │
  │  Step 5: [v1] [v2] [v2]            (3 pods)                            │
  │  Step 6: [v1] [v2] [v2] [v2]       (surge +1)                          │
  │  Step 7: [v2] [v2] [v2]            (all new, rollout complete)         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  At every step: 3 pods available → zero downtime ✅                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A/C/D? None describe the actual rolling update mechanism.

GitOps & CD Q62 of 200
What is Feature Flags (Feature Toggles) and how do they differ from canary deployments?
A) Feature flags are Kubernetes annotations that enable/disable deployment features
B) Feature flags and canary deployments are the same thing — both control traffic percentage
C) Feature flags require a complete cluster restart to enable new features
D) Feature flags toggle functionality at the APPLICATION level (code path on/off) without redeploying — independent of which pod version is running. Canary deploys a new VERSION of the application and routes traffic to it. They're complementary: flag a feature OFF in the new version, deploy via canary, then toggle the flag ON
✅ Correct Answer: D

Feature flags decouple deployment from release. Deploy code with the feature flag OFF → verify deployment health → toggle feature ON. If issues arise, toggle OFF instantly (seconds) — no rollback needed. OpenFeature is the CNCF standard, with LaunchDarkly, Flagsmith, and Unleash as providers.

Feature Flags vs Canary
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Canary: "10% of USERS get the new VERSION"                             │
  │  Feature Flag: "10% of USERS get the new FEATURE (any version)"        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Combined approach:                                                     │
  │  1. Deploy v2 with "new-search" flag OFF via canary (5% traffic)      │
  │  2. Canary metrics look good → promote to 100%                         │
  │  3. Toggle "new-search" flag ON for 1% of users → check metrics        │
  │  4. Gradually increase flag rollout → 10% → 50% → 100%                │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Flag OFF = instant rollback (milliseconds, not pod restarts)          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Feature flags are application-level, not K8s annotations. Why not B? They operate at different levels. Why not C? Flags toggle at runtime — no restart needed.

2.5 Container Registries & Image ManagementQ63-Q66

GitOps & CD Q63 of 200
What is the difference between a public and private container registry, and how does Kubernetes authenticate to private registries?
A) Public registries store open-source images; private registries store proprietary images — Kubernetes automatically detects which to use
B) There is no difference — all registries are accessible to Kubernetes by default
C) Public registries (Docker Hub, GHCR public) allow anonymous pulls. Private registries (ECR, ACR, Harbor) require authentication. Kubernetes uses imagePullSecrets (a Secret containing registry credentials) referenced in the pod spec or ServiceAccount to authenticate
D) Public registries are free; private registries charge per image pull
✅ Correct Answer: C

Private registries require authentication. Create a docker-registry Secret: `kubectl create secret docker-registry ecr-creds --docker-server=aws_account_id.dkr.ecr.region.amazonaws.com --docker-username=AWS --docker-password=$(aws ecr get-login-password)`. Then reference it as `imagePullSecrets` in the pod spec — or better, patch the ServiceAccount so all pods in the namespace auto-use it.

imagePullSecrets
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl create secret docker-registry ecr-creds \                       │
  │    --docker-server=123.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com \               │
  │    --docker-username=AWS \                                              │
  │    --docker-password=$(aws ecr get-login-password)                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Pod spec:                                                              │
  │    imagePullSecrets:                                                    │
  │    - name: ecr-creds                    ← Per-pod                       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  OR: kubectl patch sa default -p '{"imagePullSecrets":[{"name":"ecr"}]}'│
  │      → All pods in namespace auto-use the secret ✅                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? K8s doesn't automatically authenticate. Why not B? Private registries definitely require auth. Why not D? Cost is not the defining difference.

GitOps & CD Q64 of 200
What does Trivy do, and where should it run in the CI/CD pipeline?
A) Trivy monitors running containers for runtime security threats and anomalies
B) Trivy scans container images, filesystems, and Git repositories for vulnerabilities (CVEs), misconfigurations, and secrets. It should run AFTER image build and BEFORE push to registry — blocking the push if CRITICAL CVEs are found
C) Trivy is a Kubernetes admission controller that blocks pods with vulnerable images from starting
D) Trivy generates SBOMs but cannot detect vulnerabilities — it only catalogs dependencies
✅ Correct Answer: B

Trivy (by Aqua Security) is the CNCF-standard image scanner. Run it in CI: `trivy image --severity CRITICAL --exit-code 1 anihpj-api:latest`. If CRITICAL CVEs found → CI fails → image never reaches registry. Also scan IaC files (K8s manifests, Terraform) for misconfigurations and Git repos for hardcoded secrets.

Trivy in CI/CD
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CI Pipeline:                                                           │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌─────────────┐ │
  │  │ Build    │ →  │ Trivy Scan│ →  │ Pass? Push   │ →  │ Deploy      │ │
  │  │ Image    │    │ CVE check │    │ to ECR       │    │ via Argo CD │ │
  │  └──────────┘    └───────────┘    └──────────────┘    └─────────────┘ │
  │                       │                                                 │
  │                       ├── CRITICAL CVEs found → CI FAILS ❌             │
  │                       └── No CRITICAL CVEs → Continue ✅               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's Falco. Why not C? That's an admission webhook (can use Trivy results). Why not D? Trivy generates SBOMs AND detects CVEs.

GitOps & CD Q65 of 200
What is Cosign and how does it secure the software supply chain?
A) Cosign signs container images and generates attestations using Sigstore — creating a cryptographic chain of custody. You can verify before deployment that an image was signed by your CI pipeline and hasn't been tampered with since build time
B) Cosign encrypts container images at rest in the registry to prevent unauthorized access
C) Cosign is a Kubernetes operator that automatically signs all running pods for compliance auditing
D) Cosign replaces Docker Content Trust — it's the same functionality with a different name
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cosign + Sigstore = free, keyless image signing. In CI: `cosign sign --keyless anihpj-api@sha256:abc...` signs the image using OIDC (your GitHub identity). At deploy time: `cosign verify` checks the signature. If the image was modified after signing → verification fails → deployment blocked. This prevents supply chain attacks like the SolarWinds incident.

Cosign Signing & Verification
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BUILD TIME (CI):                      DEPLOY TIME (Admission):         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ cosign sign \         │            │ cosign verify \               │  │
  │  │   --keyless \         │            │   anihpj-api@sha256:abc      │  │
  │  │   anihpj-api@sha256:abc│           │                               │  │
  │  │                        │            │ ✅ Signature valid            │  │
  │  │ Signature stored in    │            │    → Allow deployment        │  │
  │  │ OCI registry alongside │            │                               │  │
  │  │ the image              │            │ ❌ Signature INVALID          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────┘            │    → BLOCK deployment!        │  │
  │                                       └──────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? Cosign signs, doesn't encrypt. Why not C? Cosign signs images, not pods at runtime. Why not D? Cosign is keyless (Sigstore); Docker Content Trust uses Notary with keys.

GitOps & CD Q66 of 200
What is a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) and which tool generates it?
A) An SBOM is a Kubernetes manifest listing all pods running in a namespace
B) An SBOM is a security audit report required by AWS before deploying to EKS
C) An SBOM is a machine-readable inventory of ALL components, libraries, and dependencies in a software artifact — like a nutrition label for your container image. Tools like Syft and Trivy generate SBOMs in SPDX or CycloneDX format so you can check if your image contains vulnerable Log4j versions during a zero-day incident
D) An SBOM is a Kubernetes operator that tracks image versions across all namespaces
✅ Correct Answer: C

SBOM = "What's inside this image?" When Log4Shell hit, teams with SBOMs could instantly search "does any image contain log4j?" and patch in hours. Teams without SBOMs spent weeks manually auditing. `syft anihpj-api:v2.1.0 -o spdx-json` generates a complete dependency tree. Store SBOMs alongside images in the OCI registry.

SBOM — Software Bill of Materials
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ syft anihpj-api:v2.1.0 -o table                                      │
  │  NAME                VERSION         TYPE                               │
  │  Django              4.2.11          python                             │
  │  djangorestframework 3.14.0          python                             │
  │  psycopg2            2.9.9           python                             │
  │  gunicorn            21.2.0          python                             │
  │  openssl             3.0.13          deb                                │
  │  libc6               2.36-9+deb12u4  deb                                │
  │  ...                                                                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Zero-day: "Is log4j 2.14 affected?" → grep SBOM → No log4j → SAFE ✅  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's kubectl get pods. Why not B? SBOMs are industry-standard, not AWS-specific. Why not D? That's a different concept entirely.

🔌 Category 3: Platform APIs & Self-Service (25%)Q67-Q80

🟣 Advanced: Building the Developer Self-Service Layer

This category separates a K8s admin from a Platform Engineer. Build CRDs, Operators, and self-service portals so developers provision everything WITHOUT tickets.

Q67-Q116 50 Questions 🟣 Advanced

3.1 CRDs & the Kubernetes Extension APIQ67-Q80

Platform APIs Q67 of 200
What is a Custom Resource Definition (CRD) in Kubernetes?
A) A CRD is a YAML template for creating Kubernetes Deployments with custom parameters
B) A CRD extends the Kubernetes API with your own resource types — after creating a CRD, you can use kubectl to manage your custom resources (DatabaseClaim, TLSSecret, EnvironmentRequest) just like native resources (Pod, Service, Deployment)
C) A CRD is a Kubernetes security policy that defines which custom images are allowed to run
D) A CRD replaces Ingress resources with a custom routing mechanism for external traffic
✅ Correct Answer: B

CRDs are how platform teams encode their domain knowledge into Kubernetes. Need developers to self-serve databases? Create a `DatabaseClaim` CRD. They run `kubectl apply -f my-db-claim.yaml` and your operator provisions a PostgreSQL instance. CRDs make the platform extensible — you teach Kubernetes new tricks.

CRD — Extending Kubernetes API
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Native K8s Resources              Custom Resources (via CRDs)          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Pod, Service,        │         │ DatabaseClaim (provision DB) │    │
  │  │ Deployment, Ingress, │    +    │ EnvironmentRequest (create   │    │
  │  │ ConfigMap, Secret,   │         │   namespace + quotas + RBAC) │    │
  │  │ PVC, NetworkPolicy   │         │ TLSCertificate (provision    │    │
  │  └──────────────────────┘         │   cert-manager Certificate)  │    │
  │                                    │ PipelineRun (Tekton)         │    │
  │  kubectl get pods ✅              └──────────────────────────────┘    │
  │  kubectl get databaseclaims ✅    ← CRD enables this!                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? CRDs define resource types, not templates. Why not C? That's an admission controller. Why not D? CRDs are general-purpose, not ingress-specific.

Platform APIs Q68 of 200
What does OpenAPI v3 schema validation in a CRD provide?
A) It validates custom resources at creation/update time — enforcing required fields, data types, enums, and patterns. If a developer submits a DatabaseClaim without specifying engine: "postgresql", the API server rejects it immediately with a clear error message
B) It automatically generates a web UI for editing custom resources based on the schema
C) It encrypts sensitive fields in the custom resource before storing them in etcd
D) It provides OpenAPI documentation that replaces the need for kubectl explain
✅ Correct Answer: A

CRD schema validation = API-level guardrails. You define what fields are required, their types (string, integer, boolean), allowed values (enums), regex patterns, and min/max constraints. The API server enforces these — invalid resources are rejected before they reach any controller. This makes your platform API self-documenting and safe.

CRD Schema Validation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CRD Schema (OpenAPI v3):                                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  openAPIV3Schema:                                                 │  │
  │  │    type: object                                                   │  │
  │  │    required: ["engine", "size"]        ← Must have these fields   │  │
  │  │    properties:                                                    │  │
  │  │      engine:                                                      │  │
  │  │        type: string                                               │  │
  │  │        enum: ["postgresql", "mysql", "redis"] ← Only these values│  │
  │  │      size:                                                        │  │
  │  │        type: string                                               │  │
  │  │        pattern: '^(small|medium|large)$'    ← Regex validation    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  kubectl apply -f db.yaml → engine: "mongodb" → REJECTED! ❌           │
  │  "engine: Unsupported value. Must be one of: postgresql, mysql, redis" │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? Schema validation is structural, not UI generation. Why not C? Encryption is separate (etcd encryption at rest). Why not D? kubectl explain works with or without CRDs.

Platform APIs Q69 of 200
What are additionalPrinterColumns in a CRD, and why are they important for platform developer experience?
A) They add extra security columns to etcd for encrypting custom resource data
B) They create additional database indexes for faster custom resource queries
C) They allow printing custom resources on physical printers for compliance documentation
D) They customize `kubectl get` output for your CRD — showing relevant fields (STATUS, ENGINE, SIZE, AGE) instead of just NAME and AGE. This makes custom resources feel like native Kubernetes resources and dramatically improves developer usability
✅ Correct Answer: D

Without additionalPrinterColumns, `kubectl get databaseclaims` shows only NAME and AGE — useless. With columns defined, it shows NAME, STATUS, ENGINE, SIZE, AGE — developers instantly see what's happening. Small detail, huge DevEx impact.

additionalPrinterColumns
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Without columns:                                                       │
  │  $ kubectl get databaseclaims                                           │
  │  NAME          AGE                                                      │
  │  my-db         5m                                                       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  With additionalPrinterColumns:                                         │
  │  $ kubectl get databaseclaims                                           │
  │  NAME     STATUS    ENGINE       SIZE    AGE                           │
  │  my-db    Ready     postgresql   medium  5m                            │
  │  cache    Pending   redis        small   2m                            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  DevEx win: Developers instantly see status without kubectl describe    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A/B/C? None of these are what additionalPrinterColumns do.

Platform APIs Q70 of 200
What is a Kubernetes Operator and how does it extend beyond a simple CRD?
A) An Operator is a human SRE who manually manages Kubernetes clusters 24/7
B) An Operator is a custom controller that watches CRDs and automates operational tasks — it encodes human operator knowledge into software. CRD defines the API (what), Operator implements the logic (how): provisioning, scaling, backup, upgrade, and self-healing
C) An Operator is a Kubernetes security plugin that restricts which images can be pulled
D) An Operator is a replacement for kubectl that provides a graphical interface for cluster management
✅ Correct Answer: B

CRD = API surface. Operator = brain behind the API. When you create a DatabaseClaim CR, the Operator controller sees it → provisions an RDS instance → creates a Service → updates the CR status to "Ready." The Operator watches → analyzes → acts in a continuous reconciliation loop. It's the platform engineer's superpower for encoding operational knowledge.

CRD + Operator = Self-Service
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Developer: kubectl apply -f db-claim.yaml                              │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  CRD "DatabaseClaim" stored in etcd                                     │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  Operator watches for new DatabaseClaims                                 │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── ① Validate the claim (size, engine, region)                   │
  │       ├── ② Call AWS API: create RDS instance                           │
  │       ├── ③ Create K8s Secret with connection credentials               │
  │       ├── ④ Create K8s Service pointing to RDS endpoint                 │
  │       └── ⑤ Update CR status: Ready ✅                                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Developer: "I asked for a DB, and it appeared. No ticket needed."      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's a human — Operators automate what humans used to do. Why not C? That's an admission controller. Why not D? Operators are controllers, not CLI replacements.

What is Backstage (by Spotify) and what role does it play in platform engineering?

B) Backstage is a Kubernetes dashboard replacement that shows pod logs and metrics
C) Backstage is a CI/CD tool that replaces Jenkins and GitHub Actions
D) Backstage is a container registry for storing and scanning Docker images
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage is the "shop window" of your platform. Developers visit one URL, see all services, their ownership, health, docs, and can scaffold new services from golden path templates. It transforms the platform from "a bunch of CLI tools" to "a product with a UI."

Backstage — Developer Portal

Platform APIs Q72 of 200
What are Golden Path Templates in platform engineering?
A) Golden path templates are YAML files that define the maximum resource limits for production deployments
B) Golden path templates are Kubernetes RBAC policies that grant admin access to senior developers
C) Golden path templates are pre-built, opinionated service scaffolds that include CI/CD pipelines, Dockerfiles, K8s manifests, monitoring dashboards, and security policies — making the "right way" the EASIEST way. Developers scaffold a production-ready service in minutes instead of days
D) Golden path templates are Helm charts that can only be installed by platform administrators
✅ Correct Answer: C

Golden paths = paved roads. Instead of developers piecing together CI/CD, K8s config, monitoring, and security from scratch (and getting it wrong), the platform team provides a template that does it ALL correctly. "Make the right thing the easiest thing" — developers get production-ready services AND the platform team ensures compliance.

Golden Path Template — What's Inside
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Scaffold "Django Service" from Backstage:                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │

  │  Time to production-ready service: 5 minutes (vs. 3 days from scratch) │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's ResourceQuota. Why not B? Golden paths are about scaffolding, not RBAC. Why not D? Golden paths are for developers to self-serve, not admin-only.

Platform APIs Q73 of 200
What is Crossplane and how does it differ from Terraform?
A) Crossplane is a UI for Terraform — it generates HCL code through a graphical interface
B) Crossplane manages cloud infrastructure using Kubernetes CRDs and the reconciliation loop. You define an RDS instance as a YAML CR, Crossplane's provider provisions it, and continuously reconciles drift. Terraform is CLI-driven with state files; Crossplane is K8s-native with no separate state
C) Crossplane replaces Kubernetes — it's a standalone infrastructure orchestrator
D) Crossplane only works with AWS; Terraform works with all cloud providers
✅ Correct Answer: B

Crossplane = infrastructure-as-data (K8s CRDs). You define an RDS instance the same way you define a Deployment — as YAML. Crossplane's provider (AWS, GCP, Azure) provisions the actual resource and continuously reconciles. No `terraform state`, no `terraform apply` — just `kubectl apply`. Infrastructure managed via GitOps alongside applications.

Crossplane vs Terraform
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Terraform:                         Crossplane:                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ terraform.tfstate    │          │ CR in etcd = desired state    │    │
  │  │ terraform apply      │          │ kubectl apply -f rds.yaml    │    │
  │  │ terraform destroy    │          │ kubectl delete rds my-db     │    │
  │  │ Separate CLI tool    │          │ Built into K8s API            │    │
  │  │ State file to manage │          │ No state file — etcd IS state│    │
  │  └──────────────────────┘          └──────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Crossplane CRD:                                                        │
  │  apiVersion: database.aws.upbound.io/v1beta1                           │
  │  kind: RDSInstance                                                      │
  │  spec:                                                                  │
  │    engine: postgres, engineVersion: "15.4"                             │
  │    instanceClass: db.t3.medium, allocatedStorage: 100                  │
  │  → Crossplane provisions actual RDS PostgreSQL in AWS ✅               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Crossplane is K8s-native, not a Terraform UI. Why not C? Crossplane runs ON K8s. Why not D? Crossplane has providers for AWS, GCP, Azure, and more.

Platform APIs Q74 of 200
What is the Operator Lifecycle Manager (OLM) and why use it?
A) OLM manages the full lifecycle of Operators — installation, upgrades, dependency resolution, and RBAC. It's like a package manager (apt/brew) for Kubernetes Operators, ensuring operators are installed correctly with all dependencies and kept up to date
B) OLM monitors the health of running Operators and restarts them if they crash
C) OLM is a Kubernetes admission controller that blocks unapproved Operators from being installed
D) OLM replaces kubectl for all Operator-related operations
✅ Correct Answer: A

OLM solves "how do I install, upgrade, and manage 20 Operators safely?" It handles CRD lifecycle (v1alpha1→v1), dependency chains (Operator A needs Operator B), upgrade paths, and channel-based subscription (stable vs. fast channel). Without OLM, you manually manage Operator YAMLs and hope nothing breaks during upgrades.

OLM — Operator Package Manager
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl operator install prometheus --channel stable                 │
  │                                                                         │
  │  OLM:                                                                   │
  │  ① Checks dependencies (Prometheus needs PrometheusOperator CRD)        │
  │  ② Installs CRDs                                                       │
  │  ③ Installs Operator deployment + RBAC                                  │
  │  ④ Creates Subscription for automatic updates                          │
  │  ⑤ Monitors for new versions in the stable channel                     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Upgrade: OLM handles CRD migration (v1alpha1 → v1), no manual steps  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's K8s' built-in liveness probes. Why not C? That's an admission webhook you'd configure separately. Why not D? OLM + kubectl work together.

Platform APIs Q75 of 200
What is cert-manager and how does it enable self-service TLS in a platform?
A) cert-manager is a Kubernetes built-in component that generates self-signed certificates for all Services
B) cert-manager is a CLI tool that developers run locally to generate TLS certificates for their applications
C) cert-manager replaces the need for HTTPS — it encrypts traffic at the kernel level without certificates
D) cert-manager is a Kubernetes Operator that automates TLS certificate issuance and renewal. Developers create a Certificate CRD → cert-manager obtains certificates from Let's Encrypt (or HashiCorp Vault) → auto-renews before expiry. Self-service TLS without knowing anything about ACME or DNS challenges
✅ Correct Answer: D

cert-manager embodies platform self-service: developers create a Certificate resource (kubectl apply -f my-cert.yaml), cert-manager handles DNS-01 or HTTP-01 challenge, obtains certificate from Let's Encrypt, stores it as a K8s Secret, and auto-renews 30 days before expiry. The developer never touches certbot, DNS records, or ACME clients.

cert-manager — Self-Service TLS
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Developer creates:                   cert-manager handles:             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kind: Certificate        │        │ ① Requests cert from Let's    │  │
  │  │ spec:                    │        │    Encrypt (ACME protocol)    │  │
  │  │   secretName: anihpj-tls│        │ ② Completes DNS-01 challenge  │  │
  │  │   dnsNames:              │        │ ③ Stores cert in K8s Secret   │  │
  │  │   - anihpj.example.com  │        │ ④ Auto-renews before expiry   │  │
  │  │   issuerRef:             │        │ ⑤ Updates Secret seamlessly   │  │
  │  │     name: letsencrypt   │        └──────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? cert-manager is an add-on, not built-in. Why not B? It's a cluster-level Operator, not a local CLI. Why not C? cert-manager issues real TLS certificates — it doesn't bypass HTTPS.

What is a Backstage Software Template and how does it scaffold new services?
A) It's a YAML file that Backstage uses to create Kubernetes Deployments directly
B) It's a pre-built Docker image that developers can deploy without modification
C) It's a declarative template that defines what to scaffold: a GitHub repo from a skeleton, CI/CD pipeline, K8s manifests, and Backstage catalog entry. Developers fill in a form (service name, team, language) → Backstage creates everything via the scaffolder

Platform APIs Q77 of 200
What is Dynamic Environment Provisioning (Ephemeral/Preview Environments) in a platform?
A) Creating permanent development environments for each developer on their local machine
B) Automatically creating a full, isolated environment (namespace + DB + app) for every pull request, running integration tests against it, and auto-deleting it when the PR is merged or closed — giving developers production-like testing without permanent infrastructure costs
C) Migrating applications between cloud providers dynamically based on cost and performance
D) Scaling production environments up and down based on time of day to save costs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Preview/Ephemeral environments = "every PR gets its own mini-production." PR #42 opens → platform creates `pr-42` namespace, deploys the app + DB, runs tests → test results posted to PR → PR merged → environment auto-destroyed. Developers test in production-like conditions without a shared "dev" environment that's always broken.

Ephemeral Environments per PR
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PR #42 opened →                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Namespace: pr-42-anihpj                                          │  │
  │  │  ├── anihpj-api (PR version)                                      │  │
  │  │  ├── anihpj-db (ephemeral PostgreSQL)                             │  │
  │  │  └── Ingress: pr-42.anihpj-dev.example.com                        │  │
  │  │  → Integration tests run → Results posted to PR                   │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  PR merged → Namespace auto-deleted → Resources freed ✅               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's local dev, not platform provisioning. Why not C? That's cloud migration. Why not D? That's schedule-based autoscaling.

Platform APIs Q78 of 200
What does External Secrets Operator (ESO) do, and why use it instead of Kubernetes Secrets directly?
A) ESO syncs secrets from external secret managers (AWS Secrets Manager, GCP Secret Manager, Azure Key Vault, HashiCorp Vault) into Kubernetes Secrets automatically. The source of truth stays in the external manager — ESO creates and refreshes K8s Secrets on a schedule. No plain-text secrets in Git, ever
B) ESO encrypts Kubernetes Secrets using an external Hardware Security Module (HSM)
C) ESO replaces Kubernetes Secrets entirely — applications read directly from the external manager without K8s Secrets
D) ESO is a Kubernetes admission controller that blocks pods from reading Secrets they shouldn't access
✅ Correct Answer: A

ESO bridges the gap between cloud secret managers and K8s. Store secrets in AWS Secrets Manager → ESO syncs to K8s Secret → your pod mounts the Secret as usual. If the secret rotates in AWS, ESO refreshes the K8s Secret automatically. Git never sees secrets; the source of truth is the cloud provider.

External Secrets Operator
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AWS Secrets Manager          ESO              Kubernetes               │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────┐         ┌──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ /prod/anihpj/db  │──────▶│ Sync │────────▶│ Secret: anihpj-db    │  │
  │  │ password: s3cr3t  │       │ every│         │ POSTGRES_PASSWORD:   │  │
  │  └──────────────────┘       │ 1h   │         │ s3cr3t               │  │
  │                              └──────┘         └──────────┬───────────┘  │
  │                                                           │              │
  │                                                           ▼              │
  │                                                    Pod: envFrom secret  │
  │                                                    Git: NEVER sees it   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? ESO syncs secrets, doesn't encrypt them. Why not C? ESO creates K8s Secrets — apps still use them normally. Why not D? That's RBAC, not ESO.

Platform APIs Q79 of 200
What are DORA Metrics and why are they critical for measuring platform engineering success?
A) DORA metrics measure Kubernetes cluster resource utilization (CPU, memory, disk, network)
B) DORA is a security compliance framework for containerized applications
C) DORA metrics track individual developer productivity (lines of code, commits per day)
D) DORA metrics measure software delivery performance: Deployment Frequency, Lead Time for Changes, Mean Time to Recovery (MTTR), and Change Failure Rate (CFR). They're the industry standard for measuring whether your platform actually makes teams faster and safer
✅ Correct Answer: D

DORA (DevOps Research and Assessment) defines 4 key metrics. Platform engineering should IMPROVE these numbers: before the platform, deploy frequency was monthly and MTTR was days. After: daily deploys and MTTR in minutes. If your platform doesn't improve DORA metrics, you're building the wrong thing.

DORA Metrics — Elite Performance
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Metric                    Elite           Low           Target         │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Deployment Frequency      On-demand       Monthly        Daily ✅      │
  │  Lead Time for Changes     < 1 hour        Months         Hours ✅      │
  │  MTTR (Recovery)           < 1 hour        Weeks          Minutes ✅    │
  │  Change Failure Rate       0-15%           45-60%         < 15% ✅      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's resource monitoring. Why not B? That's CIS/SOC2. Why not C? DORA measures delivery outcomes, not individual activity.

Platform APIs Q80 of 200
What is Developer Experience (DevEx) and how should a platform team measure it?
A) DevEx is measured by counting the number of Kubernetes clusters managed per platform engineer
B) DevEx measures how frictionless it is for developers to ship software — key metrics include Time-to-10th-PR (how fast a new dev becomes productive), cognitive load surveys, ticket deflection rate (self-service usage vs. tickets filed), and developer NPS scores. A great platform makes developers forget it exists
C) DevEx is solely about UI/UX design of the developer portal — making Backstage look pretty
D) DevEx is measured by how many features the platform team ships per quarter
✅ Correct Answer: B

DevEx = developer happiness + productivity. A platform that reduces cognitive load (developers don't need to understand K8s internals), accelerates onboarding (golden paths), and eliminates friction (self-service, no tickets) has great DevEx. Measure it: how fast can a new hire ship to production? How many support tickets does the platform deflect?

DevEx — Developer Experience Metrics
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Metric                         Good               Bad                  │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Time to 10th PR                < 1 week           > 1 month            │
  │  Ticket deflection rate         > 80% self-serve   Most via tickets     │
  │  Developer NPS                  > 50               < 0                  │
  │  Time to provision DB           < 5 min (auto)     3 days (ticket)      │
  │  "I can deploy without help"    90% agree          30% agree            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Cluster count is an infra metric, not DevEx. Why not C? DevEx spans UI, CLI, docs, APIs, and self-service — not just the portal UI. Why not D? Platform team output ≠ developer experience.

📊 Category 4: Observability & Operations (20%)Q81-Q170

🟣 Advanced: The Platform Engineer's Superpower — See Everything

Observability answers: What happened? What's happening? What WILL happen? Metrics, logs, traces, and alerts — the platform engineer's diagnostic toolkit.

Q81-Q100 20 Questions 🟣 Advanced

4.1 Metrics & MonitoringQ81-Q100

Observability Q81 of 200
What are the Three Pillars of Observability and what does each provide?
A) CPU, Memory, and Disk — the three fundamental resources to monitor
B) Development, Staging, and Production — the three environments to observe
C) Pods, Services, and Ingresses — the three Kubernetes resources to track
D) Metrics (numerical data over time — CPU%, request rate), Logs (timestamped event records — "user login failed"), and Traces (end-to-end request flow across services — "API call took 2.3s, spent 1.8s in DB query"). Together they provide a complete picture of system behavior
✅ Correct Answer: D

Metrics tell you WHAT (error rate spiked to 5%). Logs tell you WHY (NullPointerException at line 42). Traces tell you WHERE (the bottleneck is in the PostgreSQL query, not the API code). Modern platforms often add a fourth pillar: Profiles (continuous profiling of CPU/memory per function).

Three Pillars + Profiles
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  📊 METRICS:  "CPU at 85%, request rate 500/s, p99 latency 200ms"     │
  │     Tools: Prometheus, Grafana, VictoriaMetrics                         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  📝 LOGS:     "[ERROR] 2026-06-14T10:30:00Z Connection refused: anihpj-db:5432"│
  │     Tools: Loki, Elasticsearch, Fluentd/Fluent Bit                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  🔍 TRACES:   "POST /api/jobs → 2.3s total (auth:50ms, DB:1800ms, app:450ms)"│
  │     Tools: Jaeger, Tempo, OpenTelemetry                                 │
  │                                                                         │
  │  📈 PROFILES: "django.db.models.sql.query took 45% of CPU time"        │
  │     Tools: Parca, Pyroscope, Grafana Phlare                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A/B/C? Those are resources, environments, and K8s objects — not observability pillars.

Observability Q82 of 200
What is Prometheus and how does its pull-based architecture differ from push-based monitoring?
A) Prometheus pushes metrics to a central server — applications must actively send their data
B) Prometheus is a log aggregation system — it collects and indexes log files from containers
C) Prometheus scrapes (pulls) metrics from targets at configured intervals — it discovers targets via ServiceMonitors/PodMonitors, pulls /metrics endpoints, and stores time-series data locally. This pull model means Prometheus knows if a target is down (scrape fails) without the target needing to know about Prometheus
D) Prometheus replaces Grafana — it includes built-in dashboards for all Kubernetes metrics
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus = pull model. Every 15-60 seconds, Prometheus scrapes `/metrics` from each target. If a scrape fails, Prometheus knows the target is down (no heartbeat mechanism needed). ServiceMonitors auto-discover new pods via labels. This is the cloud native monitoring standard — Prometheus graduated from CNCF in 2018.

Prometheus — Pull-Based Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Prometheus Server                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Service Discovery (K8s API) → finds pods with labels            │  │
  │  │       │                                                            │  │
  │  │       ▼                                                            │  │
  │  │  Scrapes /metrics every 30s                                       │  │
  │  │       │                                                            │  │
  │  │       ├── anihpj-api pod → GET :8000/metrics → 200 OK ✅         │  │
  │  │       ├── anihpj-db pod  → GET :9187/metrics → 200 OK ✅         │  │
  │  │       └── anihpj-api-3   → GET :8000/metrics → TIMEOUT ❌        │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  Time-series DB (TSDB) → stores metrics + timestamps             │  │
  │  │  PromQL: rate(http_requests_total[5m]) → query language          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Prometheus pulls, not pushes. Why not B? That's Loki/Elasticsearch. Why not D? Prometheus + Grafana are complementary — Prometheus stores, Grafana visualizes.

Observability Q83 of 200
What is OpenTelemetry (OTel) and why is it the CNCF standard for observability?
A) OTel is a vendor-neutral, CNCF-incubating standard for generating, collecting, and exporting telemetry data (traces, metrics, logs). Instead of instrumenting code with Jaeger SDK OR Prometheus client OR Zipkin — you instrument ONCE with OTel and export to ANY backend (Jaeger, Tempo, Datadog, Honeycomb) by changing configuration, not code
B) OTel is a monitoring dashboard tool that replaces Grafana with a more modern UI
C) OTel is a proprietary observability platform by AWS that only works with EKS clusters
D) OTel is a Kubernetes admission controller that injects monitoring sidecars into pods
✅ Correct Answer: A

OTel eliminates vendor lock-in for observability. Instrument your Django app with `opentelemetry-instrument` → export to Jaeger today, switch to Grafana Tempo tomorrow by changing the exporter config. No code changes. OTel is the USB-C of observability — one standard, any backend.

OpenTelemetry — Instrument Once, Export Anywhere
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Application (anihpj-api)                                               │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       │ OpenTelemetry SDK (auto-instrumentation)                        │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  OTel Collector (runs as sidecar/daemonset/deployment)                  │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── Traces → Jaeger (today) / Tempo (tomorrow)                   │
  │       ├── Metrics → Prometheus                                          │
  │       └── Logs → Loki / Elasticsearch                                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Switch backends by changing ONE config line — zero code changes       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? OTel collects and exports — doesn't visualize (use Grafana for that). Why not C? OTel is vendor-neutral, CNCF-governed. Why not D? OTel is an SDK + collector, not an admission controller.

Observability Q84 of 200
What is Synthetic Monitoring (Blackbox monitoring) and why complement it with application monitoring?
A) Synthetic monitoring replaces Prometheus — it generates all metrics from simulated traffic
B) Synthetic monitoring tests your system from the OUTSIDE (like a user) — probing endpoints, checking TLS expiry, verifying DNS resolution — even when no real users are active. It catches "the monitoring system is down because the cluster is down" scenarios that internal monitoring misses
C) Synthetic monitoring generates fake users to inflate engagement metrics for investors
D) Synthetic monitoring is only used during load testing — not for continuous operations
✅ Correct Answer: B

Synthetic/Blackbox monitoring asks: "Can a user reach my app?" It probes from OUTSIDE the cluster. If your entire cluster is down, internal Prometheus is also down and can't alert you. Blackbox Exporter running in a DIFFERENT cluster/region would detect anihpj.example.com is unreachable and alert you. Defense in depth for monitoring.

Synthetic vs Application Monitoring
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Blackbox Exporter (outside cluster):                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  probe_http_status_code{target="anihpj.example.com"} == 0 ❌     │  │
  │  │  → "Site is DOWN! Alert on-call!"                                │  │
  │  │  → Prometheus INSIDE the cluster is also down → can't alert!     │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Lesson: Always run blackbox monitoring from OUTSIDE your primary infra │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Synthetic monitoring complements, not replaces, Prometheus. Why not C? That's fraud. Why not D? Synthetic monitoring runs continuously, not just during load tests.

Observability Q85 of 200
What is Grafana Loki and how does its architecture differ from Elasticsearch for log aggregation?
A) Loki is a clone of Elasticsearch with the same architecture but a different name
B) Loki indexes the full text of every log line for faster searching — just like Elasticsearch
C) Loki indexes only metadata (labels like namespace, pod, app) and stores log content as compressed chunks in object storage (S3). It uses LogQL (PromQL-like query language) and is designed to be cheap, simple, and integrated with Grafana — unlike Elasticsearch which indexes full text (expensive at scale)
D) Loki replaces Fluentd/Fluent Bit — it collects logs directly from containers without a log agent
✅ Correct Answer: C

Loki = "Prometheus for logs." It indexes labels (not full text), making it dramatically cheaper than Elasticsearch at scale. Logs stored in S3/GCS — practically infinite retention at minimal cost. Query with LogQL: `{namespace="anihpj", app="api"} |= "ERROR"` — label filters are fast, content search scans chunks. Perfect for K8s where metadata (namespace, pod, container) is rich.

Loki Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Fluent Bit → Loki Ingester → Index (labels only) + Chunks (S3)        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Loki indexes:   {namespace="anihpj-prod", app="api", pod="api-7d8f9"}│
  │  Loki stores:    actual log lines compressed in S3 (cheap!)            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  vs Elasticsearch:                                                      │
  │  ES indexes:      EVERY word in EVERY log line (expensive at TB scale) │
  │  ES stores:       indexed data on local SSDs/EBS (costly)              │
  │                                                                         │
  │  For 1TB/day of logs: Elasticsearch ~$5K/month, Loki ~$500/month       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Loki is architecturally different — label-indexed, not full-text. Why not B? Loki does NOT full-text index. Why not D? Loki still needs a log agent (Fluent Bit/Promtail).

Observability Q86 of 200
What are SLOs, SLIs, and SLAs and how do they relate?
A) SLI (Service Level Indicator) = what you measure (99.9% of requests succeed). SLO (Service Level Objective) = the target (99.9% success rate over 30 days). SLA (Service Level Agreement) = the contract with consequences if SLO is violated (refund, penalty). SLI → SLO → SLA — indicator, objective, agreement
B) SLO is server load optimization; SLI is service load indicator; SLA is server load agreement — they're about CPU/memory, not reliability
C) All three terms mean the same thing — they're interchangeable names for uptime guarantees
D) SLOs are set by developers; SLIs are set by managers; SLAs are set by lawyers — there's no mathematical relationship between them
✅ Correct Answer: A

SLI = raw measurement. SLO = internal target (drives alerting, error budgets). SLA = external promise (legal/financial consequences). For anihpj/jobpost: SLI = 99.95% of API requests return 2xx in 30 days. SLO = 99.9% (leaves a 0.1% error budget for risky deploys). SLA with customers = 99.5% (lower than SLO to give buffer).

SLI → SLO → SLA
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLI (measured):    99.95% success rate — just the data               │
  │  SLO (internal):    "API must be 99.9% available over 30 days"        │
  │  SLA (external):    "99.5% uptime guaranteed or 10% credit"           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Error Budget = 100% - SLO = 0.1% = 43 minutes of allowed downtime    │
  │  per month. Use it for risky deploys, experiments, chaos engineering.  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B/C/D? These are specific, related reliability concepts — not interchangeable or about server load.

Observability Q87 of 200
What is an Error Budget and how does it influence deployment decisions?
A) An error budget is the maximum dollar amount a team can spend on cloud resources before needing approval
B) Error Budget = 100% minus SLO — the acceptable amount of unreliability. If the error budget is exhausted (too many incidents this month), deployments FREEZE until reliability recovers. If there's budget remaining, teams are encouraged to deploy faster and take risks
C) An error budget is a DevOps team's allocation for fixing bugs in the next sprint
D) Error budgets are only used by Google — they're not applicable to smaller organizations
✅ Correct Answer: B

Error budgets create a healthy tension: SREs want reliability, developers want velocity. If reliability is fine (error budget > 0), deploy faster! If reliability is suffering (error budget ≤ 0), freeze deploys and invest in stability. This replaces arbitrary "no deploying on Fridays" rules with data-driven decisions.

Error Budget Burn Rate
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLO: 99.9% uptime → Error Budget: 0.1% = 43 min/month                │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Week 1: 5 min downtime  → Budget: 38 min remaining → Deploy fast ✅   │
  │  Week 2: 0 min downtime  → Budget: 38 min → Deploy faster! ✅          │
  │  Week 3: 40 min downtime → Budget: -2 min → FREEZE DEPLOYS 🔴         │
  │  Week 4: (no deploys)    → Reliability work only → Budget recovers     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's a cloud budget, not an error budget. Why not C? Error budgets are measured in time/events, not story points. Why not D? Google popularized the concept — it applies universally.

Observability Q88 of 200
What is Thanos or Cortex and what problem do they solve for Prometheus at scale?
A) Thanos replaces Prometheus entirely — it's a different time-series database with better performance
B) Thanos is a Kubernetes operator that automatically scales Prometheus pods based on metric volume
C) Thanos/Cortex extend Prometheus with long-term storage (S3/GCS), global query view across multiple Prometheus instances, high availability via replication, and downsampling for historical data — solving Prometheus's limitations around retention, durability, and multi-cluster aggregation
D) Thanos is a Grafana plugin that adds pre-built dashboards for Prometheus metrics
✅ Correct Answer: C

Prometheus's local TSDB is limited: 15 days retention by default, single-instance, no global view. Thanos adds: S3 for infinite retention ($cheap$), Store Gateway for querying historical data, Querier for federating across clusters ("show me error rate across ALL 6 clusters"), and Compactor for downsampling (1-year-old data at 5-min resolution).

Thanos — Prometheus at Scale
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Cluster 1 Prometheus ──┐                                              │
  │  Cluster 2 Prometheus ──┼── Thanos Querier → Grafana (global view)     │
  │  Cluster 3 Prometheus ──┘                                              │
  │                              │                                          │
  │                              ▼                                          │
  │                         Thanos Sidecar → S3 (long-term, cheap storage) │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Query 2-year-old metrics without keeping 2 years of local SSD data     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Thanos extends Prometheus — doesn't replace it. Why not B? Thanos doesn't scale Prometheus pods horizontally. Why not D? Thanos is a backend — Grafana is the frontend.

Observability Q89 of 200
What is the purpose of Alertmanager in the Prometheus ecosystem?
A) Alertmanager is a Prometheus component that scrapes metrics from targets
B) Alertmanager replaces Prometheus alerting rules — you write alerts directly in Alertmanager
C) Alertmanager monitors the health of Prometheus itself and restarts it if it crashes
D) Alertmanager handles alert routing, deduplication, grouping, silencing, and inhibition — it receives alerts from Prometheus, groups related alerts (don't send 50 pages for one outage), routes to the right team (Slack, PagerDuty, email), and supports maintenance windows
✅ Correct Answer: D

Alertmanager solves "alert fatigue." Without it: 50 pods go down = 50 separate PagerDuty pages at 3 AM. With it: Alertmanager groups them into ONE alert "50 pods in anihpj namespace are down — probable node failure." Silencing lets you mute alerts during planned maintenance. Routing sends DB alerts to the DB team, API alerts to the API team.

Alertmanager — Alert Routing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Prometheus fires alert → Alertmanager receives it                      │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ├── Deduplicate (same alert from multiple Prometheus instances)   │
  │       ├── Group (50 pod-down alerts → 1 "Node failure" alert)          │
  │       ├── Inhibit (node-down suppresses all pod-down alerts)           │
  │       ├── Silence (maintenance window: mute all alerts for 2 hours)    │
  │       └── Route:                                                        │
  │           ├── severity=critical → PagerDuty (wake up on-call)          │
  │           ├── severity=warning → Slack #alerts channel                 │
  │           └── team=db → PagerDuty DB team                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's Prometheus server itself. Why not B? Alerting rules are in Prometheus; Alertmanager processes the fired alerts. Why not C? That's not Alertmanager's role.

Observability Q90 of 200
What is Grafana and why is it the standard visualization layer for cloud native platforms?
A) Grafana is a proprietary monitoring platform that replaces Prometheus, Loki, and Tempo
B) Grafana is an open-source visualization platform that connects to ANY data source (Prometheus, Loki, Tempo, Elasticsearch, CloudWatch, PostgreSQL) — providing unified dashboards, alerts, and exploration across all observability data. It's the "single pane of glass" for the platform
C) Grafana is a Kubernetes operator that automatically creates dashboards for every namespace
D) Grafana is a log aggregation tool that replaces Fluentd and Elasticsearch
✅ Correct Answer: B

Grafana = "single pane of glass." One dashboard showing Prometheus metrics (CPU, request rate), Loki logs (error messages), and Tempo traces (slow DB queries) — all correlated. Click a spike in the metrics panel, Grafana auto-links to the relevant logs and traces. This unified view is why Grafana has become the de facto standard for cloud native observability.

Grafana — Unified Observability
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Grafana Dashboard: anihpj/jobpost Overview                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  📊 Metrics (Prometheus)    📝 Logs (Loki)    🔍 Traces (Tempo)  │  │

  │  │  │ CPU: 45% ████░░░░  │   │ [ERROR] DB conn │  │ POST /api     │ │  │
  │  │  │ Req/s: 500 ██████  │   │ refused at 10:30│  │ 2.3s total    │ │  │
  │  │  │ p99: 200ms ███░░░  │   │ [WARN] Retry #3 │  │ 1.8s DB query │ │  │
  │  │  └────────────────────┘   └────────────────┘  └───────────────┘ │  │
  │  │                          ↑ Click spike → auto-navigate to logs   │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Grafana visualizes — it's the UI layer, not a replacement for data sources. Why not C? Grafana doesn't auto-create dashboards. Why not D? Grafana queries logs from Loki/ES — doesn't collect them.

Observability Q91 of 200
What does kubectl logs --previous show, and when is it essential?
A) It shows logs from the PREVIOUS (crashed) container instance — essential for debugging CrashLoopBackOff pods where the current container hasn't started yet. Without --previous, you see empty logs because the new container just started (and hasn't crashed yet)
B) It shows logs from the previous day — useful for comparing today's errors with yesterday's baseline
C) It shows logs from the previous Kubernetes version before a cluster upgrade
D) It shows logs from the previous replica of a deployment before a rolling update replaced it
✅ Correct Answer: A

CrashLoopBackOff = container starts, crashes, restarts, crashes... `kubectl logs anihpj-api-7d8f9` shows nothing (new container just started). `kubectl logs anihpj-api-7d8f9 --previous` shows the crash logs from the PREVIOUS instance — the error message that caused the crash. This is THE essential debugging command for CrashLoopBackOff.

kubectl logs --previous
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
