  │  Pod: anihpj-api-7d8f9 (CrashLoopBackOff)                              │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Container lifecycle:  Start → Crash → Restart → Start → Crash...      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  kubectl logs anihpj-api-7d8f9                                         │
  │  → (empty — current container just started, no output yet)             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  kubectl logs anihpj-api-7d8f9 --previous                              │
  │  → [ERROR] django.db.utils.OperationalError: could not connect to      │
  │    server: Connection refused. Is the server running?                  │
  │  → Found it! PostgreSQL isn't ready yet. Fix the startup order.        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? --previous refers to the previous container instance, not time-based. Why not C? Not related to Kubernetes versions. Why not D? Not related to deployment replicas.

Observability Q92 of 200
What is Fluent Bit and why use it over Fluentd for Kubernetes log collection?
A) Fluent Bit is the GUI version of Fluentd — it provides a web dashboard for log analysis
B) Fluent Bit and Fluentd are the same tool — Fluent Bit is just the newer name
C) Fluent Bit is a lightweight (~450KB memory), high-performance log processor written in C — designed as a DaemonSet on every node to collect container logs and forward to Loki/ES/S3. Fluentd is Ruby-based, more feature-rich but heavier (~40MB). Fluent Bit is preferred for K8s node-level log collection; Fluentd for complex aggregation pipelines
D) Fluent Bit replaces the need for stdout logging — it intercepts application logs at the kernel level
✅ Correct Answer: C

Fluent Bit runs as a DaemonSet (one per node), tailing `/var/log/containers/*.log`, parsing JSON log lines, enriching with K8s metadata (namespace, pod, labels), and forwarding to Loki, Elasticsearch, S3, or Kafka. At < 500KB memory per node, it's the standard K8s log agent. Fluentd is used for heavy aggregation tiers (centralized pipeline processing).

Fluent Bit — Node-Level Log Collection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Node 1                                   Node 2                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐               ┌──────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ Fluent Bit (DaemonSet)│               │ Fluent Bit (DaemonSet)   │   │
  │  │ ├ /var/log/containers │               │ ├ /var/log/containers    │   │
  │  │ ├ Parse JSON logs     │               │ ├ Parse JSON logs        │   │
  │  │ ├ Add K8s metadata    │               │ ├ Add K8s metadata       │   │
  │  │ └── Forward ──────────┼───────────────┼──▶ Loki / Elasticsearch  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┘               └──────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Fluent Bit is a CLI/daemon — not a GUI. Why not B? They're different tools from the same ecosystem. Why not D? Fluent Bit reads stdout logs — doesn't intercept at kernel level.

Security & Policy Q93 of 200
What is Pod Security Admission (PSA) and how does it replace PodSecurityPolicy (PSP)?
A) PSA is the same as PSP — just renamed in Kubernetes 1.25
B) PSA requires external policy engines like OPA or Kyverno to function
C) PSA only applies to pods in the kube-system namespace — it doesn't protect user workloads
D) PSA is a built-in admission controller (since K8s 1.25) that enforces Pod Security Standards at the namespace level via labels: privileged, baseline, or restricted. It replaces the complex, difficult-to-use PSP with a simple namespace-label model
✅ Correct Answer: D

PSA simplifies pod security: label a namespace `pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted` and all pods in that namespace must comply with the Restricted standard (no privileged containers, no hostPath, non-root user, read-only root filesystem). Three modes: enforce (block), audit (log only), warn (warn user). No complex policy YAML — just labels.

Pod Security Standards
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Namespace label: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Privileged:  Everything allowed (infra/admin pods)                     │
  │  Baseline:    No hostPath, no privileged, no hostNetwork (most workloads)│
  │  Restricted:  Baseline + non-root, readOnlyRootFilesystem, seccomp     │
  │               (highest security — recommended for production)           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  For anihpj/jobpost: prod namespace = restricted, dev = baseline        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? PSA is a completely different, simpler mechanism. Why not B? PSA is built-in — no external tools needed. Why not C? PSA applies to ALL namespaces.

Security & Policy Q94 of 200
What is Kyverno and how does it differ from OPA/Gatekeeper for Kubernetes policy enforcement?
A) Kyverno and OPA/Gatekeeper are identical — both use Rego for policy language
B) Kyverno is K8s-native (policies written in YAML, not Rego) and supports generate (create resources when they don't exist), mutate (modify resources), and validate (block non-compliant resources). OPA/Gatekeeper uses Rego (a separate language) and focuses on validation. Kyverno's YAML is easier for platform teams who already know K8s YAML
C) Kyverno replaces kubectl — all cluster operations go through Kyverno's policy engine
D) Kyverno only works on AWS EKS; OPA/Gatekeeper is cloud-agnostic
✅ Correct Answer: B

Kyverno = "Kubernetes in Greek for govern." Policies are K8s YAML — no new language to learn. Example: `require-labels` policy in Kyverno is a YAML file that checks `metadata.labels.team` exists. OPA/Gatekeeper requires writing Rego code. Kyverno's `generate` feature is unique — it can auto-create default NetworkPolicies and ResourceQuotas for new namespaces.

Kyverno Policy Example (YAML)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1                                              │
  │  kind: ClusterPolicy                                                    │
  │  metadata:                                                              │
  │    name: require-team-label                                             │
  │  spec:                                                                  │
  │    validationFailureAction: Enforce                                     │
  │    rules:                                                               │
  │    - name: check-team                                                   │
  │      match:                                                             │
  │        any:                                                             │
  │        - resources:                                                     │
  │            kinds: [Pod, Deployment, Service]                            │
  │      validate:                                                          │
  │        message: "Label 'team' is required"                              │
  │        pattern:                                                         │
  │          metadata:                                                      │
  │            labels:                                                      │
  │              team: "?*"               ← Must have a team label         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Kyverno uses YAML — NOT Rego. Why not C? Kyverno is an admission controller — doesn't replace kubectl. Why not D? Kyverno works on any Kubernetes cluster.

Security & Policy Q95 of 200
What is Falco and what type of security threats does it detect?
A) Falco is a CNCF-graduated runtime security tool that monitors Linux syscalls to detect abnormal behavior: a container spawning a shell (potential breach), reading sensitive files (/etc/shadow), making unexpected network connections, or running crypto miners. It's the security camera for your cluster
B) Falco is a Kubernetes admission controller that blocks pods with CRITICAL CVEs from starting
C) Falco scans container images in the registry for known vulnerabilities before deployment
D) Falco encrypts network traffic between pods using mTLS to prevent eavesdropping
✅ Correct Answer: A

Falco = runtime detection. Trivy = pre-deployment scanning. Together they provide defense-in-depth: Trivy blocks vulnerable images BEFORE deployment; Falco detects if an attacker compromises a running container. Falco rules trigger alerts on: shell spawned in container, unexpected outbound connection, sensitive file read, privilege escalation, kernel module loaded.

Falco — Runtime Security
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Falco monitors kernel syscalls via eBPF probe                          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Rules (examples):                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  • "A shell was spawned in a container" → ALERT (possible breach)│  │
  │  │  • "Container read /etc/shadow" → ALERT (credential theft)      │  │
  │  │  • "Outbound connection to 1.2.3.4 (known C2 server)" → ALERT   │  │
  │  │  • "Process ran with unexpected capabilities" → ALERT           │  │
  │  │  • "Cryptocurrency miner binary executed" → ALERT               │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Alert routes to: Falcosidekick → Slack, PagerDuty, OpsGenie, webhook  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's an admission webhook (can be paired with Falco). Why not C? That's Trivy/Grype. Why not D? That's mTLS via service mesh.

Security & Policy Q96 of 200
What is a NetworkPolicy "default-deny" rule and why is it the foundation of zero-trust networking in Kubernetes?
A) Default-deny blocks all egress traffic from pods to the internet
B) Default-deny is automatically enabled on all Kubernetes clusters — no configuration needed
C) A default-deny NetworkPolicy applies to all pods in a namespace and blocks ALL ingress traffic unless explicitly allowed by another policy. This enforces "deny all, then allow specific" — the zero-trust principle: nothing can talk to your pods unless you explicitly permit it
D) Default-deny is a firewall rule at the cloud provider VPC level, not a Kubernetes resource
✅ Correct Answer: C

By default, all pods in Kubernetes can communicate with all other pods. This is NOT zero-trust. A default-deny policy reverses this: BLOCK everything, then explicitly ALLOW only what's needed. `kubectl apply -f default-deny.yaml` → suddenly pods are isolated → then add allow rules for anihpj-api → anihpj-db on port 5432 only.

Default-Deny NetworkPolicy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                                    │
  │  metadata: name: default-deny-ingress, namespace: anihpj                │
  │  spec:                                                                  │
  │    podSelector: {}           # Applies to ALL pods                     │
  │    policyTypes: [Ingress]    # Block all incoming traffic              │
  │    # No ingress rules = deny all → nothing can reach these pods        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  THEN add specific allow rules:                                         │
  │  - Allow monitoring namespace to scrape metrics pods                   │
  │  - Allow anihpj-api pods to reach anihpj-db on port 5432               │
  │  - Allow ingress-controller to reach anihpj-web on port 80             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Ingress and egress are separate — default-deny can apply to either or both. Why not B? Kubernetes has NO default network policies — all traffic is allowed unless you restrict it. Why not D? NetworkPolicy is K8s-native — enforced by the CNI, not the cloud VPC.

Security & Policy Q97 of 200
What is mTLS (mutual TLS) in a service mesh and why is it critical for zero-trust security?
A) mTLS encrypts AND authenticates BOTH sides of a connection — the client verifies the server AND the server verifies the client via certificates. In a service mesh, every pod gets an identity certificate — anihpj-api PROVES it's really anihpj-api before anihpj-db accepts its connection. This prevents impersonation and man-in-the-middle attacks
B) mTLS is the same as regular TLS — it just encrypts traffic between pods
C) mTLS replaces NetworkPolicy — you don't need network policies if you have mTLS
D) mTLS only works between Kubernetes clusters — not within a single cluster
✅ Correct Answer: A

Regular TLS: client verifies server (your browser checks your bank's certificate). mTLS: BOTH verify each other. In a service mesh (Istio/Linkerd/Cilium), every pod gets an SPIFFE identity (spiffe://cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/api). Before anihpj-api connects to anihpj-db, they exchange and verify certificates — mutual authentication. No attacker can impersonate a service, even from inside the cluster.

TLS vs mTLS
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TLS (one-way):                        mTLS (mutual):                   │
  │  Client ──▶ Server                     Client ◀──▶ Server              │
  │  "Are you anihpj-db?" [verify cert]    "I am anihpj-api" [cert]        │
  │  (client authenticates server)         "I am anihpj-db" [cert]         │
  │                                         BOTH authenticated!            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  For anihpj/jobpost:                                                    │
  │  anihpj-api ──mTLS──▶ anihpj-db  (both verified, traffic encrypted)   │
  │  Attacker pod ──mTLS──▶ anihpj-db  (attacker has no valid cert → DENY)│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? mTLS adds mutual authentication — TLS alone only authenticates the server. Why not C? mTLS (encryption+auth) and NetworkPolicy (allow/deny rules) are complementary layers. Why not D? mTLS works within a single cluster via service mesh.

Security & Policy Q98 of 200
Why should etcd encryption at rest be enabled, and what does it protect against?
A) It encrypts network traffic between etcd nodes to prevent eavesdropping
B) It encrypts container logs stored on worker nodes
C) It encrypts container images in the registry
D) It encrypts Kubernetes Secrets (and optionally other resources) stored in etcd — preventing anyone with access to the etcd data files (backup, snapshot, disk access) from reading Secrets in plain text. Without it, Secrets are stored as base64 (NOT encrypted!) and easily decoded
✅ Correct Answer: D

By default, Kubernetes Secrets in etcd are base64-encoded — NOT encrypted. Anyone with access to an etcd snapshot can decode all Secrets: `cat snapshot | grep password`. Encryption at rest uses a KMS provider (AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault) to encrypt Secret data before writing to etcd. Mandatory for production clusters.

etcd Encryption at Rest
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT encryption:                                                    │
  │  Secret in etcd: data: cGFzc3dvcmQxMjM=  ← base64("password123")      │
  │  Anyone with etcd access → decoded → password123 revealed! ❌          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  WITH encryption:                                                       │
  │  Secret in etcd: data: kms:v1:AwE...encrypted_blob...                  │
  │  Anyone with etcd access → encrypted blob → cannot decode ✅           │
  │  Only API server (with KMS access) can decrypt → serves to authorized │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Enable: --encryption-provider-config=/etc/kubernetes/encryption.yaml  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's etcd peer TLS (separate from encryption at rest). Why not B? Container logs are not in etcd. Why not C? That's registry-level, not etcd.

Security & Policy Q99 of 200
What are Kubernetes Admission Controllers, and what's the difference between Mutating and Validating webhooks?
A) Admission controllers are kubectl plugins that validate YAML syntax before applying
B) Admission controllers intercept API requests before objects are persisted. Mutating webhooks MODIFY objects (inject sidecars, add labels, set defaults). Validating webhooks ACCEPT or REJECT objects (block privileged pods, require labels) without modifying them. Mutating runs FIRST, then Validating
C) Admission controllers replace RBAC — they handle both authentication and authorization
D) Admission controllers are only available in managed Kubernetes services (EKS, AKS, GKE)
✅ Correct Answer: B

Admission controllers are the platform team's quality gate. When a developer runs `kubectl apply -f pod.yaml`: API server authenticates → authorizes (RBAC) → Mutating webhooks (inject Istio sidecar) → Validating webhooks (Kyverno checks for "team" label) → persisted to etcd. This is where policy-as-code lives.

Admission Controller Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                              │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  Authentication → Authorization (RBAC)                                  │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  MUTATING Webhooks (run first):                                         │
  │  • Istio injects Envoy sidecar container                                │
  │  • Kyverno adds default resource limits                                 │
  │  • cert-manager injects CA bundle                                       │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  VALIDATING Webhooks (run second):                                      │
  │  • Kyverno: "Does pod have team label?" → YES → allow / NO → reject    │
  │  • OPA/Gatekeeper: "Is image from approved registry?" → check          │
  │  • Trivy admission: "Does image have CRITICAL CVEs?" → block if yes    │
  │       │                                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  Object persisted to etcd ✅                                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Admission controllers run server-side, not in kubectl. Why not C? They run AFTER authentication and authorization — they add additional validation. Why not D? Admission controllers are core Kubernetes — available everywhere.

Security & Policy Q100 of 200
What constitutes a comprehensive platform security posture covering the full application lifecycle?
A) Defense in depth across all layers: Pre-commit (secret scanning) → CI (SAST, dependency scan, image CVE scan) → Registry (signed images, SBOM) → Admission (Kyverno/OPA policies, image signature verification) → Runtime (Falco, NetworkPolicy, mTLS, Pod Security Standards) → Continuous (drift detection, compliance reporting, audit logging)
B) Running a vulnerability scanner once per quarter on production images
C) Using only images from Docker Hub with the "Official Image" badge
D) Installing a firewall at the network perimeter and trusting all internal cluster traffic
✅ Correct Answer: A

Platform security must span the ENTIRE lifecycle — there's no single tool that protects everything. Pre-commit catches secrets before they reach Git. CI catches vulnerabilities before images are built. Admission blocks non-compliant resources. Runtime detects active attacks. Each layer catches what the previous layer missed.

Full Platform Security Lifecycle
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER               TOOLS                     PROTECTS AGAINST         │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Pre-commit          git-leaks, ggshield       Hardcoded secrets       │
  │  CI (SAST)           Semgrep, SonarQube        Code vulnerabilities    │
  │  CI (deps)           Dependabot, Snyk          Known CVE in libraries  │
  │  CI (image scan)     Trivy, Grype              OS/packages CVEs        │
  │  CI (sign)           Cosign, Sigstore           Image tampering         │
  │  CI (SBOM)           Syft, Trivy               Zero-day response       │
  │  Registry            Harbor, ECR scanning      Stored image CVEs       │
  │  Admission           Kyverno, OPA/Gatekeeper   Misconfig, bad images   │
  │  Pod security        PSA (restricted)           Privileged containers   │
  │  Network             NetworkPolicy, mTLS        Lateral movement        │
  │  Runtime             Falco, Tetragon            Active attacks          │
  │  Secrets             External Secrets, Vault    Credential exposure     │
  │  Compliance          kube-bench, CIS            Misconfiguration        │
  │  Audit               K8s audit logs             Forensic investigation  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: git-leaks on pre-commit → Trivy in CI → Cosign signing → ECR scanning → Kyverno admission policies → Falco runtime detection → continuous kube-bench compliance checks. Every layer is essential — no single tool is sufficient.

Why not B/C/D? Quarterly scans, "official image" trust, and perimeter firewalls are single points of failure — not defense in depth.

🔒 Category 5: Security & Policy Enforcement (15%)Q101-Q200

🟣 Advanced: Security Baked Into the Platform

Security that developers get for free — not as an afterthought that blocks their deploys. RBAC, audit logging, CIS benchmarks, image scanning, SBOMs, and policy-as-code.

Q101-Q130 30 Questions 🟣 Advanced

5.1 RBAC & Security ControlsQ101-Q110

Security & Policy Q101 of 200
What is the difference between a ClusterRole and a Role in Kubernetes RBAC?
A) ClusterRole is for cluster admin only; Role is for developers
B) ClusterRole grants write access; Role grants read-only access
C) ClusterRole uses YAML; Role uses JSON
D) ClusterRole is cluster-scoped — it can grant permissions to cluster-wide resources (Nodes, PVs, Namespaces) or to namespaced resources across ALL namespaces. Role is namespace-scoped — permissions only apply within a single namespace
✅ Correct Answer: D

Role = "you can get pods in namespace dev." ClusterRole = "you can get pods in ALL namespaces" OR "you can get nodes (a cluster-scoped resource)." When you bind a ClusterRole with a RoleBinding (not ClusterRoleBinding), it's scoped to that namespace — giving you cluster-level permissions but only within one namespace.

Role vs ClusterRole
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Role (namespace-scoped):               ClusterRole (cluster-scoped):   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────────┐│
  │  │ kind: Role               │          │ kind: ClusterRole            ││
  │  │ metadata:                │          │ metadata:                    ││
  │  │   namespace: anihpj      │          │   name: pod-reader           ││
  │  │ rules:                   │          │ rules:                       ││
  │  │ - apiGroups: [""]       │          │ - apiGroups: [""]            ││
  │  │   resources: ["pods"]   │          │   resources: ["pods"]        ││
  │  │   verbs: ["get","list"] │          │   verbs: ["get","list"]      ││
  │  │                          │          │                               ││
  │  │ Scope: ONLY anihpj ns    │          │ Scope: ALL namespaces!        ││
  │  └──────────────────────────┘          └──────────────────────────────┘│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A/B/C? Scope (namespace vs cluster) is the defining difference — not admin level, verb type, or format.

Security & Policy Q102 of 200
What does kubectl auth can-i do, and why is it essential for debugging RBAC issues?
A) It creates new RBAC roles and bindings imperatively
B) It checks whether a specific user/ServiceAccount CAN perform a specific action — `kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=alice -n dev` returns yes/no. Essential for verifying RBAC policies work as intended before a developer reports "I can't deploy!"
C) It resets all RBAC permissions to cluster defaults for security audits
D) It generates a report of all security vulnerabilities in the cluster's RBAC configuration
✅ Correct Answer: B

`kubectl auth can-i` is the RBAC testing tool. Before deploying a new ServiceAccount, test it: `kubectl auth can-i create deployments --as=system:serviceaccount:dev:anihpj-api -n dev`. Use `--list` to see ALL permissions. This catches RBAC misconfigurations before they become production incidents.

kubectl auth can-i
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=alice -n anihpj                  │
  │  yes  ✅ — Alice CAN create pods in anihpj namespace                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i delete secrets --as=alice -n anihpj              │
  │  no   ❌ — Alice CANNOT delete secrets (good! least privilege!)        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  $ kubectl auth can-i --list --as=alice -n anihpj                      │
  │  Resources          Non-Resource URLs   Resource Names   Verbs          │
  │  pods               []                  []               [get list]     │
  │  deployments.apps   []                  []               [get list]     │
  │  services            []                  []               [get list]     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's kubectl create role/rolebinding. Why not C? RBAC doesn't have "defaults" to reset to. Why not D? That's kube-bench or a security scanner.

Security & Policy Q103 of 200
What is Kubernetes audit logging and what does an audit policy define?
A) Audit logging records all kubectl commands typed by developers for performance tracking
B) Audit logging is automatically enabled on all clusters with a default policy capturing everything
C) Audit logging records API server requests (who did what, when, from where, with what result). An audit policy defines WHAT to log at which detail level — from Metadata (basic info) to RequestResponse (full request and response bodies). Essential for security investigations and compliance
D) Audit logging replaces Prometheus metrics — it's the primary monitoring mechanism for Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: C

Audit logs answer "Who deleted that production namespace at 3 AM?" The policy defines stages (RequestReceived, ResponseComplete) and levels (None, Metadata, Request, RequestResponse). Log secret access at RequestResponse to see who read credentials; log pod deletions at Metadata to track cleanup. Without audit logging, security incidents have no forensic trail.

Kubernetes Audit Policy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Audit Policy Stages:                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  rules:                                                            │  │
  │  │  - level: Metadata          # Log who/what/when for all requests  │  │
  │  │    verbs: ["create","update","patch","delete"]                    │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  - level: RequestResponse   # Log full request+response bodies    │  │
  │  │    resources:                                                      │  │
  │  │    - group: ""              # Only for Secrets                    │  │
  │  │      resources: ["secrets"] # (capture who read credentials)      │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  - level: None              # Don't log health checks (too noisy) │  │
  │  │    users: ["system:kube-proxy"]                                   │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Audit logging captures API requests, not CLI keystrokes. Why not B? Audit logging must be explicitly enabled and configured. Why not D? Audit logging complements monitoring — it's not a replacement.

Security & Policy Q104 of 200
What is kube-bench and what does it check?
A) kube-bench checks whether your Kubernetes cluster is configured according to the CIS Kubernetes Benchmark — a security standard with hundreds of checks covering control plane components, etcd, worker nodes, and policies. It produces a report of PASS/FAIL/WARN for each check
B) kube-bench benchmarks the performance of your Kubernetes cluster by running load tests
C) kube-bench scans container images for vulnerabilities before they are deployed
D) kube-bench is a Kubernetes-native CI/CD tool that benchmarks pipeline speed
✅ Correct Answer: A

CIS Benchmark = industry-standard security configuration guide. kube-bench automates checking it. It runs as a Job on each node type (master/worker) and checks: is anonymous auth disabled? Is etcd using TLS? Is the API server using a secure port? Are audit logs enabled? Results: PASS (compliant), FAIL (non-compliant), WARN (manual review needed).

kube-bench — CIS Benchmark
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kube-bench run --targets master                                      │
  │  [INFO] 1 Master Node Security Configuration                            │
  │  [PASS] 1.1.1 Ensure that the API server pod spec file permissions      │
  │  [FAIL] 1.1.12 Ensure that the etcd data directory ownership is set     │
  │  [PASS] 1.2.1 Ensure that anonymous-auth argument is set to false       │
  │  [WARN] 1.2.6 Ensure that the --kubelet-https argument is set           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  == Remediations ==                                                     │
  │  1.1.12 Run: chown etcd:etcd /var/lib/etcd                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? That's k6 or locust for load testing. Why not C? That's Trivy/Grype. Why not D? kube-bench is a security tool, not a CI/CD tool.

Security & Policy Q105 of 200
What does kubectl create token do, and when is it useful?
A) It creates a permanent API token for a user to authenticate with kubectl
B) It creates a GitHub personal access token for CI/CD pipeline authentication
C) It creates a cloud provider IAM token for accessing AWS/GCP/Azure resources
D) It generates a short-lived, time-bound JWT token for a ServiceAccount — useful for CI/CD pipelines, debugging pod permissions, or authenticating to the API server without long-lived secrets. Tokens are automatically mounted in pods but this command creates them manually
✅ Correct Answer: D

Since K8s 1.24, ServiceAccounts no longer auto-create long-lived Secrets. Tokens are now time-bound JWTs created via the TokenRequest API. `kubectl create token anihpj-api -n anihpj --duration=1h` generates a 1-hour token. Use it to test pod permissions, authenticate CI pipelines, or manually access the API server.

ServiceAccount Tokens (JWT)
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl create token anihpj-api -n anihpj --duration=2h             │
  │  eyJhbGciOiJSUzI1NiIsImtpZCI6Ij... (JWT token, expires in 2 hours)     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Token contains:                                                        │
  │  {                                                                      │
  │    "sub": "system:serviceaccount:anihpj:anihpj-api",                   │
  │    "kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/namespace": "anihpj",                 │
  │    "exp": 1718400000  ← Expiration timestamp                           │
  │  }                                                                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Use token to make API calls:                                           │
  │  curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" https://api-server/...         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? No permanent tokens — they're time-bound by design. Why not B? Not GitHub-related. Why not C? Not cloud IAM — K8s-native JWT.

Security & Policy Q106 of 200
What is the purpose of IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) in AWS EKS?
A) IRSA creates IAM users for each developer on the EKS cluster
B) IRSA maps Kubernetes ServiceAccounts to AWS IAM roles using OIDC — pods can assume IAM roles without static AWS credentials. The anihpj-api pod's ServiceAccount gets an IAM role allowing S3 access, and the credentials are automatically rotated. No access keys stored anywhere
C) IRSA replaces Kubernetes RBAC — it's the primary authorization mechanism on EKS
D) IRSA encrypts all pod-to-pod traffic on EKS using IAM-generated TLS certificates
✅ Correct Answer: B

IRSA is the secure bridge between K8s ServiceAccounts and AWS IAM. Annotate a ServiceAccount with an IAM role ARN → pods using that SA get temporary AWS credentials via the EKS OIDC provider. No `aws_access_key_id` in Secrets. No static credentials to rotate. Pod identity is managed declaratively.

IRSA — IAM Roles for Service Accounts
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ServiceAccount: anihpj-api                              AWS           │
  │  annotations:                                           ┌────────────┐ │
  │    eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn:                          │ IAM Role:  │ │
  │      arn:aws:iam::123:role/anihpj-s3-reader             │ S3 read    │ │
  │                                      ─── OIDC trust ──▶│ access     │ │
  │  Pod using this SA:                                     └────────────┘ │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐                                            │
  │  │ AWS SDK auto-detects   │  ← No access keys needed!                  │
  │  │ IAM role via OIDC      │                                            │
  │  │ → accesses S3 bucket   │                                            │
  │  └────────────────────────┘                                            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? IRSA maps SA→IAM role, not users. Why not C? IRSA works alongside RBAC, doesn't replace it. Why not D? That's mTLS via service mesh.

Security & Policy Q107 of 200
What is OPA/Gatekeeper and how does it differ from Kyverno?
A) OPA/Gatekeeper and Kyverno are the same tool with different names
B) OPA/Gatekeeper only works with OpenShift; Kyverno works with vanilla Kubernetes
C) OPA/Gatekeeper uses Rego (a declarative policy language) with ConstraintTemplates and Constraints — powerful and flexible but requires learning Rego. Kyverno uses K8s-native YAML policies — easier to learn but less flexible for complex logic. Both are admission controllers for policy enforcement
D) OPA/Gatekeeper replaces the Kubernetes API server's authorization layer
✅ Correct Answer: C

OPA (Open Policy Agent) was the first policy engine, later Gatekeeper made it K8s-native. Rego is powerful (Turing-complete) but has a learning curve. Kyverno's YAML approach is more accessible but less suited for deeply nested logic. Choose Kyverno for simpler K8s policies (require labels, block :latest); choose OPA/Gatekeeper for complex cross-resource rules.

OPA/Gatekeeper vs Kyverno
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  OPA/Gatekeeper (Rego):              Kyverno (YAML):                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ violation[{"msg": msg}] {│       │ spec:                         │   │
  │  │   input.review.object    │       │   rules:                      │   │
  │  │   .spec.template.spec    │       │   - name: require-team-label │   │
  │  │   .containers[_]         │       │     match:                    │   │
  │  │   .resources.limits      │       │       resources:              │   │
  │  │   .cpu == ""             │       │         kinds: [Pod]          │   │
  │  │   msg := "CPU limit req" │       │     validate:                 │   │
  │  │ }                         │       │       message: "Need team"   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘       │       pattern:                │   │
  │  Powerful, complex logic            │         metadata:             │   │
  │  Steeper learning curve             │           labels:             │   │
  │                                      │             team: "?*"        │   │
  │                                      └──────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                      Simple, YAML-native, easy to adopt │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? They use different policy languages and architectures. Why not B? Both work on any Kubernetes. Why not D? They're admission controllers — they run after authentication/authorization.

Security & Policy Q108 of 200
What is a distroless container image and why is it more secure?
A) Distroless images contain ONLY your application and its runtime dependencies — no shell, no package manager, no curl, no apt. If an attacker compromises your app, they can't spawn a shell or download tools because those don't exist in the image. Drastically reduces attack surface and CVE count
B) Distroless images are container images that run without an operating system kernel
C) Distroless images automatically encrypt all data at rest within the container
D) Distroless images are images that have been stripped of all labels and metadata for anonymity
✅ Correct Answer: A

"Distroless" = no distribution. No apt, yum, bash, curl, or any OS utilities. Just your compiled application + runtime (like Python interpreter + your Django code). If an attacker gets RCE on anihpj-api, they're stuck — no shell to run commands, no curl to exfiltrate data. Google's distroless images have 97% fewer CVEs than full Debian images.

Distroless vs Full OS Image
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Full Debian Image (200+ MB):          Distroless Python (50 MB):       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ ✅ Your app (Django)          │     │ ✅ Your app (Django)          │ │
  │  │ ✅ Python runtime            │     │ ✅ Python runtime            │ │
  │  │ ❌ bash, sh, zsh             │     │ ❌ No shell — attacker stuck! │ │
  │  │ ❌ apt, apt-get              │     │ ❌ No package manager         │ │
  │  │ ❌ curl, wget                │     │ ❌ No download tools          │ │
  │  │ ❌ ls, cat, grep, find       │     │ ❌ No file exploration        │ │
  │  │ ❌ gcc, make                 │     │ ❌ No compilers               │ │
  │  │ ❌ 200+ CVEs in OS packages  │     │ ✅ ~3 CVEs (runtime only)    │ │
  │  │ Attacker: "I'm in! Now what?"│     │ Attacker: "...now what?!"     │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘     └──────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? All containers share the host kernel — distroless still has a minimal OS layer. Why not C? Encryption at rest is a storage concern, not distroless. Why not D? Distroless means missing OS packages, not missing metadata.

Security & Policy Q109 of 200
What is HashiCorp Vault and how does it integrate with Kubernetes for secrets management?
A) Vault is a Kubernetes built-in component that replaces etcd for storing all cluster data
B) Vault is a secrets management platform that can inject secrets into Kubernetes pods via the Vault Sidecar Injector (mutating webhook adds a Vault agent sidecar that fetches and caches secrets) or via the Vault CSI Provider (mounts secrets as volumes). Secrets never touch etcd
C) Vault is an alternative container runtime that replaces containerd for enhanced security
D) Vault is a Kubernetes monitoring tool that detects unauthorized access to Secrets
✅ Correct Answer: B

Vault provides dynamic secrets (DB credentials generated on-demand, auto-expire), encryption as a service, and audit logging for all secret access. In K8s, the Vault Agent Injector mutates pods to add a Vault sidecar that authenticates via K8s ServiceAccount, fetches secrets, and writes them to a shared volume. The app reads from the volume as usual.

Vault + Kubernetes Integration
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: anihpj-api                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  App Container                   Vault Agent Sidecar             │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────────┐    │  │
  │  │  │ Reads /vault/secrets │◀──────│ Authenticates via K8s SA │    │  │
  │  │  │  POSTGRES_PASSWORD  │       │ Fetches DB creds from     │    │  │
  │  │  │  REDIS_PASSWORD     │       │ Vault → writes to volume  │    │  │
  │  │  └──────────────────────┘       │ Auto-renews before expiry │    │  │
  │  │                                  └──────────────────────────┘    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Vault (external):                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  • Dynamic DB credentials (created on-demand, expire in 24h)     │  │
  │  │  • Audit log: "Pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 read DB creds at 10:30:00"  │  │
  │  │  • Encryption as a Service (encrypt/decrypt via API)             │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Vault is external — it doesn't replace etcd. Why not C? Vault manages secrets, not containers. Why not D? That's Falco's job.

Security & Policy Q110 of 200
What is the CIS Kubernetes Benchmark and which categories does it cover?
A) It's a performance benchmark that measures how fast your cluster processes API requests
B) It's a certification exam that Kubernetes administrators must pass to prove competency
C) It's a single checklist of 10 security rules that apply to all Kubernetes clusters
D) It's a comprehensive security standard with ~100+ checks across: Control Plane (API server, scheduler, controller-manager), etcd, Worker Nodes (kubelet, kube-proxy), and Policies (RBAC, NetworkPolicy, Pod Security, Secrets). Each check has remediation steps and audit procedures
✅ Correct Answer: D

The CIS Benchmark (Center for Internet Security) provides concrete, auditable security configurations. It's organized into sections: 1=Control Plane, 2=etcd, 3=Control Plane Config, 4=Worker Nodes, 5=Policies. Each check is numbered (1.1.1, 1.2.3) with a clear description, audit command, and remediation. kube-bench automates checking against this benchmark.

CIS Kubernetes Benchmark Sections
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1. Control Plane Components  (API server, scheduler, controller-mgr)  │
  │     • Anonymous auth disabled? TLS enabled? Audit logging on?          │
  │  2. etcd                      (Data store security)                    │
  │     • TLS for peer/client? Data dir permissions correct?               │
  │  3. Control Plane Config      (Config file permissions, ownership)     │
  │  4. Worker Nodes              (kubelet config, kube-proxy)             │
  │     • --protect-kernel-defaults? ReadOnlyPort disabled?                │
  │  5. Policies                  (RBAC, NetworkPolicy, Pod Security, etc.)│
  │     • Least privilege RBAC? default-deny NetworkPolicy? PSA enforced?  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's performance testing. Why not B? That's the CKA/CKS exam. Why not C? It's comprehensive — 100+ checks across multiple categories.

5.2 Policy Engines & ComplianceQ111-Q115

Security & Policy Q111 of 200
What happens when a Kyverno policy with validationFailureAction: Enforce blocks a resource?
A) The resource is created but marked with a warning annotation for later review
B) The Kyverno controller restarts and the resource is queued for retry
C) The API server rejects the request immediately — the resource is NOT created. The user receives a clear error message explaining which policy was violated and why. This is the "hard block" mode for production enforcement
D) The resource is created but all its pods are immediately terminated by Kyverno
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kyverno as a ValidatingAdmissionWebhook intercepts the API request BEFORE the resource is persisted to etcd. `Enforce` = block (hard fail). `Audit` = log only (don't block — useful for testing policies before enforcing). Always start with Audit mode for new policies, verify no false positives, then switch to Enforce.

Kyverno — Enforce vs Audit
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  validationFailureAction: Enforce                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  $ kubectl apply -f pod-without-limits.yaml                       │  │
  │  │  Error from server: admission webhook "validate.kyverno.svc"     │  │
  │  │  denied the request:                                              │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  policy Pod/default/require-resources:                            │  │
  │  │    require-resources: validation error:                           │  │
  │  │      CPU and memory resource limits are required.                  │  │
  │  │  ❌ Resource NOT created                                           │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Audit mode logs warnings — Enforce blocks. Why not B? No restart — just rejection. Why not D? Pods are never created — admission happens before persistence.

Security & Policy Q112 of 200
What is container image vulnerability scanning and where should it run in the delivery pipeline?
A) Scan images in production every night and patch vulnerabilities the next morning
B) Scan at multiple stages: in CI after build (block push on CRITICAL CVEs), in the registry continuously (detect newly discovered CVEs in existing images), and at admission (block deployment of images exceeding CVE thresholds). Defense in depth: catch CVEs at every gate
C) Scanning is only needed before the initial deployment — running containers are immune to new CVEs
D) Image scanning is handled automatically by Kubernetes — no additional tools needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Scan everywhere: CI blocks new CVEs, registry scanning detects CVEs discovered yesterday in images built last week, admission blocks deploying images with CVEs above threshold even if they're already in the registry. A CVE discovered in OpenSSL today might affect your image built 3 months ago — registry scanning catches this.

CVE Scanning — Defense in Depth
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① CI (Trivy):    Image built → scanned → CRITICAL CVE? → BLOCK PUSH   │
  │  ② Registry (ECR): Image stored → continuous scan → new CVE? → ALERT   │
  │  ③ Admission:      kubectl apply → webhook checks scan results → BLOCK  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Example: Image built Jan 1 (clean). OpenSSL CVE found Jan 15.          │
  │  Registry scan catches it Jan 15 → Admission blocks deploy Jan 16.     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Nightly-only scanning leaves a 24-hour window for vulnerable images. Why not C? New CVEs are discovered daily — running images need continuous scanning. Why not D? K8s doesn't scan images — you need Trivy/Grype/Snyk.

Security & Policy Q113 of 200
What is a Security Context in Kubernetes and which key settings should be configured for production pods?
A) Security Context defines privilege and access control settings for pods/containers. Production must-have: runAsNonRoot: true (no root), readOnlyRootFilesystem: true (immutable FS), allowPrivilegeEscalation: false (no setuid binaries), capabilities.drop: [ALL] (no Linux capabilities), seccompProfile (syscall filtering)
B) Security Context is a Kubernetes dashboard that shows which pods have security vulnerabilities
C) Security Context defines which network ports a pod can listen on
D) Security Context is the encryption key used to secure pod-to-pod communication
✅ Correct Answer: A

Security Context is your pod's security baseline. `runAsNonRoot: true` blocks containers running as UID 0. `readOnlyRootFilesystem: true` prevents attackers from writing malware to disk. `allowPrivilegeEscalation: false` blocks setuid binaries. `capabilities.drop: [ALL]` removes all Linux capabilities. These should be default for all workloads.

Pod Security Context — Production Settings
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                                   │
  │    securityContext:              # Pod-level                            │
  │      runAsNonRoot: true          # ❌ No root (UID 0)                   │
  │      seccompProfile:                                                     │
  │        type: RuntimeDefault      # Syscall filtering                    │
  │    containers:                                                           │
  │    - name: anihpj-api                                                   │
  │      securityContext:            # Container-level                      │
  │        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true         # ❌ Immutable filesystem    │
  │        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false      # ❌ No setuid              │
  │        capabilities:                                                      │
  │          drop: [ALL]             # ❌ No Linux capabilities              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B/C/D? None of these describe Security Context — it's about privilege and access control settings.

Security & Policy Q114 of 200
What is certificate rotation in Kubernetes and how is it managed?
A) Certificates in Kubernetes never expire — they are valid for the lifetime of the cluster
B) Certificate rotation requires manually deleting and recreating the entire cluster every year
C) Certificate rotation is handled automatically by the cloud provider — on-prem clusters must be manually restarted
D) Kubernetes certificates have expiration dates (typically 1 year). `kubeadm certs check-expiration` shows when they expire. `kubeadm certs renew all` renews all certificates. kubelet certificates can auto-renew if the CSR approver is configured. cert-manager handles application TLS auto-renewal
✅ Correct Answer: D

Certificate expiry = cluster outage if not managed. Check: `kubeadm certs check-expiration` shows days remaining for each cert (apiserver, etcd, front-proxy). Renew: `kubeadm certs renew all`. After renewal, restart control plane pods. For kubelet certs, enable the TLS bootstrapping + CSR auto-approver. Monitor cert expiry in Prometheus + set alerts.

Certificate Lifecycle
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubeadm certs check-expiration                                       │
  │  CERTIFICATE                EXPIRES                  RESIDUAL TIME      │
  │  admin.conf                 Jun 14, 2027 10:00 UTC   364d               │
  │  apiserver                  Jun 14, 2027 10:00 UTC   364d               │
  │  apiserver-etcd-client      Jun 14, 2027 10:00 UTC   364d               │
  │  etcd-healthcheck-client    Jun 14, 2027 10:00 UTC   364d               │
  │                                                                         │
  │  $ kubeadm certs renew all  ← Renew ALL certs before expiry            │
  │  $ systemctl restart kubelet ← Restart to pick up new certs            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? All K8s certs expire (typically 1 year for kubeadm). Why not B? `kubeadm certs renew` renews without cluster rebuild. Why not C? On-prem clusters can auto-renew too via CSR approver and cert-manager.

Security & Policy Q115 of 200
What is the Principle of Least Privilege and how does Kubernetes RBAC enforce it?
A) Every user gets cluster-admin by default and permissions are removed as needed
B) Users and ServiceAccounts get ONLY the minimum permissions needed for their specific task — and nothing more. Implemented via narrowly-scoped Roles (specific verbs on specific resources in specific namespaces) rather than broad ClusterRoles. Audited via `kubectl auth can-i --list`
C) Least privilege means all users share a single read-only ServiceAccount
D) Least privilege is automatically enforced by Kubernetes — no configuration needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Least privilege: anihpj-api's ServiceAccount needs get/list pods in anihpj namespace — give it a Role with ONLY those verbs, ONLY on pods, ONLY in anihpj. Not cluster-admin. Not get secrets. Not create namespaces. Every permission must be justified. Regularly audit: `kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:anihpj:api` and remove unused permissions.

Least Privilege RBAC
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ BAD (over-permission):            ✅ GOOD (least privilege):        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole            │    │ kind: Role                    │  │
  │  │ rules:                       │    │   namespace: anihpj           │  │
  │  │ - apiGroups: ["*"]          │    │ rules:                        │  │
  │  │   resources: ["*"]          │    │ - apiGroups: ["apps"]          │  │
  │  │   verbs: ["*"]               │    │   resources: ["deployments"]  │  │
  │  │ → Access to EVERYTHING       │    │   verbs: ["get","list","watch"]│  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────┘    │ → Only list deployments       │  │
  │                                       │   in anihpj namespace        │  │
  │                                       └──────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's the opposite — maximum privilege. Why not C? Least privilege is per-user/SA, not shared. Why not D? K8s has NO default RBAC — you must configure it.

4.2 Distributed Tracing & Log AggregationQ116-Q120

Observability Q116 of 200
What is distributed tracing and why can't logs alone solve microservice debugging?
A) Distributed tracing is a log aggregation tool that collects logs from all microservices
B) Logs are sufficient for debugging — distributed tracing is an optional optimization
C) Distributed tracing follows a single request ACROSS multiple services — showing the complete journey with timing per service. With 10 microservices, one slow request generates 10 separate log entries with no correlation. Tracing links them into one waterfall view: "POST /api/jobs took 2.3s — 1.8s in DB query, 0.3s in Redis, 0.2s in auth"
D) Distributed tracing replaces the need for metrics and logs — it provides all observability data
✅ Correct Answer: C

Without tracing: "anihpj-api is slow." With tracing: "POST /api/jobs → 2.3s → 1.8s spent in PostgreSQL SELECT, 300ms in Redis GET, 200ms in auth service." You instantly know WHERE to fix. Tracing uses span IDs and trace IDs propagated via HTTP headers (W3C traceparent) to link all service calls into one end-to-end view.

Distributed Trace — Request Journey
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Trace ID: abc123 — POST /api/jobs                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  anihpj-web (200ms)                                               │  │
  │  │    ├── anihpj-api (2.1s)                                          │  │
  │  │    │   ├── auth-service (200ms)                                   │  │
  │  │    │   ├── Redis GET (300ms)                                      │  │
  │  │    │   └── PostgreSQL SELECT (1.8s) ← BOTTLENECK!                │  │
  │  │    └── anihpj-api responds (50ms)                                 │  │
  │  │  Total: 2.3s                                                      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  Without tracing: "The API is slow" — WHERE?                            │
  │  With tracing: "PostgreSQL query takes 1.8s" — FIX IT!                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Tracing follows request paths — logs are individual entries. Why not B? At microservice scale, logs alone can't show the full request journey. Why not D? Tracing complements metrics and logs — doesn't replace them.

Observability Q117 of 200
What is Jaeger and what are its key components for distributed tracing?
A) Jaeger is a CNCF-graduated distributed tracing system. Key components: Jaeger Agent (receives spans from apps), Collector (processes and stores), Query (UI for searching traces), and Ingester (for Kafka-based pipelines). It visualizes traces as waterfall diagrams showing service-to-service timing
B) Jaeger is a Prometheus replacement that collects metrics instead of traces
C) Jaeger is a Kubernetes log aggregator similar to Loki
D) Jaeger is a security tool that traces attacker movements through the cluster
✅ Correct Answer: A

Jaeger (by Uber, now CNCF Graduated) is the original cloud native tracing system. It visualizes traces as Gantt charts showing each service's contribution to total latency. Sampling strategies: constant (always), probabilistic (x%), rate-limiting (N/sec), and adaptive (more samples when errors spike). Integrates with OpenTelemetry for instrumentation.

Jaeger Architecture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  App (OTel SDK) → Jaeger Agent/Collector → Storage (Cassandra/ES/S3)   │
  │                                                    │                    │
  │                                                    ▼                    │
  │                                              Jaeger Query UI            │
  │                                              ┌──────────────────────┐   │
  │                                              │ Trace: abc123         │   │
  │                                              │ Duration: 2.3s        │   │
  │                                              │ Spans: 5              │   │
  │                                              │ ▬▬▬ anihpj-web 200ms │   │
  │                                              │  ▬▬▬▬▬ anihpj-api 2.1s│  │
  │                                              │   ▬ auth 200ms        │   │
  │                                              │   ▬ Redis 300ms       │   │
  │                                              │   ▬▬▬▬▬ DB 1.8s ← SLOW│  │
  │                                              └──────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? Jaeger does tracing, Prometheus does metrics — complementary. Why not C? That's Loki. Why not D? That's Falco.

Observability Q118 of 200
What is Grafana Tempo and how does it differ from Jaeger for distributed tracing?
A) Tempo is a fork of Jaeger with an identical architecture but a different UI
B) Tempo only traces HTTP requests; Jaeger traces all protocols including gRPC and TCP
C) Tempo replaces Prometheus for metrics collection alongside tracing capabilities
D) Tempo stores traces in object storage (S3/GCS) without indexing — dramatically cheaper at scale. Instead of indexing every span, it uses a bloom-filter-based search. Integrates natively with Grafana for unified metrics+logs+traces view. Jaeger is battle-tested with richer query features but higher storage cost
✅ Correct Answer: D

Tempo's killer feature: cost. Object storage (S3) is ~10x cheaper than Elasticsearch/Cassandra for long-term trace retention. No indexing means fewer moving parts but also less flexible search (you search by trace ID or tags, not arbitrary span fields). For 1TB/day of traces: Jaeger+ES ~$3K/month, Tempo+S3 ~$200/month.

Jaeger vs Tempo
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Feature           Jaeger                    Tempo                      │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  Storage           Cassandra/ES (indexed)     S3/GCS (object, no index)│
  │  Search            Rich (span fields, tags)   By trace ID + tags       │
  │  Cost (1TB/day)    High (~$3K/mo)             Low (~$200/mo)           │
  │  Grafana integ.    Via data source            Native (Tempo data src)  │
  │  CNCF status       Graduated                  Sandbox                  │
  │  Best for          Rich query needs           Cost-sensitive, Grafana  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Completely different architecture — Tempo is object-storage-based. Why not B? Both support all protocols via OTel. Why not C? Tempo does tracing, not metrics.

Observability Q119 of 200
What is the purpose of structured logging (JSON logs) vs unstructured text logs?
A) Structured logs are only needed for compliance audits — for debugging, text logs are sufficient
B) Structured logs (JSON) are machine-parseable — tools like Loki and Elasticsearch can automatically extract fields (timestamp, level, trace_id, user_id, error) without regex parsing. This enables fast filtering: `{app="api"} | json | level="ERROR" | trace_id="abc123"`. Unstructured logs require fragile regex patterns
C) Structured logs are larger than text logs — they waste storage and should be avoided
D) Structured logging means encrypting all log output for security compliance
✅ Correct Answer: B

JSON logs = queryable logs. `{"timestamp":"2026-06-14T10:30:00Z","level":"ERROR","message":"DB connection refused","trace_id":"abc123","user_id":"456"}`. Loki can extract `trace_id` and link directly to Tempo traces. Grafana can show "all ERROR logs with trace_id X." Text logs require fragile, slow regex parsing that breaks when log format changes.

Structured vs Unstructured Logging
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ Unstructured:                                                       │
  │  [2026-06-14 10:30:00] ERROR anihpj-api Connection refused: anihpj-db  │
  │  → Regex needed: \[(?P<timestamp>.*?)\]\s+(?P<level>\w+)\s+(?P<msg>.*)│
  │  → Brittle! Format change breaks all queries.                           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ Structured (JSON):                                                  │
  │  {"ts":"2026-06-14T10:30:00Z","level":"ERROR","msg":"DB refused",      │
  │   "service":"anihpj-api","trace_id":"abc123","user_id":"456"}          │
  │  → {service="anihpj-api"} | json | level="ERROR" | line_format "{{.msg}}"│
  │  → Fast, flexible, format-independent                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Structured logging is essential for operational debugging at scale. Why not C? The overhead is negligible; the operational benefits are massive. Why not D? Encryption is separate — structured logging is about format.

Observability Q120 of 200
What is the purpose of correlating metrics, logs, and traces in a unified observability platform?
A) Correlation enables the debugging flow: Metrics show WHAT (error rate spiked at 10:30) → click to see relevant Logs showing WHY (NullPointerException) → click to see the Trace showing WHERE (1.8s in DB query). This unified workflow reduces MTTR from hours to minutes
B) Correlation means storing all three data types in the same database to reduce storage costs
C) Correlation is only needed for compliance reporting — not for operational debugging
D) Correlation means metrics replace logs and traces — only one data type is needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Grafana exemplifies this: Prometheus graph shows latency spike → click the spike → Grafana queries Loki for logs in that time window → one log has `trace_id=abc123` → click → Grafana queries Tempo → waterfall shows PostgreSQL query taking 1.8s. Three clicks, root cause found. Without correlation: search metrics, switch to log tool, search logs, switch to trace tool, search traces — 15+ minutes.

Unified Observability — Metrics → Logs → Traces
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① 📊 Grafana Dashboard: p99 latency spiked at 10:30 (Prometheus)      │
  │       │ Click the spike                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  ② 📝 Loki: Shows ERROR logs with trace_id=abc123 at 10:30             │
  │       │ Click trace_id                                                  │
  │       ▼                                                                  │
  │  ③ 🔍 Tempo: Trace abc123 shows PostgreSQL SELECT took 1.8s            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Root cause found in < 30 seconds. Fix: Add index to slow DB query.    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not B? They're stored in different backends — correlation is at the query/Grafana level. Why not C? Correlation is essential for fast incident response. Why not D? Each pillar provides unique insights — they complement, not replace, each other.

4.3 Platform Operations & ReliabilityQ121-Q125

Observability Q121 of 200
What is the correct sequence for upgrading a kubeadm cluster to a new Kubernetes version?
A) Upgrade all worker nodes first, then upgrade the control plane node last
B) Upgrade kubeadm FIRST, then `kubeadm upgrade plan` → `kubeadm upgrade apply` on control plane, then upgrade kubelet+kubectl on control plane, THEN drain each worker → upgrade kubeadm+kubelet on worker → uncordon. Control plane must be upgraded before workers
C) Delete the cluster and recreate it from scratch with the new version
D) Run `kubectl upgrade cluster --version=v1.31.0` from any node
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubeadm upgrade follows a strict order: 1) Upgrade kubeadm binary, 2) `kubeadm upgrade plan` to verify, 3) `kubeadm upgrade apply` on control plane, 4) Upgrade kubelet+kubectl + restart kubelet on CP, 5) For each worker: drain → upgrade kubeadm → `kubeadm upgrade node` → upgrade kubelet → uncordon. Control plane MUST be upgraded first.

kubeadm Upgrade Sequence
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Control Plane (FIRST):                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  apt-mark unhold kubeadm && apt-get install kubeadm=1.31.x       │  │
  │  │  kubeadm upgrade plan                                              │  │
  │  │  kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.x                                    │  │
  │  │  apt-get install kubelet=1.31.x kubectl=1.31.x                   │  │
  │  │  systemctl restart kubelet                                        │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Workers (SECOND, one at a time):                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets                       │  │
  │  │  (SSH to worker) apt-get install kubeadm=1.31.x kubelet=1.31.x  │  │
  │  │  kubeadm upgrade node                                             │  │
  │  │  systemctl restart kubelet                                        │  │
  │  │  kubectl uncordon worker-1                                        │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? Workers can't be newer than control plane — K8s enforces ≤1 version skew. Why not C? In-place upgrades are supported and preserve workloads. Why not D? There's no kubectl upgrade command.

Observability Q122 of 200
What is etcd backup and restore and why is it the most critical platform operation?
A) etcd backup copies container images to a backup registry for disaster recovery
B) etcd backup is optional — Kubernetes automatically replicates all data across nodes
C) etcd stores the ENTIRE cluster state (all objects, secrets, configs). Without a backup, losing etcd means losing your entire cluster. `etcdctl snapshot save` creates a point-in-time backup. Restore with `etcdctl snapshot restore` + update etcd manifest. Test restores regularly!
D) etcd backup is managed automatically by the cloud provider — no manual intervention needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

etcd IS your cluster. It stores every Pod, Service, Deployment, Secret, ConfigMap, CRD, and RBAC rule. Lose etcd = lose everything. Backup: `ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd-$(date +%Y%m%d).db` with TLS flags. Restore: create new data dir, restore snapshot, point etcd manifest to new dir. TEST your restore process — an untested backup is not a backup.

etcd Backup & Restore
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKUP:                                                                │
  │  ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd.db \                  │
  │    --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \                                 │
  │    --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \                           │
  │    --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \                         │
  │    --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key                            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  RESTORE:                                                               │
  │  etcdctl snapshot restore /backup/etcd.db \                             │
  │    --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restore                                     │
  │  # Update /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml to use new data-dir       │
  │  # kubelet auto-restarts etcd pod → cluster state recovered!           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's registry replication, not etcd backup. Why not B? etcd replicates within its cluster but doesn't protect against corruption/deletion. Why not D? Even on EKS/AKS/GKE, you should verify backup policies — the control plane is managed but backup is your responsibility.

Observability Q123 of 200
What is a PodDisruptionBudget (PDB) and what happens when a drain violates it?
A) PDB limits the total number of pods that can exist in a namespace
B) PDB automatically reschedules pods when a node becomes unhealthy
C) PDB increases pod replicas before a drain to ensure zero downtime
D) PDB defines the minimum number of pods that must remain AVAILABLE during voluntary disruptions (drain, node upgrade). If a drain would violate the PDB (e.g., 1 replica with minAvailable:1), the drain command BLOCKS until the PDB is satisfied or you force the drain
✅ Correct Answer: D

PDB protects application availability during maintenance. `minAvailable: 2` on a 3-replica Deployment means: during a drain, at least 2 pods must be running at all times. The drain will evict pods one at a time, waiting for new pods to become ready before evicting the next. If PDB can't be satisfied (e.g., minAvailable:1 on a 1-replica StatefulSet), `kubectl drain` will hang until you resolve it.

PodDisruptionBudget
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  anihpj-api: 3 replicas, PDB: minAvailable=2                            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  kubectl drain node-1:                                                  │
  │  Step 1: Evict api-pod-1 (2 remain) → wait for new pod on node-2       │
  │  Step 2: Evict api-pod-2 (1 remain) → wait for new pod on node-2       │
  │  Step 3: All pods moved → drain complete ✅                             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  anihpj-db: 1 replica, PDB: minAvailable=1                              │
  │  kubectl drain node-1:                                                  │
  │  → BLOCKED! Can't evict the only pod without violating PDB              │
  │  → Fix: Temporarily scale to 2 replicas OR force drain                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? That's ResourceQuota. Why not B? That's Kubernetes self-healing, not PDB. Why not C? PDB doesn't auto-scale — it just blocks violations.

Observability Q124 of 200
What is the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) and what metrics can trigger scaling?
A) HPA only scales based on CPU utilization — memory and custom metrics require manual scaling
B) HPA automatically adjusts pod replicas based on: resource metrics (CPU%, memory% via metrics-server), custom metrics (requests/sec, queue length via Prometheus Adapter), and external metrics (SQS queue depth, Pub/Sub messages). Multiple metrics can be combined with the largest target determining the replica count
C) HPA replaces the need for Deployment replicas — it's the only way to set pod counts
D) HPA scales cluster nodes, not pods — use VPA for pod-level scaling
✅ Correct Answer: B

HPA formula: `desiredReplicas = ceil[currentReplicas * (currentMetricValue / desiredMetricValue)]`. For anihpj-api: if current CPU is 80% and target is 50%, HPA scales up by 80/50 = 1.6x. Custom metrics enable request-driven scaling: scale based on HTTP request rate or Celery queue depth — much more relevant than CPU for API services.

HPA — Scaling Triggers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Resource:  CPU > 70% → scale up (standard)                            │
  │  Pods:      requests_per_second > 1000 → scale up (custom metric)      │
  │  Object:    SQS queue depth > 500 → scale up (external metric)         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Example: anihpj-api HPA                                                │
  │  minReplicas: 2, maxReplicas: 10                                       │
  │  metrics:                                                               │
  │  - type: Resource, resource: cpu, target: 50% utilization              │
  │  - type: Pods, pods: http_requests_per_second, target: 500             │
  │  → Scale based on whichever metric demands more replicas               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Why not A? HPA supports resource, custom, and external metrics. Why not C? HPA adjusts replicas — you still set the base in your Deployment. Why not D? HPA scales pods; Cluster Autoscaler/Karpenter scales nodes.

Observability Q125 of 200
What is the difference between Liveness, Readiness, and Startup probes in Kubernetes?
A) Liveness: "Is my app alive?" — if fails, container is RESTARTED. Readiness: "Is my app ready to serve traffic?" — if fails, pod is REMOVED from Service endpoints. Startup: "Is my app done booting?" — if fails, container is restarted, but with a longer grace period for slow-starting apps. All three prevent routing traffic to broken pods
B) All three probes do the same thing — they're different names for the same health check
C) Liveness checks if the node is alive; Readiness checks if the pod is alive; Startup only runs once at cluster boot
D) Startup probe replaces both Liveness and Readiness — only one probe type is needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

Probes prevent cascading failures. Liveness catches deadlocked apps (restart them). Readiness catches apps that are alive but not ready (database connection not established yet — remove from load balancer). Startup gives slow apps (like Django loading models) extra time before liveness kicks in, preventing premature restarts.

Probe Types
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Startup probe (runs FIRST, during boot):                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  initialDelaySeconds: 10, periodSeconds: 5                       │  │
  │  │  failureThreshold: 30    # Django can take 150s to boot!          │  │
  │  │  → Once Startup succeeds, Liveness + Readiness take over          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Liveness probe (runs AFTER startup):                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  "Is my app deadlocked?" → RESTART container                     │  │
  │  │  ⚠️  Don't check external dependencies! (DB down → restart won't  │  │
  │  │     fix it → cascading failure)                                  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Readiness probe (runs AFTER startup):                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  "Can my app serve traffic?" → REMOVE from Service endpoints     │  │
