  │      resources: ["pods", "deployments"]                                │
  │                                                                         │
  │  - level: Request          ← Log request details (who, what IP)        │
  │    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                                     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  AUDIT LOG ENTRY (JSON):                                                │
  │  {                                                                      │
  │    "stage": "ResponseComplete",                                         │
  │    "level": "RequestResponse",                                          │
  │    "timestamp": "2026-06-14T14:32:01Z",                                │
  │    "user": {"username": "alice", "groups": ["platform-team"]},         │
  │    "sourceIPs": ["10.0.1.5"],                                          │
  │    "verb": "delete",                                                    │
  │    "objectRef": {                                                       │
  │      "resource": "pods",                                                │
  │      "namespace": "anihpj-prod",                                        │
  │      "name": "anihpj-api-7d4f8b9c-x2k9p"                              │
  │    },                                                                   │
  │    "responseStatus": {"code": 200, "status": "Success"},               │
  │    "annotations": {                                                     │
  │      "authorization.k8s.io/decision": "allow",                         │
  │      "authorization.k8s.io/reason": "RBAC: allowed by RoleBinding"     │
  │    }                                                                    │
  │  }                                                                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: configure audit policy to log all Secret access at Metadata level, all DELETE/PATCH operations at RequestResponse level, and all denied requests regardless of resource. Ship audit logs to a dedicated Elasticsearch cluster (separate from app logs). Set up alerts: "someone accessed secrets in anihpj-prod" → Slack #platform-security. Retain audit logs for 1 year minimum for compliance. Audit logs answer the question every security incident starts with: "Who did what, and when?"

Why not A? Audit logs are a specific API server feature, not container stdout. Why not C? Audit logging must be explicitly configured via audit policy and backend — it's off by default. Why not D? Audit policy controls what is logged — you can log successful, failed, or both.

Observability Q151 of 200
What is distributed tracing and why is it essential for debugging microservice architectures?
A) Distributed tracing is a centralized logging system that collects all microservice logs into one place
B) Distributed tracing monitors CPU and memory usage across all nodes in a cluster — it's an alternative to Prometheus
C) Distributed tracing follows a SINGLE request as it travels through multiple microservices — recording each service's processing time and result. A trace is a tree of spans: the root span represents the incoming HTTP request, and child spans represent calls to downstream services (auth, database, Redis, external API). When `/api/jobs` takes 3 seconds, tracing shows: 200ms in auth, 300ms in Redis, 2.3s in PostgreSQL query, 100ms in response serialization — pinpointing the exact bottleneck
D) Distributed tracing is a security tool that traces attacker movements through the network
✅ Correct Answer: C

Without tracing: "The API is slow." With tracing: "The PostgreSQL query `SELECT * FROM jobs WHERE...` takes 2.3s because the index is missing on `created_at`." A trace = a directed acyclic graph of spans. Each span has: operation name, start time, duration, parent span ID, tags (http.method=GET, db.statement=SELECT...), and status (OK/Error). Traces propagate via HTTP headers (traceparent: 00-traceId-spanId-01) so every service automatically passes context downstream. This is the ONLY way to understand request latency in a distributed system with 10+ microservices.

Distributed Trace — Request Journey
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TRACE ID: 4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736                             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  anihpj-web (ROOT SPAN) — 2.8s total                              │  │
  │  │  │                                                                 │  │
  │  │  ├── anihpj-api — 2.6s                                            │  │
  │  │  │   ├── auth-service — 200ms                                     │  │
  │  │  │   │   └── Redis GET session — 5ms                              │  │
  │  │  │   ├── Redis GET job-cache — 10ms                               │  │
  │  │  │   ├── PostgreSQL query — 2.1s ← BOTTLENECK! 🔴                │  │
  │  │  │   │   └── SELECT * FROM jobs WHERE created_at > ...            │  │
  │  │  │   └── JSON serialize — 150ms                                   │  │
  │  │  └── anihpj-web render — 50ms                                     │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Without tracing: "The jobs page is slow"                               │
  │  With tracing: "Missing index on jobs.created_at — create it!"        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: instrument the Django app with OpenTelemetry Python SDK. Every HTTP request becomes a root span. Every DB query becomes a child span. Every Redis command becomes a child span. Export traces to Jaeger or Tempo. When a job listing page loads slowly, open the trace and see exactly which span is the bottleneck. Distributed tracing turns "the system is slow" into "line 42 of jobs/views.py takes 2.1s."

Why not A? Tracing follows request paths across services — logs are individual, unconnected entries. Why not B? Tracing measures latency per service call, not resource usage. Why not D? Tracing is for performance debugging, not security forensics.

Observability Q152 of 200
What is OpenTelemetry (OTel) and why is it the CNCF standard for observability data collection?
A) OpenTelemetry is a proprietary monitoring SaaS platform that replaces Prometheus and Grafana
B) OpenTelemetry is a CNCF incubating project that provides a UNIFIED standard (vendor-neutral, language-agnostic) for generating, collecting, and exporting telemetry data: Traces (span creation, context propagation), Metrics (Counter, Gauge, Histogram), and Logs (structured log correlation). It defines the OTLP protocol for sending data to ANY backend (Jaeger, Tempo, Prometheus, Datadog, Honeycomb). Apps instrument ONCE with OTel SDKs, then switch backends without changing code — no vendor lock-in
C) OpenTelemetry is a distributed tracing tool that only supports Python applications
D) OpenTelemetry is a Kubernetes operator that automatically creates traces for all pods without any application changes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Before OpenTelemetry: every observability vendor had their own SDK. Switching from Jaeger to Tempo meant rewriting instrumentation code. OpenTelemetry solves this: instrument ONCE with OTel SDK, export to ANY backend via OTLP or exporters. The OTel Collector is a separate binary that receives OTLP data, processes it (batch, filter, sample, enrich), and exports to multiple backends simultaneously — traces to Tempo, metrics to Prometheus, logs to Loki, all from one agent. CNCF graduated project status expected soon. It's the USB-C of observability: one standard connector for everything.

OpenTelemetry — Instrument Once, Export Anywhere
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  APPLICATION (anihpj-api)                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  from opentelemetry import trace                                  │  │
  │  │  tracer = trace.get_tracer("anihpj-api")                          │  │
  │  │  with tracer.start_as_current_span("get_jobs"):                   │  │
  │  │      jobs = Job.objects.filter(...)  ← auto-instrumented DB span │  │
  │  │      return JsonResponse(...)                                      │  │
  │  │  # NO backend-specific code!                                       │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │       │ OTLP (gRPC/HTTP)                                                │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  OTel Collector (runs as Deployment or DaemonSet)                 │  │
  │  │  • Receives OTLP from all services                                │  │
  │  │  • Batches, filters, samples, enriches                            │  │
  │  │  • Exports to MULTIPLE backends:                                  │  │
  │  │    → Traces: Jaeger, Tempo, Datadog                               │  │
  │  │    → Metrics: Prometheus, VictoriaMetrics                         │  │
  │  │    → Logs: Loki, Elasticsearch                                    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: add `opentelemetry-instrumentation-django` and `opentelemetry-exporter-otlp` to requirements.txt. Configure the OTel Collector as a Deployment in the monitoring namespace. Export traces to Tempo, metrics to Prometheus. Instrument once with OTel — switch backends without touching application code.

Why not A? OTel is an open standard and collection framework, not a SaaS platform. Why not C? OTel supports Python, Java, Go, Node.js, .NET, Rust, C++, and more. Why not D? OTel requires application instrumentation — it's not automatic (though auto-instrumentation agents help).

Observability Q153 of 200
What is Jaeger and what are its key features for distributed tracing?
A) Jaeger is a CNCF-graduated distributed tracing system (originally Uber). Key features: Trace UI (waterfall view showing span hierarchy and timing), Dependency Graph (visualizes service-to-service call relationships — see that anihpj-api calls auth-service, Redis, and PostgreSQL), Adaptive Sampling (dynamically adjusts sample rate based on traffic to control overhead), and multiple storage backends (Elasticsearch, Cassandra, badger). Jaeger Agent runs as a DaemonSet, Collector processes spans, Query serves the UI
B) Jaeger is a log aggregation tool similar to Loki — it collects and indexes log lines from containers
C) Jaeger is a Kubernetes security tool that traces API server requests for audit compliance
D) Jaeger is a Prometheus-compatible metrics database optimized for long-term storage
✅ Correct Answer: A

Jaeger was the first CNCF-graduated tracing project. Architecture: Jaeger Client (embedded in your app, sends spans to Agent) → Jaeger Agent (DaemonSet, receives UDP spans and batches them) → Jaeger Collector (validates, indexes, stores) → Storage (Elasticsearch/Cassandra) → Jaeger Query (UI + API). The Dependency Graph is particularly valuable: it automatically builds a map of which services call which — critical for understanding microservice architecture in large platforms. Adaptive sampling keeps overhead low: sample 100% of errors, 10% of successful requests.

Jaeger Architecture & Dependency Graph
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  JAEGER ARCHITECTURE                                                     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐  │
  │  │ App+Jaeger│───▶│  Jaeger  │───▶│ Collector│───▶│ Elasticsearch    │  │
  │  │ Client    │    │  Agent   │    │ (process,│    │ / Cassandra      │  │
  │  │ (in-pod)  │    │(DaemonSet│    │  index)  │    │                  │  │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └────────┬─────────┘  │
  │                                                           │            │
  │                                                    ┌──────▼──────┐    │
  │                                                    │ Jaeger Query│    │
  │                                                    │ (UI + API)  │    │
  │                                                    └─────────────┘    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  DEPENDENCY GRAPH (auto-discovered):                                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │       anihpj-web ◀──────────────────────────────┐                      │
  │           │                                      │                      │
  │           ▼                                      │                      │
  │       anihpj-api ──────▶ auth-service ──────────┘                      │
  │           │                                                             │
  │           ├──────▶ Redis                                                │
  │           └──────▶ PostgreSQL                                           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy Jaeger via the Jaeger Operator. Use Elasticsearch as the storage backend (shared with EFK stack). The Dependency Graph auto-discovers that anihpj-web → anihpj-api → (auth-service, Redis, PostgreSQL). Set adaptive sampling: 100% of errored traces, 10% of successful ones. Jaeger shows you not just what's slow, but how your services are connected.

Why not B? Jaeger is for traces, not logs — use Loki or ELK for logs. Why not C? Falco handles runtime security; Jaeger is for performance tracing. Why not D? Thanos/Cortex handle long-term metrics; Jaeger is a tracing system.

Observability Q154 of 200
What is Grafana Tempo and how does its architecture differ fundamentally from Jaeger?
A) Tempo is identical to Jaeger — it's just a rebranded version with a different UI
B) Tempo requires Elasticsearch as a mandatory dependency — it cannot use object storage
C) Tempo is a metrics database that competes with Prometheus, not a tracing system
D) Tempo uses object storage (S3/GCS) with NO indexing — traces are stored as flat files organized by trace ID. Unlike Jaeger (which indexes every span for fast search), Tempo relies on external metadata (from Loki logs or Prometheus metrics) to discover trace IDs, then fetches the full trace from object storage. This makes Tempo massively cheaper to operate (no index management, no expensive storage) but requires integration with Loki/metrics to FIND traces to view
✅ Correct Answer: D

Tempo's philosophy: "Don't index traces. Use external signals to find them." You discover traces through: (1) Exemplars — Prometheus metrics link to example traces ("p99 latency spike at 14:32 — click to see the trace"), (2) Logs — Loki log entries include trace_id; click to jump to the full trace in Tempo, (3) Service graph — Tempo builds a service dependency map from span data. Because Tempo doesn't index, storage costs are 10-50x cheaper than Jaeger with Elasticsearch. Perfect for platforms that already run Grafana + Loki + Prometheus — Tempo completes the "Grafana observability trinity."

Tempo — Object Storage, No Index
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  JAEGER approach:              TEMPO approach:                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ Index EVERY span:     │      │ NO index — store flat in S3:     │    │
  │  │ service=anihpj-api    │      │ s3://tempo-traces/               │    │
  │  │ operation=get_jobs    │      │   4bf92f35/ (trace ID prefix)    │    │
  │  │ duration>1s           │      │     4bf92f35...json               │    │
  │  │ http.status=500       │      │                                   │    │
  │  │                        │      │ Find traces via:                  │    │
  │  │ 100GB traces → 150GB  │      │ • Prometheus exemplars            │    │
  │  │ (index + data)        │      │ • Loki log trace_id links         │    │
  │  │ Fast search, $$$       │      │ • Service graph auto-build        │    │
  │  └──────────────────────┘      │                                   │    │
  │                                 │ 100GB traces → 30GB data          │    │
  │  WHEN TO USE:                  │ Cheap, Grafana-native              │    │
  │  • Need instant span search    └──────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │  • No existing Grafana stack     WHEN TO USE:                           │
  │  • Budget is not a concern       • Already use Grafana + Loki          │
  │                                  • Want minimal ops overhead            │
  │                                  • Cost-sensitive                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy Tempo with S3-compatible storage (MinIO). Configure the OTel Collector to export traces to Tempo via OTLP. In Grafana, configure Tempo as a data source. When investigating a slow request in Loki (`{app="anihpj-api"} |= "slow"`), the trace_id in the log line becomes a clickable link to the full trace in Tempo. Tempo + Loki + Prometheus + Grafana: the open-source observability quad.

Why not A? Tempo has a fundamentally different architecture — no indexing, object-storage-native. Why not B? Tempo uses object storage (S3/GCS/Azure Blob) — no Elasticsearch needed. Why not C? Tempo is a tracing backend, not a metrics database.

Observability Q155 of 200
What is trace context propagation and how does the W3C Trace Context standard (traceparent header) work?
A) Trace context propagation passes trace information between services via HTTP headers. The W3C `traceparent` header format is: `00-{trace_id}-{span_id}-{trace_flags}`. When anihpj-web receives a request, it generates a trace_id. When it calls anihpj-api, it adds `traceparent: 00-4bf92f35...-a3ce929d...-01` to the HTTP request. anihpj-api extracts this header, creates its span as a CHILD of the incoming span, and passes traceparent to downstream calls (auth, DB, Redis). This links all spans into one trace across service boundaries
B) Trace context is stored in a shared database that all services query to link their spans
C) Trace context propagation requires all services to be in the same Kubernetes namespace — it doesn't work across namespaces
D) The W3C Trace Context uses cookies to propagate trace IDs — it only works for browser-based applications
✅ Correct Answer: A

Trace context is the glue that makes distributed tracing work. Without propagation, each service creates its own independent trace — you get 5 separate traces instead of 1 end-to-end trace. The W3C standard ensures interoperability: anihpj-web (Python/OTel) calls anihpj-api (Python/OTel) which calls auth-service (Go/OTel) which calls Redis — all linked by the same traceparent header. The `tracestate` header carries vendor-specific data alongside traceparent. Key rule: every service MUST forward traceparent to downstream calls. If a service doesn't propagate context, the trace breaks into fragments.

W3C Trace Context Propagation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  traceparent: 00-4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736-a3ce929d0e0e4736-01  │
  │                │  │                                  │                  │
  │                │  │                                  └─ trace-flags     │
  │                │  │                                     (01=sampled)    │
  │                │  └─ parent-span-id                                     │
  │                └─ trace-id (same across ALL services!)                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  FLOW:                                                                  │
  │  User → anihpj-web                                                    │
  │    │  trace_id=4bf92f35, span_id=aaa                                   │
  │    │  traceparent: 00-4bf92f35-aaa-01                                  │
  │    ▼                                                                    │
  │  anihpj-api                                                            │
  │    │  trace_id=4bf92f35, span_id=bbb, parent_span_id=aaa               │
  │    │  traceparent: 00-4bf92f35-bbb-01                                  │
  │    ▼                                                                    │
  │  PostgreSQL                                                            │
  │       trace_id=4bf92f35, span_id=ccc, parent_span_id=bbb               │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ ALL spans share the SAME trace_id → linked into ONE trace           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: the Django app's OTel middleware automatically extracts traceparent from incoming requests and injects it into outgoing HTTP calls, DB queries, and Redis commands. No manual header handling needed — the OTel SDK handles all context propagation. One trace ID follows the request from browser → web → API → database → response.

Why not B? A shared DB would add latency to every request — headers are zero-overhead. Why not C? HTTP headers work across any network boundary — namespaces, clusters, clouds. Why not D? It uses HTTP headers, not cookies — works for any HTTP-based communication.

Observability Q156 of 200
What is trace sampling and when should you use head-based vs tail-based vs adaptive sampling?
A) Sampling means collecting traces only during business hours — it's a scheduling feature
B) Head-based sampling decides at request START (before processing): "keep 10% of all requests." Simple, low overhead, but may miss rare errors. Tail-based sampling decides at request END: "keep all traces with errors or latency > 500ms." Captures all anomalies at higher storage cost. Adaptive sampling adjusts the rate dynamically: sample 100% when error rate spikes, drop to 1% during normal operation. Best practice: head-based for normal traffic + tail-based for errors = capture every anomaly without storing everything
C) Sampling is deprecated — modern tracing systems collect 100% of all traces with zero overhead
D) Sampling only applies to metrics collection — traces must always be collected at 100%
✅ Correct Answer: B

At 10,000 requests/second, 100% tracing = 10,000 traces/second = 864M traces/day. That's expensive and noise-heavy. Sampling strategies: Head-based (probabilistic): flip a coin at request start — 10% sample rate means 90% of traces are never created. Simple, stateless, but you might miss the ONE request that errored. Tail-based: the OTel Collector buffers spans, and when a trace completes, decides to keep it based on outcome (error, high latency). Keeps ALL interesting traces but needs buffering infrastructure. Adaptive: dynamically adjusts rate — when error_rate > 1%, sample 100%; when normal, sample 5%. Best of both worlds.

Sampling Strategies
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HEAD-BASED (at request start):                                         │
  │  • 10% sample rate → 90% of requests never traced                       │
  │  • ✅ Simple, low overhead, no buffering                                │
  │  • ❌ Misses rare errors in the 90% unsampled                            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  TAIL-BASED (at request end):                                           │
  │  • Collect ALL spans, buffer briefly                                    │
  │  • Decision: keep if error=true OR duration>500ms                       │
  │  • ✅ Captures EVERY anomaly                                            │
  │  • ❌ Needs buffering, higher storage for span buffer                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ADAPTIVE (dynamic rate):                                               │
  │  • Normal traffic: 5% sample rate                                       │
  │  • Error rate spike: auto-switch to 100%                                │
  │  • ✅ Complete picture during incidents                                 │
  │  • ✅ Low overhead during normal operation                              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: configure the OTel Collector with tail-based sampling: keep 100% of traces with `error=true` or `http.status_code >= 500` or `duration > 1s`. For normal traffic, use 10% head-based sampling. During incident response, manually switch to 100% via a config change. Sample smart: capture every failure, sample enough successes to know what normal looks like.

Why not A? Sampling controls what percentage of traces to keep, not when to collect. Why not C? 100% tracing at high throughput is infeasible — sampling is essential. Why not D? Trace sampling is critical — metrics are aggregations and much cheaper to store at full resolution.

Observability Q157 of 200
What is OpenTelemetry auto-instrumentation and how does it add tracing to applications without code changes?
A) Auto-instrumentation is a Kubernetes admission webhook that injects tracing sidecars into every pod
B) Auto-instrumentation requires rewriting every function in the application to add span creation — it's "auto" in name only
C) Auto-instrumentation uses language-specific agents that monkey-patch or wrap common libraries at runtime. For Python: `opentelemetry-instrument django-admin ...` automatically creates spans for Django HTTP requests, PostgreSQL queries (via psycopg2 wrapper), Redis commands (via redis wrapper), and external HTTP calls (via requests/urllib wrapper). Zero code changes — the agent intercepts library calls and creates spans automatically. Custom spans still require manual instrumentation for business logic
D) Auto-instrumentation only works for Java Spring Boot applications — no other languages are supported
✅ Correct Answer: C

Auto-instrumentation is the "80% solution." Without writing a single line of tracing code, the OTel agent instruments: HTTP frameworks (Django, Flask, Express, Spring), database drivers (psycopg2, pymongo, JDBC), message queues (Kafka, RabbitMQ, Celery), and gRPC/HTTP clients. The agent wraps these libraries at import time, adding span creation before/after each call. Result: you get spans for every HTTP endpoint, DB query, and Redis command — automatically. For business-logic spans ("calculate_match_score", "send_notification_email"), you add manual instrumentation.

Auto-Instrumentation — Python Example
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT auto-instrumentation (manual):                                 │
  │  from opentelemetry import trace                                        │
  │  tracer = trace.get_tracer(__name__)                                     │
  │  @app.route('/api/jobs')                                                │
  │  def get_jobs():                                                        │
  │      with tracer.start_as_current_span("get_jobs"):                     │
  │          with tracer.start_as_current_span("db_query"):                 │
  │              jobs = Job.objects.filter(...)                              │
  │  # Every endpoint, every DB call — MANUAL spans!                        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  WITH auto-instrumentation (ZERO code changes):                         │
  │  $ pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-django                     │
  │  $ pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-psycopg2                   │
  │  $ pip install opentelemetry-instrumentation-redis                      │
  │  $ opentelemetry-instrument \                                           │
  │      --traces_exporter otlp \                                           │
  │      gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application                                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  @app.route('/api/jobs')  ← AUTO-SPAN for HTTP request                 │
  │  def get_jobs():                                                        │
  │      jobs = Job.objects.filter(...)  ← AUTO-SPAN for DB query          │
  │      cache.get('jobs')               ← AUTO-SPAN for Redis             │
  │  # Zero code changes — spans created automatically!                     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: add `opentelemetry-instrumentation-django`, `opentelemetry-instrumentation-psycopg2`, `opentelemetry-instrumentation-redis`, and `opentelemetry-instrumentation-celery` to requirements.txt. Start the app with `opentelemetry-instrument gunicorn anihpj.wsgi`. Instantly get spans for all HTTP endpoints, DB queries, Redis commands, and Celery tasks. Add manual spans for critical business logic paths. Auto-instrumentation gives you 80% observability with 0% code changes.

Why not A? It's a language-level agent, not a K8s webhook — it runs inside the application container. Why not B? It truly requires zero code changes for standard library calls. Why not D? OTel auto-instrumentation supports Python, Java, Node.js, .NET, Go, and more.

Observability Q158 of 200
How do you link traces to logs to create a seamless debugging experience from log entry → full trace → root cause?
A) Traces and logs are fundamentally incompatible — they use different formats and can't be linked
B) You must manually copy trace IDs from the tracing UI and paste them into the log search bar — there's no automated linking
C) Traces automatically include all log lines generated during the request — no separate log system is needed
D) Include `trace_id` and `span_id` in every structured log line. When Loki indexes logs with trace_id as a label or field, you can query `{app="anihpj-api"} | trace_id="4bf92f35..."` to see ALL logs for a single request. Grafana auto-links: click a log line in Loki → "View trace in Tempo" button. Click a span in Tempo → "View logs for this span" button. The trace_id is the bridge between logs and traces
✅ Correct Answer: D

The trace_id is the universal correlation ID. Every log line from a request includes the trace_id. Every span in the trace has the same trace_id. Result: debugging workflow becomes "see ERROR log in Loki → click trace_id → see full trace in Tempo → identify slow DB span → see DB query text → add missing index." Without this link, you'd manually search logs by timestamp and guess which log lines belong to which request. The OTel SDK automatically adds trace_id to Python's logging context — any `logging.info()` call during a traced request inherits the trace_id.

Log-Trace Correlation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STRUCTURED LOG WITH trace_id:                                          │
  │  {"timestamp":"...","level":"ERROR","msg":"DB timeout",                 │
  │   "trace_id":"4bf92f3577b34da6a3ce929d0e0e4736",                       │
  │   "span_id":"ccc123", "duration_ms":2100}                               │
  │                                                                         │
  │  DEBUGGING WORKFLOW:                                                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Grafana Loki:                                                          │
  │  {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR"                                          │
  │  → "DB timeout, trace_id=4bf92f35..., duration_ms=2100"                │
  │     │                                                                   │
  │     │  [Click "View Trace" button → Tempo]                              │
  │     ▼                                                                   │
  │  Grafana Tempo:                                                         │
  │  Trace 4bf92f35:                                                        │
  │    anihpj-api (2.6s)                                                    │
  │      ├── auth-service (200ms) ✅                                        │
  │      ├── Redis GET (10ms) ✅                                            │
  │      └── PostgreSQL SELECT (2.1s) ❌ ← BOTTLENECK                       │
  │          └── SELECT * FROM jobs WHERE created_at > ...                  │
  │     │                                                                   │
  │     │  [View DB span attributes → see the exact query]                  │
  │     ▼                                                                   │
  │  FIX: CREATE INDEX idx_jobs_created_at ON jobs(created_at);            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: configure Django logging to include trace_id and span_id in every JSON log line (using OTel's logging integration). In Grafana, configure the Loki data source to derive a Tempo link field from trace_id. Now: click any log line → "View Trace" → see the full request journey. trace_id is the bridge that turns isolated logs into a complete debugging story.

Why not A? They're designed to be linked — trace_id in logs is the standard pattern. Why not B? Grafana auto-links logs and traces via trace_id — no copying needed. Why not C? Logs and traces are separate data types — traces carry span metadata, logs carry application messages; they complement each other.

Observability Q159 of 200
What is the difference between SLO, SLI, and SLA in platform engineering?
A) SLO is a legal contract with customers; SLI is an internal target; SLA is the metric itself — they're interchangeable in practice
B) SLI (Service Level Indicator) is the MEASUREMENT — e.g., "99.5% of API requests completed in < 200ms over the last 30 days." SLO (Service Level Objective) is the TARGET — e.g., "99.9% of requests must be faster than 200ms." SLA (Service Level Agreement) is the CONTRACT — e.g., "if availability < 99.5%, customer gets 25% refund." SLI = what you measure. SLO = what you aim for. SLA = what you promise (with consequences for breaking)
C) SLO and SLA are the same thing — both are legal agreements with customers about uptime guarantees
D) SLI is only for network latency; SLO is only for disk I/O; SLA only applies to cloud providers, not internal platforms
✅ Correct Answer: B

Every platform team must define SLOs. Without them, you can't prioritize: should you fix a bug or add a feature? SLOs answer: "Is the platform reliable enough?" Error budget = 100% - SLO. If SLO is 99.9% availability, you have 0.1% error budget (~43 minutes/month of acceptable downtime). Use error budget to decide: if you've burned < 50% of budget, ship features. If > 80% burned, freeze all deploys and fix reliability. This replaces subjective "is it stable enough?" debates with data-driven decisions.

SLI → SLO → SLA Hierarchy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLI (Service Level Indicator) — THE MEASUREMENT                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Availability: 100 * (successful_requests / total_requests)       │  │
  │  │  Latency: histogram_quantile(0.99, http_request_duration_seconds)│  │
  │  │  Error rate: rate(http_errors_total[5m]) / rate(http_total[5m])  │  │
  │  │  These are PromQL queries — objective, measurable, automatic     │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  SLO (Service Level Objective) — THE TARGET                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Availability SLO: 99.9% (error budget = 0.1% = 43min/month)     │  │
  │  │  Latency SLO: p99 < 200ms (error budget: 1% over 200ms)          │  │
  │  │  Error rate SLO: < 0.1% of all requests                          │  │
  │  │  Target set by platform team based on user expectations          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  SLA (Service Level Agreement) — THE CONTRACT                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  "Platform will be 99.5% available. If not, 10% credit."         │  │
  │  │  SLA is STRICTER than SLO (you promise less than you aim for)    │  │
  │  │  SLA: 99.5% ← SLO: 99.9% ← you have 0.4% buffer                 │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: define SLOs — API availability 99.9%, p99 latency < 200ms, error rate < 0.1%. Track SLIs in Prometheus. Create a Grafana dashboard showing SLO compliance and error budget burn rate. When error budget burn rate exceeds 10%/hour, page the on-call. SLOs turn "the platform feels slow" into "we've burned 82% of our latency error budget — stop features, fix performance."

Why not A? SLA is a contract with penalties; SLO is an internal target; SLI is the metric. Why not C? SLO is internal, SLA is contractual — they serve different purposes. Why not D? SLI/SLO/SLA apply to any measurable service quality: availability, latency, throughput, freshness, correctness.

Observability Q160 of 200
What is an error budget and how does it drive platform decision-making?
A) Error budget is the amount of money allocated for fixing bugs — it's a financial term unrelated to SLOs
B) Error budget is a fixed amount of downtime (e.g., 1 hour per year) that the platform is allowed regardless of actual reliability
C) Error budget = 100% - SLO target. For 99.9% availability SLO, error budget = 0.1% = ~43 minutes/month of acceptable downtime. When error budget is healthy (> 50% remaining): teams can ship features, take risks, deploy on Friday. When error budget is critically low (< 20% remaining): freeze all deployments, redirect ALL engineering effort to reliability improvements. Error budget makes the "features vs stability" trade-off objective and data-driven
D) Error budget is only relevant for external cloud providers like AWS — internal platforms don't need it
✅ Correct Answer: C

Error budget is the single most powerful concept in platform reliability. It replaces endless debates ("Can we deploy on Friday?" "Is it safe to add this feature?") with a single number. Burn rate matters more than absolute budget: burning 2% of error budget in 1 hour is a CRITICAL emergency (you'll exhaust the monthly budget in 5 hours). Burning 10% over 3 weeks is normal. Alert on burn rate: "error budget burn rate > 10%/hour" → page on-call immediately. "> 2%/hour" → warn. This catches catastrophic failures fast while tolerating slow, chronic issues.

Error Budget — Decision Framework
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SLO: 99.9% availability → Error Budget: 0.1% = 43 min/month           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  100% ████████████████████████████████████████████████████████    │  │
  │  │        │                                                          │  │
  │  │   80%  │  ✅ GREEN ZONE — Ship features freely                    │  │
  │  │        │  • Deploy on Friday? Yes!                                │  │
  │  │        │  • Risky refactor? Go for it.                            │  │
  │  │        │  • Engineering velocity: MAXIMUM                         │  │
  │  │   50%  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────    │  │
  │  │        │  🟡 YELLOW ZONE — Caution, prioritize carefully          │  │
  │  │        │  • Deploy on Friday? Only low-risk changes.              │  │
  │  │   20%  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────    │  │
  │  │        │  🔴 RED ZONE — FREEZE. All hands on reliability.         │  │
  │  │        │  • No features. No non-critical deploys.                 │  │
  │  │        │  • Engineering velocity: ZERO on features.               │  │
  │  │    0%  │  SLO VIOLATED — postmortem, fix root cause.             │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a Grafana dashboard showing error budget remaining for availability, latency, and error rate SLOs. Configure alerts on burn rate. When error budget is healthy, ship new job board features freely. When in the red zone, the entire platform team focuses on reliability. Error budget turns "I think we should be careful" into "we have X% budget remaining — here's what we can do."

Why not A? It's a reliability concept measured in time, not a financial budget. Why not B? It's derived from SLO, not an arbitrary fixed amount. Why not D? Internal platforms benefit enormously from error budgets — it manages the tension between the platform team and developer users.

Observability Q161 of 200
What are the DORA Metrics and how do they measure platform and delivery performance?
A) DORA (DevOps Research and Assessment) defines four key metrics: Deploy Frequency (how often code reaches production — elite: multiple times/day), Lead Time for Changes (time from commit to production — elite: < 1 hour), Mean Time to Restore/Recover — MTTR (time to fix a production failure — elite: < 1 hour), Change Failure Rate (percentage of deploys causing failure — elite: 0-15%). These metrics objectively measure delivery performance and are the industry standard for assessing DevOps maturity
B) DORA metrics measure only infrastructure costs — CPU utilization, memory efficiency, and storage spending per deployment
C) DORA is a security framework that measures vulnerability remediation time and patch compliance rates
D) DORA metrics are deprecated — they've been replaced by the SPACE framework for measuring developer productivity
✅ Correct Answer: A

DORA metrics are the yardstick for platform engineering success. The platform exists to help developers ship software faster and more safely. If your platform is "working" but deploy frequency is once per week and MTTR is 4 hours, the platform is FAILING its users. Elite performers deploy multiple times per day with < 1 hour lead time and < 1 hour MTTR. The platform enables this through: GitOps automation (Argo CD auto-sync), progressive delivery (canary → automated promotion), comprehensive observability (instant root cause identification), and self-service (no ticket queue slowing deploys).

DORA Metrics — Platform Performance
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  METRIC              │  ELITE         │  HIGH       │  MEDIUM    │ LOW  │
  │  ────────────────────┼────────────────┼─────────────┼────────────┼──────│
  │  Deploy Frequency    │  On-demand     │  Daily-     │  Weekly-   │ Month│
  │                      │  (multiple/day)│  Weekly     │  Monthly   │ ly   │
  │  Lead Time for       │  < 1 hour      │  1 day-     │  1 week-   │ 1-6  │
  │  Changes             │                │  1 week     │  1 month   │ months│
  │  MTTR (recovery)     │  < 1 hour      │  < 1 day    │  < 1 day   │ 1 wk │
  │  Change Failure Rate │  0-15%         │  16-30%     │  16-30%    │ >30% │
  │                                                                         │
  │  PLATFORM ENABLERS for elite performance:                               │
  │  • Argo CD auto-sync → instant deploys                                  │
  │  • Canary + automated rollback → low change failure rate                │
  │  • Distributed tracing → fast MTTR (find bottleneck in seconds)        │
  │  • Self-service provisioning → no ticket queue for infrastructure      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: measure DORA metrics. Deploy frequency = count of Argo CD syncs to prod/day. Lead time = time from git push to Argo CD sync complete. MTTR = time from PagerDuty alert to incident resolved. Change failure rate = deploys that triggered rollback / total deploys. Display on the platform team's dashboard. DORA metrics tell you if your platform is actually helping developers ship faster.

Why not B? DORA focuses on delivery performance, not infrastructure costs. Why not C? DORA is about delivery, not security — though security practices correlate with elite performance. Why not D? DORA is the current industry standard; SPACE complements it for developer experience.

Observability Q162 of 200
What platform efficiency metrics should a platform engineering team track to measure success beyond DORA?
A) Only infrastructure costs matter — all other metrics are vanity
B) Platform efficiency equals the number of Kubernetes clusters managed — more clusters = more efficient
C) Platform efficiency is measured by how many tickets the platform team closes per week
D) Time-to-deploy (from PR merge to production — should be < 15 minutes with GitOps), ticket deflection rate (% of infrastructure requests handled via self-service instead of tickets — target > 80%), developer onboarding time (time from new hire → first production deploy — target < 1 day), platform adoption (% of teams using the platform vs doing their own thing), and cost per deployment ($ per successful production deploy). The goal: make developers MORE productive and LESS dependent on the platform team
✅ Correct Answer: D

Ticket deflection is the ultimate platform metric. If developers still file tickets for "I need a database" or "please create a namespace for my team," the platform is NOT providing self-service. Every ticket is a platform failure. Time-to-deploy measures the pipeline: PR merged → CI passes → image pushed → Argo CD syncs → healthy. If this takes > 15 minutes, your pipeline has bottlenecks. Developer onboarding time: can a new team member deploy to production on day 1? If not, your platform's golden paths are too narrow.

  │  │ │ Teams DIY:   3   │ │  │ │ Trending: ▂▃▂▂▂ (stable)          │ │  │

For anihpj/jobpost: track time-to-deploy (GitHub Actions duration + Argo CD sync time), ticket deflection (% of "create DB" requests handled via Crossplane DatabaseClaim CRD vs Jira tickets), onboarding time (time from laptop setup → first kubectl apply), and cost per deploy. Display on the platform team's Backstage homepage. If 3 developers file Jira tickets for databases this week, the platform team failed 3 times — build a self-service CRD this sprint.

Why not A? Cost is one metric, but developer productivity impact is more important. Why not B? More clusters can mean more overhead — efficiency is about outcomes, not cluster count. Why not C? Ticket closure is an anti-goal — the goal is zero tickets through self-service.

Observability Q163 of 200
What are alerting best practices for platform engineering — avoiding alert fatigue while catching real problems?
A) Alert on everything — more alerts mean better coverage. Developers can filter what matters
B) Alert on SYMPTOMS, not causes: "API error rate > 1% for 5 minutes" (symptom, actionable) vs "CPU > 80%" (cause, maybe fine). PAGE on SLO burn rate > 10%/hour (requires immediate human response). TICKET on SLO burn rate > 2%/hour (can wait until morning). DASHBOARD for informational metrics. Every alert must require human action — if the alert doesn't change what you DO, delete it. This prevents the #1 observability failure: alert fatigue where on-call ignores ALL alerts
C) Only alert on complete system outages — partial degradations don't need alerts because users will report them
D) Send all alerts to a shared Slack channel — no need for PagerDuty or on-call rotations
✅ Correct Answer: B

The cardinal rule: "Never alert on something you wouldn't wake someone up at 3 AM for." Alert fatigue is the #1 observability failure mode. When on-call gets 50 alerts/night and 48 are false positives, they learn to ignore ALL alerts — including the 2 real ones. Symptom-based alerting: "users are experiencing errors" (page!) vs "CPU is high" (maybe fine — batch job might be running). SLO-based alerting: page when error budget is burning fast, not on every minor blip. Use `for: 5m` on alerts to filter transient spikes. Multi-window alerting: alert if error rate > 1% for 5m AND > 0.5% for 1h — catches both acute and chronic issues.

Alert Severity Pyramid
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🔴 PAGE (wake someone up)                             │
  │                    • SLO burn rate > 10%/hour                            │
  │                    • User-facing error rate > 5% for 5 min              │
  │                    • Database unavailable                                │
  │                    • TLS cert expiring in < 24 hours                    │
  │                    → PagerDuty, 24/7, immediate response                │
  │                                                                         │
  │               🟡 TICKET (investigate during work hours)                 │
  │               • SLO burn rate > 2%/hour                                 │
  │               • Disk > 80% (not yet critical)                          │
  │               • Slow memory leak detected                               │
  │               → Jira/ServiceNow, next business day                     │
  │                                                                         │
  │          🟢 DASHBOARD (watch, don't alert)                              │
  │          • CPU utilization trends                                       │
  │          • Weekly deploy frequency                                      │
  │          • Cost per namespace                                           │
  │          → Grafana dashboard, no alert                                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: set up multi-window SLO alerts. Page if error budget burn rate > 10%/hour. Ticket if > 2%/hour. Set `for: 5m` on all page-level alerts. Every quarter, review all alerts and delete any that haven't fired or resulted in action. The best alert is the one you never need to fire. The second best is the one that fires ONCE and the root cause gets permanently fixed.

Why not A? Over-alerting causes alert fatigue — more alerts = less effective response. Why not C? Partial degradations are early warnings — catch them before they become complete outages. Why not D? Slack is not an alerting system — alerts get lost in channel noise.

Observability Q164 of 200
What is an effective on-call rotation and incident response practice for platform teams?
A) On-call rotation: team members rotate weekly, with a secondary as backup. Every alert has a RUNBOOK (step-by-step troubleshooting guide). Blameless postmortems after every incident: "What happened? What did we learn? What will we change?" — focus on systems and processes, NOT individuals. Runbooks evolve: after each incident, update the runbook so the NEXT on-call person resolves the same issue in 5 minutes instead of 2 hours. Escalation path: primary → secondary → engineering manager → VP
B) On-call is only for senior engineers — junior engineers should never be on-call because they lack experience
C) The platform team doesn't need on-call — developers who use the platform handle their own incidents
D) On-call means being available by email during business hours — there's no need for 24/7 coverage or PagerDuty
✅ Correct Answer: A

The platform team owns the platform's reliability 24/7. Runbooks are the key to sustainable on-call: when an alert fires, the on-call engineer opens the runbook and follows step-by-step instructions. "Check Prometheus dashboard X. If metric Y > threshold, run `kubectl logs -l app=anihpj-api --tail=100`. If DB connection errors, check RDS console for CPU utilization." Blameless postmortems: the goal is learning, not punishment. "The deployment pipeline didn't catch the misconfigured environment variable" → add validation to the pipeline. NOT "Alice forgot to set the variable." Over time, runbooks and automation reduce MTTR from hours to minutes.

On-Call & Incident Response Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ALERT FIRES (PagerDuty) → On-call engineer acknowledges              │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  OPEN RUNBOOK: "anihpj-api High Error Rate"                            │
  │  Step 1: Check Grafana dashboard → confirm error rate spike            │
  │  Step 2: Check Loki logs → {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR"              │
  │  Step 3: Check Tempo traces → find slowest span                        │
  │  Step 4: If DB errors → check PostgreSQL metrics                       │
  │  Step 5: If recent deploy → consider rollback                          │
  │  Step 6: If degraded > 15min → escalate to secondary                   │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  MITIGATE → COMMUNICATE (Slack #incidents) → RESOLVE                   │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  BLAMELESS POSTMORTEM (within 24-48 hours):                             │
  │  • Timeline: What happened and when?                                    │
  │  • Root cause: What system/process failed?                              │
  │  • Action items: What will we change? (with owners + deadlines)        │
  │  • Update runbook with new knowledge                                    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create runbooks for the top 5 incident types (DB connection failure, high API latency, Redis unavailable, cert expiry, pod CrashLoopBackOff). Rotate on-call weekly between all platform team members. After every incident, hold a 30-minute blameless postmortem and update the runbook. Runbooks turn every incident into a learning opportunity for the whole team.

Why not B? Everyone should participate in on-call — it builds empathy and system knowledge. Pair juniors with seniors initially. Why not C? The platform is critical infrastructure — the platform team owns its reliability. Why not D? Platform incidents happen at 3 AM — 24/7 coverage is essential for production platforms.

Observability Q165 of 200
What is the incident lifecycle and what are the key stages from detection to prevention?
A) Incident lifecycle = just fix it and move on — documenting incidents slows down the team
B) Incidents follow a simple 2-step process: detect the problem → restart the pod. That handles 95% of issues
C) Detect (monitoring alert or user report) → Respond (acknowledge, assess severity, declare incident) → Mitigate (stop the bleeding — rollback, scale up, failover — do NOT root-cause yet) → Resolve (service restored, verify with monitoring) → Postmortem (blameless analysis: what happened, why, how to prevent) → Remediate (implement action items, update runbooks, add tests/monitoring). The key insight: MITIGATE first, ROOT-CAUSE later. Restoring service comes before understanding why it broke
D) The incident lifecycle only applies to security incidents — performance issues don't follow this process
✅ Correct Answer: C

The #1 incident response mistake: trying to find the root cause BEFORE restoring service. Anihpj-api is returning 500 errors. Don't spend 45 minutes analyzing logs to find WHY. ROLL BACK to the last known-good deployment first (1 minute). NOW investigate why the new deployment failed. Mitigation and root-cause are separate phases. The postmortem must be blameless — "The CI pipeline allowed a deployment with missing environment variables" → add validation. NOT "Bob forgot to set DB_PASSWORD." Action items must have owners and deadlines; track them to completion.

Incident Lifecycle
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔔 DETECT          → Alert fires: "anihpj-api error rate > 5%"        │
  │  👋 RESPOND          → On-call acknowledges within 5 min                │
  │                       → Assess severity: "Users can't post jobs"        │
  │                       → Declare: #INC-042, Slack #incidents            │
  │  🩹 MITIGATE         → STOP THE BLEEDING (don't root-cause yet!)       │
  │                       → Rollback to v2.0.3 (1 min)                      │
  │                       → Scale replicas up if overloaded (30 sec)        │
  │                       → Failover to standby DB if primary down (2 min)  │
  │  ✅ RESOLVE          → Verify: error rate back to 0.1%                  │
  │                       → Communicate: "Service restored at 14:45"       │
  │  📝 POSTMORTEM       → Blameless: what system/process failed?          │
  │                       → Timeline of events with timestamps              │
  │                       → Action items: 1) Add env var validation to CI   │
  │                                       2) Add canary analysis step       │
  │  🔧 REMEDIATE        → Implement action items by deadline               │
  │                       → Update runbook with new knowledge               │
  │                       → Verify fix with chaos engineering test          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: pre-configure rollback commands in Argo CD (`argocd app rollback anihpj-prod`). Practice incident response with game days — simulate "database down" and time how fast the team can failover. After every incident, hold a 30-minute postmortem and track action items in Jira. Mitigate first, understand later. Blame the process, not the person.

Why not A? Undocumented incidents repeat — postmortems prevent recurrence. Why not B? "Just restart" doesn't prevent the next occurrence; root cause analysis does. Why not D? The lifecycle applies to ALL incidents: performance, availability, security, data loss.

Observability Q166 of 200
What is the M.O.R.E platform troubleshooting framework?
A) MORE is a Kubernetes debugging tool that replaces kubectl describe and kubectl logs with a unified interface
B) MORE stands for "Manual Operations Required Everywhere" — it means always fixing problems manually instead of automating
C) MORE is a proprietary monitoring SaaS that performs automatic root cause analysis using AI
D) Measure (collect data: metrics, logs, traces, events — "what changed?"), Observe (look for patterns, anomalies, correlations — "what's different from normal?"), Reason (form hypotheses based on platform knowledge, test each — "if it's DNS, pods can't resolve services"), Execute (apply the fix, verify, and document). M.O.R.E is a structured diagnostic approach that replaces "randomly try things until it works" with systematic troubleshooting
✅ Correct Answer: D

The M.O.R.E framework prevents the most common troubleshooting anti-pattern: "randomly kubectl delete pod, restart the deployment, check if it works, repeat." Instead: Measure first — `kubectl describe pod`, `kubectl logs`, check Prometheus metrics, check Loki logs, check Tempo traces. Observe — compare current state to known-good baseline. Was there a recent deploy? Did CPU spike at 14:30? Are only pods on node-3 failing? Reason — form hypotheses based on platform architecture knowledge. "If only node-3 pods fail, maybe the CNI on node-3 is broken." Execute — apply the fix surgically, verify, document.

M.O.R.E Troubleshooting Framework
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  M — MEASURE (collect data — NO guessing!)                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ kubectl describe pod anihpj-api-xxx | grep -A20 Events            │  │
  │  │ kubectl logs anihpj-api-xxx --tail=100                            │  │
  │  │ Check Grafana: CPU, memory, error rate, latency for last 30 min   │  │
  │  │ Check Loki: {app="anihpj-api"} |= "ERROR" (last 30 min)         │  │
  │  │ Check Argo CD: was there a recent sync?                           │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  O — OBSERVE (what's different from normal?)                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ • Error rate spiked at 14:32 — exactly when Argo CD synced        │  │
  │  │ • Only pods on node-3 are failing                                 │  │
  │  │ • Logs show "connection refused" to PostgreSQL                    │  │
  │  │ • PostgreSQL metrics show max_connections reached                 │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  R — REASON (form and test hypotheses)                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Hypothesis 1: New deploy changed connection pool settings          │  │
  │  │ Test: Check diff between current and previous deployment YAML    │  │
  │  │ Hypothesis 2: Traffic spike exhausted DB connections              │  │
  │  │ Test: Check request rate before vs after 14:32                   │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  E — EXECUTE (fix, verify, document)                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Fix: Rollback to previous deployment                              │  │
  │  │ Verify: Error rate returns to normal, pods healthy                │  │
  │  │ Document: Update runbook, create postmortem, add monitoring       │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: train every platform team member on the M.O.R.E framework. Create a troubleshooting checklist for the top 10 failure modes following the M.O.R.E structure. When an incident occurs, the on-call engineer follows the checklist instead of panicking. M.O.R.E replaces "try random things" with "follow the evidence."

Why not A? MORE is a methodology, not a tool. Why not B? It's the opposite — structured troubleshooting to find the right fix. Why not C? MORE is a mental framework anyone can apply, not SaaS.

Observability Q167 of 200
What are the most common Kubernetes platform failure modes that every platform engineer must know how to diagnose?
A) Kubernetes is self-healing — failures are rare and don't require manual diagnosis
B) Control plane failures (etcd quorum loss, API server unavailable — kubectl returns "connection refused"), CNI/Networking failures (pods can't communicate, CoreDNS unavailable → service discovery broken), Storage failures (PV/PVC not binding — CSI driver down, StorageClass missing), Scheduling failures (pods stuck Pending — insufficient resources, nodeSelector/affinity unsatisfiable, taints blocking), and CrashLoopBackOff (app fails to start — wrong command, missing ConfigMap, OOMKilled). These five categories cover 80%+ of platform incidents
C) The only real failure mode is running out of disk space — everything else is handled automatically
D) Platform failures only happen on cloud providers — on-premise Kubernetes never has issues
✅ Correct Answer: B

Every platform engineer must internalize these five failure domains. Control plane: `kubectl get nodes` returns "connection refused" → check if API server static pod is running on control plane node → check etcd health via `etcdctl endpoint health`. CNI: pods in different nodes can't communicate → check if Calico/Cilium/Flannel DaemonSet pods are running on all nodes. Storage: PVC stuck Pending → `kubectl describe pvc` → "no persistent volumes available" → check StorageClass, CSI driver pods. Scheduling: pod stuck Pending → `kubectl describe pod` → check Events for "0/3 nodes are available: 3 insufficient cpu." CrashLoopBackOff → `kubectl logs` → app error → fix config, rebuild, redeploy.

Top 5 Platform Failure Modes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  1️⃣  CONTROL PLANE FAILURE                                             │
  │     Symptom: kubectl returns "connection refused"                       │
  │     Check: kubectl get nodes (from another context), etcdctl health    │
  │     Fix: Restart kubelet, restore etcd from snapshot if quorum lost    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  2️⃣  CNI / NETWORKING FAILURE                                          │
  │     Symptom: Pod A can't reach Pod B, DNS resolution fails             │
  │     Check: kubectl -n kube-system get pods | grep calico/cilium       │
  │     Fix: Restart CNI DaemonSet pods, check CoreDNS pods               │
  │                                                                         │
  │  3️⃣  STORAGE FAILURE                                                   │
  │     Symptom: PVC stuck Pending, pod can't start                        │
  │     Check: kubectl describe pvc, kubectl get sc, CSI driver pods      │
  │     Fix: Create StorageClass, deploy CSI driver, resize PVC           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  4️⃣  SCHEDULING FAILURE                                                │
  │     Symptom: Pod stuck in Pending state                                │
  │     Check: kubectl describe pod → Events section                       │
  │     Fix: Add resources, remove nodeSelector, add tolerations          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  5️⃣  APPLICATION CRASH (CrashLoopBackOff)                              │
  │     Symptom: Pod restarts repeatedly, STATUS: CrashLoopBackOff         │
  │     Check: kubectl logs  --previous, kubectl describe pod        │
  │     Fix: Fix app config, add missing ConfigMap/Secret, increase memory│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a "Failure Mode Reference Card" pinned in the platform team's Slack. For each of the 5 categories, list the top 3 symptoms, diagnostic commands, and fixes. When on-call is paged at 3 AM, they don't need to think — they follow the reference card. 80% of incidents are caused by 5 failure modes. Know them cold.

Why not A? Kubernetes automates recovery from SOME failures (restarting crashed pods) but can't fix control plane issues, CNI failures, or storage problems. Why not C? Disk pressure is one of many failures — networking and control plane issues are equally common. Why not D? Platform failures are universal — on-prem and cloud both experience all five categories.

Observability Q168 of 200
What is a platform health check and what components should be verified regularly?
A) A platform health check is a systematic verification of all critical platform components: etcd health (quorum, disk, response time), control plane (API server, scheduler, controller-manager all Running), nodes (all Ready, no disk/memory pressure), CoreDNS (resolution works for internal + external names), CNI (pod-to-pod communication across nodes), storage (CSI drivers Running, PVs bound), ingress (responds to HTTP probes from external), and platform services (Argo CD, Prometheus, Grafana, Kyverno all healthy). Automate with a health-check CronJob or use kube-bench/kube-hunter
B) A health check means running `kubectl get pods --all-namespaces` and confirming all pods are Running
C) Platform health checks are only needed after incidents — healthy platforms don't need regular checks
D) Cloud providers handle all platform health checks — the platform team doesn't need to verify anything
✅ Correct Answer: A

A platform health check catches problems BEFORE users notice. Run it daily as a CronJob or as a Grafana dashboard showing all health indicators in one view. Key checks: etcd — `etcdctl endpoint health` (all members healthy), `etcdctl endpoint status` (leader exists, no excessive db size). Control plane — all static pods in kube-system Running. CoreDNS — `dig anihpj-api.anihpj-prod.svc.cluster.local` must resolve. CNI — create a test pod on each node, verify pod-to-pod communication. Ingress — curl the external health endpoint. Automate ALL checks and alert if any fail.

Platform Health Check Checklist
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☐ ETCD: etcdctl endpoint health → all healthy                         │
  │  ☐ API SERVER: kubectl get --raw /healthz → ok                         │
  │  ☐ NODES: kubectl get nodes → all Ready, no conditions                 │
  │  ☐ CoreDNS: nslookup kubernetes.default → resolves                     │
  │  ☐ CNI: Test pod ping between nodes → succeeds                         │
  │  ☐ STORAGE: kubectl get pv → all Bound (not Failed/Pending)           │
  │  ☐ INGRESS: curl -I https://anihpj.com/health → 200 OK               │
  │  ☐ ARGO CD: argocd app list → all Healthy/Synced                      │
  │  ☐ PROMETHEUS: curl prometheus:9090/-/healthy → OK                    │
  │  ☐ GRAFANA: curl grafana:3000/api/health → OK                         │
  │  ☐ KYVERNO: kubectl -n kyverno get pods → all Running                 │
  │  ☐ CERTIFICATES: All certs expire > 30 days from now                   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Run: Daily CronJob. Alert on: ANY check failure → page on-call.      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a Kubernetes CronJob that runs health checks every hour. Expose results as Prometheus metrics (`platform_health_check{component="etcd"} 1`). Create a Grafana dashboard with green/red indicators for each component. Alert if any component fails for > 5 minutes. Don't wait for users to tell you the platform is broken — know before they do.

Why not B? Pods "Running" doesn't mean they're functional — a Running CoreDNS pod might not resolve DNS. Why not C? Regular health checks catch silent failures before they become incidents. Why not D? Cloud providers ensure infrastructure health, not platform service health (your Argo CD, your Kyverno, your app ingress).

Observability Q169 of 200
What is capacity monitoring and how does a platform engineer forecast resource needs before outages occur?
A) Capacity monitoring means checking disk space once a month in a spreadsheet — it's a manual process
B) Capacity monitoring tracks resource utilization trends (CPU, memory, disk, network, etcd storage) over time and forecasts saturation points. Key metrics: node CPU/memory utilization trends (linear regression to predict "when will we hit 80%?"), PVC usage growth rate (predict when disk will fill), etcd database size (alert at 6GB, compact at 8GB — default quota), and pod density per node (prevent scheduler starvation). Use Prometheus `predict_linear()` to forecast: `predict_linear(node_filesystem_free[7d], 30*86400) < 0` = "disk will be full in 30 days"
C) Capacity monitoring is only needed for cloud platforms — on-premise clusters have infinite capacity
D) Kubernetes auto-scales everything — capacity planning is handled entirely by the cluster autoscaler and HPA
✅ Correct Answer: B

Auto-scaling handles short-term spikes. Capacity monitoring handles long-term growth. A platform with 50 microservices growing 5% per month: disk will be full in ~14 months. Catch this at month 10, order hardware/migrate to larger instances with zero downtime. Catch it at month 14: emergency at 3 AM. Key PromQL: `predict_linear(node_filesystem_free{mountpoint="/"}[30d], 30*86400)` — predicts free disk in 30 days. `etcd_db_total_size_in_bytes / etcd_quota_backend_bytes` — etcd fill percentage. Alert when any resource is predicted to saturate within 30 days. This is the difference between planned maintenance and emergency pages.

Capacity Forecasting with predict_linear
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DISK USAGE TREND (Prometheus + predict_linear):                        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  100% │                                              ██████ FORECAST   │
  │       │                                        ▄▄▄▄██                  │
  │   80% │                              ▄▄▄▄▄▄▄▄███ ← Alert threshold    │
  │       │                        ▄▄▄▄██                                  │
  │   60% │                  ▄▄▄▄██                                         │
  │       │            ▄▄▄▄██                                               │
  │   40% │      ▄▄▄▄██                                                     │
  │       │▄▄▄▄██                                                           │
  │   20% │██                                                                │
  │       └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────   │
  │       Jan  Feb  Mar  Apr  May  Jun  Jul  Aug  Sep  Oct  Nov  Dec       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ALERT RULE:                                                            │
  │  - alert: DiskWillFillIn30Days                                          │
  │    expr: predict_linear(node_filesystem_free[7d], 30*86400) < 0        │
  │    for: 1h                                                              │
  │    annotations:                                                         │
  │      summary: "Disk on {{ $labels.node }} predicted to fill in < 30d"  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create capacity dashboards tracking node CPU/memory trends, PVC usage growth, etcd database size, and pod count per node. Alert when `predict_linear` shows saturation within 30 days. Review monthly with the platform team. Don't wait for the disk to fill at 2 AM — predict it 30 days ahead and add storage during business hours.

Why not A? Spreadsheet-based capacity planning is reactive — Prometheus predict_linear is continuous and automated. Why not C? On-prem clusters have finite hardware too — capacity planning is universal. Why not D? Cluster Autoscaler adds nodes but doesn't predict long-term trends; HPA scales pods but doesn't manage underlying capacity.

Observability Q170 of 200
What is the Observability Maturity Model — from Crawl to Fly?
A) Observability maturity is measured by how many monitoring tools the platform uses — more tools = more mature
B) The maturity model has only two levels: you either have monitoring or you don't
C) 🐛 CRAWL (Reactive): Logs after a user reports an issue — "the site is down, let me check the logs." 🚶 WALK (Proactive): Metrics + dashboards — "CPU on node-3 trending high, opened a ticket before it fails." 🏃 RUN (Predictive): Traces + SLOs + error budgets — "error budget burn rate is accelerating, freeze deploys now." 🚀 FLY (Self-Healing): Automated remediation — "Prometheus fired alert → Argo Rollouts auto-rollback → incident auto-resolved without human intervention." Each level adds capability AND reduces MTTR
D) The Fly stage means replacing all observability tools with a single AI-powered platform that does everything automatically
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Observability Maturity Model is a roadmap. Most teams start at Crawl: SSH into servers and grep logs. Walk: deploy Prometheus + Grafana, create dashboards, get paged when thresholds break. Run: distributed tracing for root cause in seconds, SLO-based alerting eliminates noise, error budgets drive deployment decisions. Fly: the platform self-heals. Pod OOMKilled → HPA scales up → Argo Rollouts analysis detects latency spike → auto-rollback → incident resolved before the on-call even opens their laptop. Most platforms should target RUN; FLY is aspirational. Assess your current level and plan the next investment.

Observability Maturity Model
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🐛 CRAWL (Reactive)                                                    │
  │  • Tools: SSH + grep + tail -f                                          │
  │  • Detection: Users report issues                                       │
  │  • MTTR: Hours to days                                                  │
  │  • "The site is down — let me check the logs"                           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  🚶 WALK (Proactive)                                                    │
  │  • Tools: Prometheus + Grafana + basic alerts                          │
  │  • Detection: Dashboards show anomalies                                 │
  │  • MTTR: 30 min to hours                                                │
  │  • "CPU trending high — let's investigate before it fails"             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  🏃 RUN (Predictive)                                                    │
  │  • Tools: Traces + SLOs + Error Budgets + Runbooks                     │
  │  • Detection: SLO burn rate alerts BEFORE users notice                  │
  │  • MTTR: < 15 minutes                                                   │
  │  • "Error budget at 80% burn — freeze deploys, focus on reliability"   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  🚀 FLY (Self-Healing)                                                  │
  │  • Tools: Auto-remediation + Chaos Engineering                         │
  │  • Detection: System auto-detects AND auto-fixes                        │
  │  • MTTR: < 1 minute (automated)                                         │
  │  • "Alert fired → auto-rollback → resolved. Postmortem auto-generated."│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: assess current maturity — likely WALK (Prometheus + Grafana deployed, basic alerts).
