Next step to RUN: implement distributed tracing (Tempo + OTel), define SLOs with error budgets, create runbooks. Long-term aspiration to FLY: Argo Rollouts with automated analysis and rollback. You don't jump from Crawl to Fly — it's a journey. Know where you are and what's next.

Why not A? Tool count doesn't equal maturity — 10 poorly configured tools is worse than 3 well-integrated ones. Why not B? The model has four distinct levels with clear capabilities at each. Why not D? AI augments observability but doesn't replace the fundamental levels — you still need metrics, logs, and traces.

5.3 Secure Communication & RBACQ171-Q180

Security & Policy Q171 of 200
What is a Kubernetes NetworkPolicy and how does it enable zero-trust networking between pods?
A) NetworkPolicy is a firewall appliance that must be installed as a separate hardware device in the data center
B) NetworkPolicy only controls ingress traffic — egress traffic is always allowed and cannot be restricted
C) NetworkPolicy is automatically enabled on all Kubernetes clusters — pods are isolated by default with no configuration
D) NetworkPolicy is a Kubernetes resource defining allowed network traffic between pods. It uses podSelector (which pods this applies to), namespaceSelector (allow traffic from specific namespaces), and ipBlock (allow/deny CIDR ranges). Policy types: Ingress (incoming traffic), Egress (outgoing traffic), or both. By default, Kubernetes allows ALL pod-to-pod traffic; NetworkPolicies enforce the principle of least privilege — "deny all, then allow only what's necessary"
✅ Correct Answer: D

Without NetworkPolicies, any pod can reach any other pod — anihpj-web can directly connect to the PostgreSQL database, bypassing the API entirely. NetworkPolicies enforce defense in depth: anihpj-web → anihpj-api (allowed), anihpj-api → PostgreSQL (allowed), anihpj-web → PostgreSQL (DENIED). NetworkPolicies require a CNI that supports them (Calico, Cilium, Weave Net — NOT Flannel by default). Without a NetworkPolicy-aware CNI, you can create policies but they won't be enforced.

NetworkPolicy — Zero-Trust Pod Networking
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                                        │
  │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                                     │
  │  metadata:                                                               │
  │    name: anihpj-api-ingress                                              │
  │    namespace: anihpj-prod                                                │
  │  spec:                                                                   │
  │    podSelector:                                                          │
  │      matchLabels:                                                        │
  │        app: anihpj-api      ← Applies to anihpj-api pods                │
  │    policyTypes:                                                          │
  │    - Ingress                                                            │
  │    ingress:                                                              │
  │    - from:                                                               │
  │      - podSelector:                                                      │
  │          matchLabels:                                                    │
  │            app: anihpj-web  ← Allow traffic FROM anihpj-web ONLY       │
  │        ports:                                                            │
  │        - protocol: TCP                                                   │
  │          port: 8000                                                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  DEFAULT-DENY (apply first, then allow specific):                        │
  │  spec:                                                                   │
  │    podSelector: {}          ← Applies to ALL pods in namespace         │
  │    policyTypes:                                                          │
  │    - Ingress                                                            │
  │    - Egress                                                             │
  │    # No ingress/egress rules → denies ALL traffic                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a default-deny NetworkPolicy in anihpj-prod namespace. Then create allow policies: anihpj-web → anihpj-api:8000, anihpj-api → PostgreSQL:5432, anihpj-api → Redis:6379. All other traffic denied. NetworkPolicy: the firewall you write in YAML.

Why not A? It's a Kubernetes resource enforced by the CNI plugin, not hardware. Why not B? NetworkPolicy supports both Ingress and Egress rules. Why not C? Kubernetes pods are NOT isolated by default — all traffic is allowed until you create NetworkPolicies.

Security & Policy Q172 of 200
What is the default-deny NetworkPolicy pattern and why is it the foundation of zero-trust Kubernetes networking?
A) Default-deny means blocking all internet access to the cluster — internal pod-to-pod traffic is unaffected
B) Default-deny is a NetworkPolicy with empty podSelector `{}` and no ingress/egress rules — it denies ALL traffic to/from pods in the namespace. After applying it, you create explicit ALLOW policies for each required communication path (web→api, api→db, api→redis). This enforces zero-trust: "nothing can talk to anything unless explicitly permitted." Without default-deny, you're in an allow-all posture where a compromised web pod can attack the database directly
C) Default-deny is enabled by default on all Kubernetes clusters — no configuration is needed
D) Default-deny only applies to traffic between namespaces — pods within the same namespace can always communicate freely
✅ Correct Answer: B

Default-deny is the first NetworkPolicy every platform engineer should apply to every namespace. It flips the default from "allow all" to "deny all." Then you explicitly allow each required communication path. This enforces the principle of least privilege in networking. The order doesn't matter — NetworkPolicies are additive (union of all allow rules). Pro tip: apply default-deny FIRST in a new namespace, then test all application functionality to discover what communication paths need allow rules. If you apply default-deny to an existing namespace without testing, you'll break everything.

Default-Deny → Explicit Allow Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Step 1: DEFAULT-DENY (blocks everything)                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  kind: NetworkPolicy                                              │  │
  │  │  metadata:                                                        │  │
  │  │    name: default-deny-all                                         │  │
  │  │    namespace: anihpj-prod                                         │  │
  │  │  spec:                                                            │  │
  │  │    podSelector: {}        ← ALL pods                              │  │
  │  │    policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress]                                 │  │
  │  │    # No rules = DENY ALL                                          │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Step 2: ALLOW specific paths                                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  anihpj-web ──▶ anihpj-api:8000     (allow)                      │  │
  │  │  anihpj-api ──▶ PostgreSQL:5432     (allow)                      │  │
  │  │  anihpj-api ──▶ Redis:6379          (allow)                      │  │
  │  │  anihpj-api ──▶ auth-service:9000   (allow)                      │  │
  │  │  anihpj-web ──▶ PostgreSQL:5432     (BLOCKED by default-deny!)   │  │
  │  │  anihpj-api ──▶ external-ftp:21     (BLOCKED!)                    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: apply default-deny NetworkPolicy to anihpj-prod namespace. Then create allow policies for each required path. Test thoroughly in staging first. Monitor for dropped packets or failed connections. Default-deny: if you didn't explicitly allow it, it's blocked.

Why not A? Default-deny blocks ALL pod traffic (ingress + egress), not just external access. Why not C? Kubernetes defaults to allow-all; NetworkPolicies must be explicitly created. Why not D? Default-deny blocks traffic within the same namespace too — you must explicitly allow even same-ns communication.

Security & Policy Q173 of 200
What is mTLS (mutual TLS) and why is it essential for service-to-service communication in a service mesh?
A) mTLS is two-way TLS: both client AND server present certificates to prove their identity. In a service mesh (Istio/Linkerd), the sidecar proxy automatically handles mTLS — anihpj-api's sidecar presents its cert to PostgreSQL's sidecar, and PostgreSQL's sidecar presents its cert back. Both verify each other. This encrypts ALL pod-to-pod traffic AND authenticates both ends. Even if an attacker compromises a pod, they can't impersonate another service because they don't have its certificate. Zero application code changes — the sidecar handles everything
B) mTLS is standard TLS but with mutual authentication disabled — it only encrypts traffic, no identity verification
C) mTLS requires every application to implement its own certificate management — sidecars don't help
D) mTLS is only for external HTTPS traffic — internal pod-to-pod traffic in Kubernetes is always secure by default
✅ Correct Answer: A

Without mTLS, pod-to-pod traffic is unencrypted plaintext. Anyone with network access can sniff database credentials, API tokens, and sensitive data flowing between services. With mTLS via a service mesh: every pod gets an identity certificate from the mesh's CA. Every connection is automatically encrypted and mutually authenticated. Istio's Citadel or Linkerd's identity controller handles certificate issuance and rotation — certificates auto-rotate every 24 hours by default. The application code doesn't change — the sidecar proxy transparently upgrades all TCP connections to mTLS.

mTLS — Mutual Authentication
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT mTLS (plaintext):                                              │
  │  anihpj-api ──"SELECT * FROM users"──▶ PostgreSQL  ← Anyone can sniff! │
  │                                                                         │
  │  WITH mTLS (service mesh sidecars):                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐                    ┌─────────────────┐            │
  │  │  anihpj-api pod  │                    │  PostgreSQL pod  │            │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐ │    🔒 mTLS 🔒     │ ┌─────────────┐ │            │
  │  │  │   App       │ │◀══════════════════▶│ │   App       │ │            │
  │  │  │ (no TLS     │ │                    │ │ (no TLS     │ │            │
  │  │  │  code!)     │ │                    │ │  code!)     │ │            │
  │  │  └──────┬──────┘ │                    │ └──────┬──────┘ │            │
  │  │         │        │                    │        │        │            │
  │  │  ┌──────▼──────┐ │                    │ ┌──────▼──────┐ │            │
  │  │  │Envoy Sidecar│ │  Cert exchange:    │ │Envoy Sidecar│ │            │
  │  │  │spiffe://... │ │  "I am anihpj-api" │ │spiffe://... │ │            │
  │  │  │  /anihpj-api│ │◀══════════════════▶│ │ /postgresql │ │            │
  │  │  └─────────────┘ │  "I am postgresql" │ └─────────────┘ │            │
  │  └─────────────────┘                    └─────────────────┘            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ Encrypted, authenticated, ZERO application code changes             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy Istio with mTLS in STRICT mode for the anihpj-prod namespace. All traffic between anihpj-web, anihpj-api, PostgreSQL, and Redis is automatically encrypted and authenticated. Certificates auto-rotate every 24 hours. mTLS via service mesh: encryption and identity without a single line of application code.

Why not B? mTLS = MUTUAL TLS — both parties authenticate, not just encryption. Why not C? Service mesh sidecars handle certificate management transparently — the app doesn't need to know about mTLS. Why not D? Internal pod-to-pod traffic is UNENCRYPTED by default — mTLS secures it.

Security & Policy Q174 of 200
What is cert-manager and how does it automate TLS certificate lifecycle management in Kubernetes?
A) cert-manager is a manual certificate generation tool — it creates TLS certs that you must copy-paste into YAML files
B) cert-manager is a Kubernetes-native certificate management controller. It uses CRDs: Issuer (namespace-scoped) and ClusterIssuer (cluster-scoped) define HOW to get certificates (Let's Encrypt ACME, Vault, self-signed, Venafi). The Certificate CRD defines WHAT certificate you want: domain name, secret name to store it in, duration, renewal policy. cert-manager automatically: orders the certificate, completes ACME DNS-01/HTTP-01 challenges, stores the cert+key in a Kubernetes Secret, and auto-renews 30 days before expiry — all zero-touch after initial setup
C) cert-manager only supports Let's Encrypt — it can't work with other certificate authorities or HashiCorp Vault
D) cert-manager replaces Kubernetes Secrets — certificates are stored in its own database, not in etcd
✅ Correct Answer: B

Before cert-manager: manually generate certs with openssl, create Kubernetes Secret, remember to renew before expiry, manually update the Secret, restart pods. Every 90 days: panic. After cert-manager: create a Certificate CRD once. cert-manager handles issuance, storage (K8s Secret), and renewal — forever. Let's Encrypt integration: cert-manager completes the ACME challenge (HTTP-01 places a file at /.well-known/acme-challenge/; DNS-01 creates a TXT record in your DNS provider). After validation, Let's Encrypt issues the cert, cert-manager stores it in the specified Secret, and your Ingress automatically picks it up.

cert-manager — Automated TLS Lifecycle
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ClusterIssuer (cluster-scoped):                                        │
  │  apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1                                         │
  │  kind: ClusterIssuer                                                     │
  │  metadata:                                                               │
  │    name: letsencrypt-prod                                               │
  │  spec:                                                                   │
  │    acme:                                                                 │
  │      server: https://acme-v02.api.letsencrypt.org/directory             │
  │      email: platform@anihpj.com                                         │
  │      privateKeySecretRef:                                                │
  │        name: letsencrypt-prod-key                                       │
  │      solvers:                                                            │
  │      - http01:                                                           │
  │          ingress:                                                        │
  │            class: nginx                                                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Certificate (namespace-scoped):                                        │
  │  apiVersion: cert-manager.io/v1                                         │
  │  kind: Certificate                                                       │
  │  spec:                                                                   │
  │    secretName: anihpj-tls          ← Stores cert+key here              │
  │    dnsNames:                                                             │
  │    - anihpj.com                                                          │
  │    - api.anihpj.com                                                      │
  │    issuerRef:                                                            │
  │      name: letsencrypt-prod                                              │
  │      kind: ClusterIssuer                                                 │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ cert-manager auto-renews 30 days before expiry — forever            │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy cert-manager. Create a ClusterIssuer for Let's Encrypt (production). Create Certificate CRDs for anihpj.com and api.anihpj.com using HTTP-01 challenge. Ingress references the `anihpj-tls` Secret. cert-manager auto-renews before expiry. Never manually create a TLS certificate again. cert-manager handles it forever.

Why not A? cert-manager is fully automated — it orders, stores, and renews certificates without human intervention. Why not C? cert-manager supports Let's Encrypt, Vault, Venafi, AWS PCA, Google CAS, self-signed, and more. Why not D? cert-manager stores certificates in standard Kubernetes Secrets — your Ingress/Gateway references them normally.

Security & Policy Q175 of 200
How does Ingress TLS work — from TLS termination to Let's Encrypt HTTP-01 and DNS-01 challenges?
A) Ingress TLS stores certificates as plain text in the Ingress YAML — no Kubernetes Secrets are involved
B) HTTP-01 challenge requires modifying DNS records; DNS-01 requires placing a file on a web server — the names are reversed
C) Ingress TLS terminates HTTPS at the Ingress Controller (e.g., nginx-ingress) and forwards plain HTTP to backend pods. The TLS certificate is stored in a Kubernetes Secret referenced by the Ingress. HTTP-01 challenge: Let's Encrypt places a token file at `/.well-known/acme-challenge/` on your web server to prove domain ownership (requires port 80 open). DNS-01 challenge: Let's Encrypt verifies a TXT record you create at `_acme-challenge.anihpj.com` (works even if port 80 is blocked — suitable for internal services, TCP services, and wildcard certs like `*.anihpj.com`)
D) Ingress TLS requires a separate TLS termination proxy outside Kubernetes — Ingress Controllers can't handle TLS
✅ Correct Answer: C

TLS termination at Ingress: client → HTTPS → Ingress Controller (decrypts) → HTTP → backend pod. The Ingress Controller handles TLS, so your application doesn't need TLS code — it just serves HTTP. The Secret must be in the same namespace as the Ingress. HTTP-01: simplest, requires port 80 open to internet, cert-manager auto-creates a temporary Ingress for the challenge. DNS-01: more flexible, works for internal services (no public port 80 needed), supports wildcard certificates (`*.anihpj.com`), but requires cert-manager to have API access to your DNS provider (Route53, Cloudflare, etc.) to create/delete TXT records.

Ingress TLS — Termination & ACME Challenges
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TLS TERMINATION at Ingress:                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐      │
  │  │  User ──HTTPS──▶ Ingress Controller (decrypts TLS)             │      │
  │  │                        │                                       │      │
  │  │                   HTTP (plain)                                  │      │
  │  │                        ▼                                       │      │
  │  │              anihpj-api pod (port 8000)                        │      │
  │  │  • App serves HTTP only — no TLS code needed!                  │      │
  │  │  • TLS cert stored in Secret → referenced by Ingress          │      │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  HTTP-01 CHALLENGE:                     DNS-01 CHALLENGE:               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┐        ┌────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Requires: Port 80 open     │        │ Requires: DNS API access   │  │
  │  │ Works for: Public services │        │ Works for: Everything!     │  │
  │  │ Wildcard certs: ❌         │        │ Wildcard certs: ✅         │  │
  │  │ Internal services: ❌      │        │ Internal services: ✅      │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────┘        └────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: use DNS-01 challenge with Route53. Create a Certificate CRD for `*.anihpj.com` (wildcard). cert-manager creates the TXT record in Route53, Let's Encrypt validates it, issues the wildcard cert, cert-manager stores it in a Secret. All subdomains (anihpj.com, api.anihpj.com, admin.anihpj.com) use the same wildcard cert. DNS-01 + cert-manager = wildcard TLS certificates, automated forever.

Why not A? TLS certs are stored in Kubernetes Secrets, never in plaintext YAML. Why not B? HTTP-01 uses a web server file; DNS-01 uses DNS TXT records. Why not D? Ingress Controllers handle TLS termination natively.

Security & Policy Q176 of 200
What advanced TLS features does the Gateway API provide that Ingress cannot?
A) Gateway API supports TLS passthrough (TCP proxy — backend handles TLS termination, Gateway passes encrypted traffic through for end-to-end encryption), TLS termination (Gateway decrypts, forwards HTTP to backend), and TLS re-encryption (Gateway decrypts external TLS, re-encrypts with internal TLS to backend — defense in depth). It also supports cross-namespace TLS certificate references, multiple certificates per Gateway listener, and SNI-based certificate selection. Ingress only supports basic TLS termination with one cert per host
B) Gateway API and Ingress have identical TLS capabilities — Gateway API is just a newer YAML format
C) Gateway API doesn't support TLS at all — it's designed for plain HTTP routing only
D) Gateway API TLS requires a hardware security module (HSM) — software TLS termination is not supported
✅ Correct Answer: A

Gateway API is the next-generation Kubernetes networking API. TLS capabilities beyond Ingress: Passthrough mode — the Gateway acts as a TCP proxy, passing encrypted traffic directly to the backend. This enables the backend to handle its own TLS (useful for mTLS with service mesh or applications that need end-to-end encryption). Re-encryption: external HTTPS → Gateway decrypts → re-encrypts with internal TLS → backend. This ensures traffic is encrypted even within the cluster (defense in depth). SNI-based routing: multiple certificates on the same listener, Gateway selects the right cert based on the client's SNI hostname.

Gateway API — TLS Modes
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TLS TERMINATION (standard):                                            │
  │  Client ──HTTPS──▶ Gateway ──HTTP──▶ Backend                           │
  │                   (decrypt)                                             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  TLS PASSTHROUGH (end-to-end):                                          │
  │  Client ──HTTPS──▶ Gateway ──HTTPS──▶ Backend                          │
  │                   (TCP proxy,          (handles its                     │
  │                    no decrypt)          own TLS)                        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  TLS RE-ENCRYPTION (defense in depth):                                  │
  │  Client ──HTTPS──▶ Gateway ──HTTPS──▶ Backend                          │
  │          (cert-A)  (decrypt A,  (cert-B)                               │
  │                     encrypt B)                                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: use Gateway API for production traffic. Terminate external TLS at the Gateway with a Let's Encrypt certificate. Use re-encryption to encrypt traffic between the Gateway and anihpj-api pods with an internal cert-manager certificate. Gateway API: Ingress evolved. More flexibility, better security, cross-namespace design.

Why not B? Gateway API adds passthrough, re-encryption, SNI, and cross-namespace certs — significant TLS capabilities beyond Ingress. Why not C? Gateway API has rich TLS support across all three modes. Why not D? Gateway API TLS works entirely in software via the Gateway controller implementation.

Security & Policy Q177 of 200
What is the core relationship between ServiceAccount, Role/ClusterRole, and RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding in Kubernetes RBAC?
A) ServiceAccount = identity for pods ("who am I?"). Role/ClusterRole = set of permissions ("what can I do?"). RoleBinding/ClusterRoleBinding = connection ("this SA has these permissions in this namespace/cluster"). A pod runs as a ServiceAccount, and permissions are granted by binding a Role to that ServiceAccount. No direct assignment — SA → RoleBinding → Role → permissions. This indirection allows one Role to be bound to many SAs, and one SA to have multiple Roles
B) ServiceAccounts and Roles are the same thing — just different API versions of the same resource
C) Permissions are assigned directly to ServiceAccounts — Roles and RoleBindings are optional wrappers
D) ServiceAccounts are only for human users — pods use a different authentication mechanism
✅ Correct Answer: A

RBAC is a three-layer system. Layer 1 (Identity): ServiceAccount — every pod runs as exactly one SA. If unspecified, uses the `default` SA in the namespace. Layer 2 (Permissions): Role (namespace-scoped) or ClusterRole (cluster-scoped) defines what verbs can be performed on what resources. Layer 3 (Binding): RoleBinding connects a SA to a Role within a namespace. ClusterRoleBinding connects a SA to a ClusterRole cluster-wide. The indirection is powerful: define a `pod-reader` Role once, bind it to multiple SAs in different namespaces.

RBAC: SA → RoleBinding → Role
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ServiceAccount                Role (permissions)                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ kind: ServiceAccount │     │ kind: Role                           │ │
  │  │ name: anihpj-api     │     │ name: pod-reader                     │ │
  │  │ namespace: prod      │     │ rules:                               │ │
  │  │                      │     │ - apiGroups: [""]                    │ │
  │  │ "I am anihpj-api"   │     │   resources: ["pods"]                 │ │
  │  └──────────┬───────────┘     │   verbs: ["get","list","watch"]     │ │
  │             │                 │   resourceNames: []                  │ │
  │             │                 └──────────────────┬───────────────────┘ │
  │             │                                    │                      │
  │             └──────────┐    ┌────────────────────┘                      │
  │                        │    │                                           │
  │                   ┌────▼────▼────┐                                      │
  │                   │ RoleBinding  │  "anihpj-api SA can get/list/watch  │
  │                   │ name: api-rb │   pods in prod namespace"            │
  │                   └──────────────┘                                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a dedicated SA `anihpj-api` for the API pods (NOT the default SA). Create a Role allowing get/list on ConfigMaps and Secrets in the prod namespace. Create a RoleBinding linking the SA to the Role. Never use the default ServiceAccount — create dedicated SAs with least-privilege Roles.

Why not B? SA = identity, Role = permissions — they serve entirely different purposes. Why not C? Permissions are NEVER assigned directly to SAs — RoleBinding is mandatory. Why not D? ServiceAccounts are for pods and applications; Users and Groups (via OIDC/certificates) are for humans.

Security & Policy Q178 of 200
What is the principle of least privilege in Kubernetes RBAC and how do you implement it?
A) Least privilege means giving every ServiceAccount cluster-admin so nothing is ever blocked
B) Least privilege means using only the default ServiceAccount for all pods — it has the minimum necessary permissions
C) Least privilege means granting the MINIMUM permissions required for a workload to function — no more. Implementation: (1) Create a dedicated SA per workload, (2) Grant ONLY the specific verbs on specific resources needed (e.g., "get, list pods in prod namespace" — NOT "get, list, watch, create, update, patch, delete all resources"), (3) Use resourceNames to restrict to specific named resources when possible, (4) Regularly audit with `kubectl auth can-i --list`, (5) Remove unused Roles/RoleBindings. If a pod doesn't need to delete pods, don't grant delete on pods
D) Least privilege is automatically enforced by Kubernetes — no manual RBAC configuration is needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

RBAC mistakes are the #1 Kubernetes security vulnerability. The `default` SA in each namespace often accumulates permissions over time as teams add RoleBindings. Create dedicated SAs: anihpj-api needs get/list on ConfigMaps and Secrets (to read configuration), create on Events (to record events), but NOT delete on pods (that's the platform's responsibility). Use resourceNames to scope further: `resourceNames: ["anihpj-config"]` — this SA can only access that specific ConfigMap, not all ConfigMaps. Regular audit: `kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:anihpj-api -n prod` reveals all effective permissions.

Least Privilege RBAC
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ OVER-PRIVILEGED (common mistake):                                   │
  │  rules:                                                                 │
  │  - apiGroups: ["*"]     ← ALL API groups                                │
  │    resources: ["*"]     ← ALL resources                                 │
  │    verbs: ["*"]         ← ALL verbs — essentially cluster-admin!       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ LEAST PRIVILEGE (correct):                                          │
  │  rules:                                                                 │
  │  - apiGroups: [""]                                                      │
  │    resources: ["configmaps", "secrets"]                                 │
  │    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                                     │
  │    resourceNames: ["anihpj-config"]  ← Only THIS ConfigMap!           │
  │  - apiGroups: [""]                                                      │
  │    resources: ["events"]                                                │
  │    verbs: ["create"]                                                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  AUDIT: kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:prod:sa  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: audit all existing RoleBindings. Remove wildcard permissions. Create dedicated SAs for anihpj-api, anihpj-worker, and anihpj-web with precisely scoped Roles. Run `kubectl auth can-i --list` quarterly. If a compromised pod has cluster-admin, the attacker owns your cluster. Least privilege limits blast radius.

Why not A? That's maximum privilege — the opposite of least privilege. Why not B? The default SA has NO permissions by default — but teams often add bindings to it, making it over-privileged. Why not D? Kubernetes defaults to RBAC disabled or allow-all in some configurations — least privilege must be manually implemented.

Security & Policy Q179 of 200
What are ClusterRole aggregation labels and how do they simplify RBAC management at scale?
A) Aggregation labels combine multiple ServiceAccounts into one — reducing the number of SAs needed
B) ClusterRole aggregation uses labels (`rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-admin: "true"`) to automatically MERGE rules from multiple ClusterRoles into an aggregated ClusterRole. When you create a new CRD with an aggregation-labeled ClusterRole, its rules are automatically added to the `admin`, `edit`, and `view` default ClusterRoles. This means: create a CRD → create a ClusterRole with aggregation label → existing RoleBindings to `admin` automatically include the new CRD permissions — no binding updates needed
C) Aggregation labels compress RBAC rules to save etcd storage space — they don't affect permissions
D) Aggregation labels are deprecated — they were replaced by OPA/Gatekeeper policies
✅ Correct Answer: B

Without aggregation: every time you install a CRD (cert-manager, Argo Rollouts, Kyverno), you must manually update the `admin` ClusterRole to include permissions for the new custom resources. Miss one, and developers with `admin` role can't use the new CRD. With aggregation: the CRD's operator creates a ClusterRole with `rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-admin: "true"`. Kubernetes automatically merges those rules into the `admin` ClusterRole. Zero manual updates. This is how the built-in `admin`, `edit`, and `view` roles stay current with every CRD you install.

ClusterRole Aggregation
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Custom ClusterRole (created by cert-manager):                          │
  │  metadata:                                                              │
  │    name: cert-manager-admin-aggregation                                 │
  │    labels:                                                              │
  │      rbac.authorization.k8s.io/aggregate-to-admin: "true"  ← MAGIC     │
  │  rules:                                                                 │
  │  - apiGroups: ["cert-manager.io"]                                       │
  │    resources: ["certificates", "issuers"]                              │
  │    verbs: ["*"]                                                         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Kubernetes AUTO-MERGES into admin ClusterRole:                         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  admin ClusterRole (before)          admin ClusterRole (after)    │  │
  │  │  rules:                              rules:                       │  │
  │  │  - apiGroups: [""]                  - apiGroups: [""]             │  │
  │  │    ... pods, services ...             ... pods, services ...      │  │
  │  │  - apiGroups: ["apps"]              - apiGroups: ["apps"]         │  │
  │  │    ... deployments ...                ... deployments ...         │  │
  │  │                                      - apiGroups: ["cert-manager"]│  │
  │  │                                        resources: [certificates]  │  │
  │  │                                        verbs: ["*"]  ← AUTO-ADDED│  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when creating CRDs for platform services, always include aggregation-labeled ClusterRoles. This ensures developers with `admin` role in their namespace automatically get permissions for new CRDs. Aggregation labels: install a CRD once, RBAC updates itself forever.

Why not A? Aggregation merges ClusterRole RULES, not ServiceAccounts. Why not C? Aggregation affects permissions, not storage — it auto-adds rules to aggregated roles. Why not D? Aggregation labels are current and widely used — they're the standard pattern for CRD RBAC.

Security & Policy Q180 of 200
What special RBAC considerations apply to Kubernetes Operators and their ServiceAccounts?
A) Operators need narrowly scoped RBAC for their specific CRDs — not cluster-admin. An operator that manages DatabaseClaim CRDs needs: get/list/watch on DatabaseClaim, create/update on Events (for status updates), get on Pods (to verify health), and update on DatabaseClaim/status (to update status). It does NOT need: delete on namespaces, get on secrets, or wildcard permissions. Operators should use a dedicated SA per operator, and permissions should be scoped to the exact resources the operator's reconciliation loop touches. Use `kubebuilder` RBAC tags to auto-generate least-privilege ClusterRoles from code annotations
B) Operators always need cluster-admin — their reconciliation logic requires unrestricted access
C) Operators don't need RBAC at all — they run inside the control plane with built-in permissions
D) Operator RBAC is identical to application RBAC — no special considerations are needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The most dangerous RBAC pattern: installing an operator with cluster-admin "because it's easier." If that operator has a vulnerability, the attacker gets cluster-admin. Operator SDK/Kubebuilder generate RBAC based on code annotations: `//+kubebuilder:rbac:groups=database.example.com,resources=databaseclaims,verbs=get;list;watch;create;update;patch;delete`. This generates precisely the ClusterRole needed. Operators also typically need access to owned resources (Pods, Services, PVCs they create) and Events (for recording status changes). Use a dedicated SA per operator, NOT the default SA. Never use `verbs: ["*"]` or `resources: ["*"]`.

Operator RBAC — Least Privilege
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Operator: DatabaseClaim Controller (manages PostgreSQL instances)     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  NEEDS (scoped to its function):                                        │
  │  rules:                                                                 │
  │  - apiGroups: ["database.anihpj.com"]                                   │
  │    resources: ["databaseclaims"]           ← Its own CRD               │
  │    verbs: ["get","list","watch","update","patch"]                      │
  │  - apiGroups: ["database.anihpj.com"]                                   │
  │    resources: ["databaseclaims/status"]    ← Status subresource        │
  │    verbs: ["update"]                                                    │
  │  - apiGroups: ["apps"]                                                  │
  │    resources: ["statefulsets"]             ← Creates StatefulSets      │
  │    verbs: ["get","list","watch","create","update","delete"]            │
  │  - apiGroups: [""]                                                      │
  │    resources: ["events"]                   ← Records events            │
  │    verbs: ["create"]                                                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  DOES NOT NEED (not in its scope):                                      │
  │  ❌ secrets, configmaps, namespaces, nodes, clusterroles               │
  │  ❌ delete on anything except its own StatefulSets                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: if building a custom operator for the job board platform, use Kubebuilder with RBAC annotations. Generate a ClusterRole with exactly the permissions needed. Test with `kubectl auth can-i`. Never grant cluster-admin to an operator. An operator with cluster-admin is a single vulnerability away from total cluster compromise.

Why not B? Cluster-admin is almost never needed — operators manage specific resources, not the entire cluster. Why not C? Operators run as pods with ServiceAccounts like any other workload — they need explicit RBAC. Why not D? Operators need broader permissions than typical apps (creating/deleting resources), making least-privilege scoping critical.

5.4 Policy Engines & ComplianceQ181-Q200

Security & Policy Q181 of 200
What are Pod Security Standards (PSS) and what are the three policy levels?
A) PSS is a CNCF certification program for Kubernetes distributions — it's not related to pod security
B) PSS defines three policy levels: Privileged (unrestricted — system pods, CI/CD runners), Baseline (prevents known privilege escalations — blocks hostPath volumes, hostNetwork, privileged containers), and Restricted (hardened following pod hardening best practices — enforces runAsNonRoot, drops ALL capabilities, seccomp profiles required, read-only root filesystem). These replace the deprecated PodSecurityPolicy (PSP) and are enforced by Pod Security Admission (PSA) controller built into Kubernetes 1.25+
C) PSS has only one level — "secure" — that applies equally to all pods in all namespaces
D) PSS is enforced by a third-party admission controller Kyverno — Kubernetes has no built-in pod security
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pod Security Standards replaced the complex PodSecurityPolicy (deprecated in 1.21, removed in 1.25). PSS is simpler: three named levels, enforced per namespace via labels. `pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted` blocks any pod that violates the Restricted profile. `pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted` logs violations without blocking (for testing). `pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted` warns users via kubectl output without blocking. Typical strategy: production namespaces = Restricted, CI/CD = Baseline, kube-system = Privileged.

Pod Security Standards — Three Levels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PRIVILEGED (unrestricted):                                             │
  │  • Everything allowed — hostNetwork, hostPID, privileged containers    │
  │  • Use for: kube-system, monitoring agents, CNI plugins, CSI drivers   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  BASELINE (prevent known exploits):                                     │
  │  • Blocks: hostPath, hostNetwork, hostPID, privileged containers       │
  │  • Allows: runAsUser: 0 (root) in container (not on host)              │
  │  • Use for: CI/CD runners, build containers, trusted internal apps     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  RESTRICTED (hardened best practices):                                  │
  │  • Enforces: runAsNonRoot, drop ALL capabilities, seccomp profile      │
  │  • Requires: readOnlyRootFilesystem (write to mounted volumes only)    │
  │  • Use for: ALL application pods in production                         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Namespace label:                                                       │
  │  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: label anihpj-prod namespace `enforce: restricted`. The Django app must run as non-root (USER 1001 in Dockerfile), drop all capabilities, use read-only root filesystem with writable /tmp as emptyDir. CI/CD namespace gets `enforce: baseline`. PSS: simple, built-in, no external admission controller needed. Label your namespaces and K8s enforces pod security.

Why not A? PSS is a built-in Kubernetes feature, not a certification program. Why not C? Three levels allow flexibility — not all workloads need the same security posture. Why not D? Pod Security Admission is BUILT INTO Kubernetes 1.25+ — no third-party controller needed.

Security & Policy Q182 of 200
How does Pod Security Admission (PSA) enforce Pod Security Standards through namespace labels?
A) PSA requires installing Kyverno or OPA — Kubernetes has no built-in enforcement mechanism
B) PSA only logs violations — it never blocks pod creation regardless of namespace labels
C) PSA is a built-in admission controller (enabled by default in 1.25+) that enforces PSS via three namespace labels: `enforce` (block violating pods), `audit` (log violations — for testing policies before enforcing), and `warn` (return warnings to the user). Labels can be set per policy level: `pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted` blocks any pod that violates the Restricted standard. Version labels like `enforce-version: latest` or `enforce-version: v1.29` pin to specific K8s PSS version definitions, preventing breakage when upgrading clusters
D) PSA labels are set on individual pods — each pod specifies its own security level
✅ Correct Answer: C

PSA is Kubernetes' built-in replacement for PodSecurityPolicy. Three-mode enforcement per namespace: enforce blocks, audit logs, warn messages. Use audit first to evaluate impact without breaking workloads: set `audit: restricted` on prod namespace for a week, check audit logs for violations, fix pods, THEN set `enforce: restricted`. Version pinning is critical: `enforce-version: v1.29` ensures your policies don't change when you upgrade Kubernetes. Without version pinning, a K8s upgrade might add new restrictions that suddenly block your pods.

PSA Namespace Labels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Namespace: anihpj-prod                                                 │
  │  labels:                                                                │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted                      │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: v1.32                   │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted                        │
  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted                         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  RESULT:                                                                │
  │  $ kubectl apply -f privileged-pod.yaml -n anihpj-prod                 │
  │  Warning: would violate PodSecurity "restricted:v1.32"                 │
  │  Error from server: pods "bad-pod" is forbidden:                       │
  │    violates PodSecurity "restricted:v1.32":                            │
  │    privileged (container "app" must not set securityContext.privileged)│
  │                                                                         │
  │  ROLLOUT STRATEGY:                                                      │
  │  1. Set audit + warn → monitor for 1 week                               │
  │  2. Fix violating pods                                                  │
  │  3. Set enforce → now blocking                                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: label all production namespaces with `audit: restricted` first. Review audit logs for 2 weeks. Fix any violations (add runAsNonRoot, drop capabilities). Then add `enforce: restricted` with version `v1.32`. PSA: audit first, enforce second. Never enforce without auditing first.

Why not A? PSA is built into Kubernetes 1.25+ — no external admission controller required. Why not B? The `enforce` label actively blocks violating pods — audit and warn are the non-blocking modes. Why not D? PSA labels are on NAMESPACES, not pods — the namespace defines the security level for all pods within it.

Security & Policy Q183 of 200
How do you analyze audit logs to detect suspicious RBAC activity and security threats?
A) Audit log analysis requires a dedicated ML platform — manual analysis is impossible
B) Key patterns to detect: RBAC escalation (a user suddenly performing operations they've never done before — "alice never deleted pods before, now deleted 50 in 5 minutes"), privilege escalation (new ClusterRoleBinding created binding to cluster-admin), secret enumeration (rapid get/list on secrets across multiple namespaces — possible credential theft), unusual source IPs (kubectl from an IP in a foreign country), and failed authorization spikes (someone probing what they can access). Ship audit logs to Loki/ES, create dashboards, and alert on these patterns
C) Audit logs are only for compliance checkboxes — they have no security analysis value
D) Audit log analysis is fully automated by Kubernetes — it detects and blocks suspicious patterns automatically
✅ Correct Answer: B

Audit logs are your security camera footage. Without analysis, they're just terabytes of JSON sitting in S3. Key queries: `{resource="secrets"} | json | verb="list" | line_format "{{.user}} listed secrets in {{.namespace}}"` — detect secret enumeration. `{verb="create"} | json | objectRef.resource="clusterrolebindings"` — detect new privilege grants. `count by user, verb` — establish baseline behavior, alert on deviations. Integrate with Falco: Falco detects a suspicious syscall → correlate with audit logs to see who deployed that pod and what RBAC they had.

Audit Log Threat Detection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🚨 DETECT THESE PATTERNS:                                             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  1. Secret enumeration:                                                 │
  │     {resource="secrets"} | json | verb in ["list","get"]               │
  │     50+ requests in 1 minute from same user → ALERT                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  2. Privilege escalation:                                               │
  │     {objectRef.resource="clusterrolebindings"} | json | verb="create"  │
  │     New binding to "cluster-admin" → CRITICAL ALERT                    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  3. Mass deletion:                                                      │
  │     {verb="delete"} | json | count by user                             │
  │     > 10 deletes in 1 minute → ALERT (possible ransomware)              │
  │                                                                         │
  │  4. Anomalous geography:                                                │
  │     {sourceIPs=~"185\\..*"} | json                                      │
  │     Access from unexpected country → ALERT                              │
  │                                                                         │
  │  5. RBAC probing:                                                       │
  │     {responseStatus.code="403"} | json | count by user                 │
  │     Spike in 403s → someone probing permissions                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: ship audit logs to Loki. Create Grafana alerts for secret enumeration, privilege escalation, and mass deletion. Create a security dashboard showing audit activity. Audit logs without analysis are just expensive storage. With analysis, they're your security early warning system.

Why not A? Basic pattern detection with Loki/LogQL is sufficient for most threats — ML is optional. Why not C? Audit logs are a primary security detection tool. Why not D? Kubernetes logs audit events but doesn't analyze them — you must build detection yourself.

Security & Policy Q184 of 200
What is an SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) and how does it improve platform supply chain security?
A) An SBOM is a machine-readable inventory of EVERY component in a container image: base image (alpine:3.19), OS packages (openssl 3.1.4), language dependencies (Django 5.0.1, psycopg2 2.9.9), and transitive dependencies. Generated by tools like syft or trivy in SPDX or CycloneDX format. When CVE-2024-XXXX is announced in openssl, you query your SBOM database: "Which of our 50 images contain openssl?" → instant answer → patch only affected images. Without SBOM, you don't know what's inside your images
B) SBOM is a deployment manifest listing which pods run on which nodes — it's an inventory of the cluster, not images
C) SBOM is generated automatically by Kubernetes for every running pod — no tools needed
D) SBOM replaces vulnerability scanning — if you have an SBOM, you don't need Trivy or Grype
✅ Correct Answer: A

SBOM answers the question every platform security team dreads: "Are we affected by this new CVE?" Without SBOM: manually check 50 Dockerfiles, email every team, takes 2 days. With SBOM: `grype sbom:anihpj-api.spdx.json | grep CVE-2024-XXXX` → instant. Generate SBOMs in CI after building images with `syft anihpj-api:latest -o spdx-json > anihpj-api.spdx.json`. Attach SBOMs to container images using Cosign: `cosign attach sbom --sbom anihpj-api.spdx.json anihpj-api:latest`. Store in an SBOM registry (Dependency-Track) for continuous monitoring.

SBOM — Know What's Inside Your Images
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ syft anihpj-api:v2.1.0 -o spdx-json                                 │
  │                                                                         │
  │  anihpj-api:v2.1.0 (SPDX)                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Base image: python:3.12-slim                                     │  │
  │  │  ├── openssl 3.1.4-r0                                            │  │
  │  │  ├── libcrypto3 3.1.4-r0                                         │  │
  │  │  ├── libssl3 3.1.4-r0                                            │  │
  │  │  └── ...                                                         │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  Python packages (pip):                                           │  │
  │  │  ├── Django 5.0.1                                                 │  │
  │  │  ├── psycopg2 2.9.9                                              │  │
  │  │  ├── celery 5.3.6                                                │  │
  │  │  ├── redis 5.0.1                                                 │  │
  │  │  ├── gunicorn 21.2.0                                             │  │
  │  │  └── requests 2.31.0  ← CVE-2024-XXXX discovered!                │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  When CVE-2024-XXXX hits: query SBOMs, find anihpj-api:v2.1.0 affected│
  │  → Patch requests, rebuild, redeploy — DONE in 30 minutes              │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: add `syft` to the CI pipeline. Generate SPDX SBOM after every image build. Attach SBOM to the image with Cosign. Upload to Dependency-Track for continuous monitoring. SBOM: when the next Log4j/OpenSSL vulnerability drops, you'll know if you're affected in 30 seconds, not 2 days.

Why not B? SBOM is for software components inside images, not cluster inventory. Why not C? K8s doesn't generate SBOMs — you need external tools. Why not D? SBOM tells you WHAT's inside; scanning tells you if it's VULNERABLE — complementary tools.

Security & Policy Q185 of 200
What is the CIS Kubernetes Benchmark and how does kube-bench assess cluster security posture?
A) CIS Benchmark is a Kubernetes performance benchmark — it measures API server throughput and etcd latency
B) CIS Benchmark is only for cloud-managed Kubernetes (EKS, AKS, GKE) — self-managed clusters can't use it
C) The CIS Kubernetes Benchmark is a comprehensive security configuration guide (~200+ checks) covering: control plane (API server flags, etcd security), worker nodes (kubelet config, container runtime), and policies (RBAC, NetworkPolicy, PSP/PSS). kube-bench is an open-source tool that runs these checks automatically: `kube-bench run --targets master` checks the control plane, returns PASS/FAIL/WARN for each check with remediation steps. Critical for: security audits, compliance (PCI-DSS, SOC2), and hardening before production
D) CIS Benchmark mandates using a specific Kubernetes distribution — it's not applicable to vanilla Kubernetes
✅ Correct Answer: C

The CIS Benchmark is the industry standard for Kubernetes hardening. It's a PDF document with ~200 recommendations: "1.1.1 Ensure that the API server pod specification file permissions are set to 600 or more restrictive" (scored, automated check). kube-bench automates these checks by running as a Job or pod, reading config files, and comparing against CIS recommendations. Run kube-bench monthly and after every cluster upgrade. Prioritize FAILs over WARNs, and scored checks over unscored. Most managed K8s services (EKS, AKS) publish their CIS compliance reports — but you still need to verify.

kube-bench — CIS Compliance
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  $ kubectl apply -f job-master.yaml    (runs kube-bench as a Job)      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  [PASS] 1.1.1 API server pod spec file permissions: 600 ✅             │
  │  [PASS] 1.1.2 API server pod spec file ownership: root:root ✅         │
  │  [FAIL] 1.2.6 Ensure --insecure-port=0: --insecure-port=8080 ❌       │
  │         Fix: Add --insecure-port=0 to kube-apiserver manifest          │
  │  [WARN] 1.2.7 Ensure --authorization-mode=Node,RBAC:                  │
  │         --authorization-mode includes ABAC ⚠️                           │
  │  [PASS] 2.1.1 etcd cert file permissions: 600 ✅                       │
  │  [FAIL] 4.2.6 Ensure --protect-kernel-defaults: flag not set ❌       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  SUMMARY: 145 PASS, 12 FAIL, 8 WARN                                     │
  │  CRITICAL FAILS: #1.2.6, #4.2.6 — FIX IMMEDIATELY                      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: run kube-bench as a CronJob monthly. Review results and remediate FAILs within 1 sprint. Track compliance score over time in a Grafana dashboard. CIS Benchmark + kube-bench: your cluster's security report card. Run it, fix the FAILs, repeat.

Why not A? CIS is a security benchmark, not a performance test. Why not B? CIS has benchmarks for vanilla K8s AND managed services (EKS, AKS, GKE). Why not D? CIS is distribution-agnostic — it applies to any Kubernetes cluster.

Security & Policy Q186 of 200
How do you map Kubernetes security controls to compliance frameworks like PCI-DSS, SOC2, and HIPAA?
A) Kubernetes security controls have no relationship to compliance frameworks — compliance is handled at the application level only
B) Achieving any Kubernetes compliance certification automatically satisfies all frameworks — PCI-DSS, SOC2, and HIPAA are identical in their K8s requirements
C) Compliance frameworks don't apply to Kubernetes because it's infrastructure, not application code
D) Map each compliance requirement to a K8s control: PCI-DSS requirement 7 (access control) → RBAC with least privilege + audit logging; requirement 10 (monitoring) → API audit logs + Falco runtime monitoring. SOC2 CC6.1 (logical access) → NetworkPolicies + mTLS. HIPAA 164.312 (technical safeguards) → encryption at rest (PVC encryption) + encryption in transit (mTLS). Generate compliance reports by aggregating kube-bench results, audit logs, and policy engine reports
✅ Correct Answer: D

Compliance mapping is the bridge between security engineers and auditors. Auditors ask: "How do you control access to production data?" You answer: "RBAC with least-privilege ServiceAccounts (here's our quarterly `kubectl auth can-i` audit), NetworkPolicies restricting pod-to-pod communication (here's our default-deny policy), mTLS via Istio (here's the mesh configuration), and audit logs tracking all API access (here's the audit policy)." Automated evidence collection: run kube-bench for CIS compliance, export Kyverno policy reports, collect audit logs, generate PDF report — all scriptable, all repeatable.

Compliance Mapping — K8s Controls → Frameworks
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PCI-DSS REQUIREMENT         KUBERNETES CONTROL                         │
  │  ────────────────────────    ───────────────────────────────────────── │
  │  7. Access Control           RBAC (SA + Role + RoleBinding)             │
